## FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

THE ADVISORS TO Kg Lbegla were sspcs f the telegraph wres beg stretched acrss ther lad by the Brtsh Sth Afrca Cmpay the late eteeth cetry. They beleved the whte me's pla was t se the wres t te ad restra ther kg, rler f the Nrther Ndebele peple Matabelelad. Eve whe the cal prpse f the telegraph was explaed t them, they were stll dsmssve. Why wld sch a thg be eeded, they asked, whe they already pssessed eectve meas f lg-dstace cmmcat sch as drms ad smke sgals?

T may, ths mght sd lke a famlar stry: the stry f premder peple stadg the way f evtable prgress, r the stry f msgded resstace t a techlgy that evetally paved the way t a better wrld.

Bt the advsrs t Kg Lbegla were jsted ther sspcs. The Brtsh Sth Afrca Cmpay, led by Cecl Rhdes, declared war agast the Ndebele 1893, ad cted wth the sppress f the Matabelelad ad Mashalad prsgs 1896. Oe f the pretexts sed t wage war was that the lcals were stealg the cpper wre t make ramets ad htg tls. The telegraph was important or other reasons too. From a military perspective, it would prove to be crucial or orchestrating the colonisation o southern Arica, including what would become southern Rhodesia (now Zimbabwe). It would have been much more dicult to coordinate troop movements and send alerts without it. As a result o those wars, by 1930 about 50 per cent o the country's land – 49 million acres – had been granted to European migrants, who represented only 5 per cent o the population.1

In other words, it was a landgrab. Colonialism may have proceeded by dierent methods at dierent times and dierent places in history, but in the end it always boiled down to the same thing: a seizing o land (and the riches and labour that went with it) perpetrated by orce or deception.

Two things made colonialism distinctive rom other asset seizures in history. First, this landgrab was global, reaching truly planetary proportions. From 1800 to 1875, about 83,000 square miles rom all over the world were acquired each year by European colonisers. From 1875 to 1914, that gure jumped to 240,000 square miles per year. By the end o that period, Britain had 55 colonies, France 29, Germany 10, Portugal and the Netherlands 8 each, Italy 4, and Belgium 1.2 Colonialism is a story not only about the Ndebele in Zimbabwe, but also about the Bororo in Brazil and the countless other peoples who witnessed the simultaneous arrival o the telegraph, the rife and the cross – or whatever specic combination o colonial technologies, weaponry and belies they were colonised with. For none o them did these things bring peace and progress, only dispossession and injustice.

The second point is that colonial stories have long lives. We are not just talking about an isolated war here or the introduction o a technology there. Colonialism is a process that took centuries to unold, and its repercussions continue to be elt. To put it dierently: the historic landgrab may be over (obviously, southern Arica

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

is no longer a colony o the British), but the impacts o the landgrab continue to reverberate. Compare present-day England and Zimbabwe, and you soon realise that, overall, the benefts have continued to accrue to the coloniser nation in the orm o accumulated wealth, while the burdens have continued to accrue to the colonised in the orm o poverty, violence and lack o opportunity. We are increasingly sensing an urgent need to reinterpret our past and present in the light o that colonial landgrab.

But something else today is amiss that goes beyond this necessary reckoning with the past. Colonialism lives on in another way, through a new kind o landgrab. It is still new, but we can already sense how it could reshape our present and our uture just as signifcantly as the old one.

This latest seizure entails not the grabbing o land, but the grabbing o data – data that is potentially as valuable as land, because it provides access to a priceless resource: the intimacy o our daily lives, as a new source o value. Is the exploitation o human lie an entirely new phenomenon? O course not. But this new resource grab should concern us because it exhibits some very colonial characteristics. It is global: nowhere is human lie sae rom this orm o exploitation. It is very large-scale: the worldwide users o Facebook, You Tube, Whats App and Instagram each exceed the individual populations o China and India, the world's largest countries, with the Chinese platorms We Chat and Tik Tok not coming ar behind.3 It is creating unprecedented wealth based on extraction: Big Tech companies are among the wealthiest in the world (or instance, with its stock market value o US \$2.9 trillion,4 Apple is bigger than the entire stock market o any country in the world except the US and Japan).5 It is shaping the very structure o the world's communications, with experts worried that the world's two largest data powers, the US and China, are increasingly associated with exclusive networks o undersea communications cables.6 And most importantly, it continues the legacy o dispossession and injustice started by colonialism.

This book is the story o this data grab, and why it represents a reshaping o the world's resources that is worthy o comparison to historical colonialism's landgrab. It is the story, in other words, o a data colonialism that superimposes a data grab over the historical landgrab.7 We already know how the colonial story develops. To get a preview o the kinds o long-term impacts data colonialism will likely have, we don't need to engage in hypotheticals. We need only to look at the historical record. Our present and not just our past is irredeemably colonial, and the new data colonialism is a core part o that.

#### The Four X's of Colonialism

Today, you are the King o England. But just as easily you could have ruled over colonial Spain, France or the Netherlands. Regardless o your choice, the task ahead o you is essentially the same: there are territories to be settled, resources to be traded, cities to be built, and native populations to be pacifed. A air amount o ambition and greed seem to be a requirement or the job.

Explore, expand, exploit and exterminate – the tools o your trade. With a ew clicks, you apply these strategies in succession as you establish your empire. Then you apply them again. And again. And i your empire should all, throttled by the competition or vanquished in a war, that's not a problem. You can simply start anew, because this is only a videogame: Sid Meier's Colonization, a turnbased strategy game released in 1994 (re-issued in 2008).

Explore. Expand. Exploit. Exterminate. This is the time-tested 'Four-X' ormula or playing strategy video games. But it is also a air summary o the ormula applied by European colonisers to create vast ortunes or themselves, vast misery or everyone else, and in the process reshape completely the organisation o the world's resources.

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

Colonialism was a complicated project that required complicated enterprises. We've mentioned the British South Africa Company already, and there was of course the East India Company as well. The Spanish had the Casa de la Contratación de las Indias, while the Portuguese founded the Companhia do Commércio da Índia. The Dutch had their own Verenigde Oostindische Compagnie, which during the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries employed over a million Europeans to work in Asia, exporting 2.5 million tons of goods, and which was legally sanctioned to declare war, engage in piracy, establish colonies and coin money.8 All of these companies had close links with their respective nation's rulers, and complex bureaucracies emerged around them.

In their operations, they neatly followed the Four-X model. They explored the world by launching missions to 'discover' new places they could control through military and technological means; they expanded their dominions by establishing colonies where native labour and resources could be appropriated by force; they exploited those colonies by setting up a global system of trade where those resources could be converted into wealth, always to the advantage of the coloniser; and they exterminated any opposition by the colonised, in the process eliminating their ways of being in the world. From 1492 to about the middle of the twentieth century, that's the story of colonialism in a nutshell. By applying the Four-X model, European colonisers managed to control over 84 per cent of the globe, even though Europe represents only 8 per cent of the planet's landmass.9

Let's see how this matches the actions of Big Tech corporations.

Today, Big Tech's eorts to explore and expand don't involve continental land, but the virtual territories of our dataed lives: our shopping habits, for sure, but also our interactions with family, friends, lovers and co-workers, the space of our homes, the space of our towns, our hobbies and entertainment, our workouts, our political discussions, our health records, our commutes, our studies, and on and on. There is hardly a territory or activity that is beyond this kind o colonisation, and there is hardly a corner o the world that remains untouched by its technologies and platorms.

But, as with historical colonialism, that territorial capture is just the start. Once colonies were established, a system was put in place or the continuous extraction o resources rom these territories, and or the transormation o these resources into riches. Big Tech has achieved a similar eat o exploitation by setting up business models that convert 'our' data – that is, data resulting rom tracking our lives and those o others – into wealth and power or them (but not or us). At the micro level, this means that our data is used to target us individually through advertising or profling. At the macro level, this means that our data is aggregated and used to make decisions or predictions impacting large groups o people, such as the training o an algorithm to discriminate based on race, gender, economic status or medical condition. This is possible thanks to a rearrangement o many aspects o our daily lie in such a way that ensures we are continuously generating data.

Which brings us to the ourth 'X', where the picture is more complex. In history, colonial extermination took many orms. Principally, there were deaths caused by war, mass suicide, disease, starvation and other orms o violence: 175 million indigenous people in the Americas at the hands o the Spanish, Portuguese, British and US; 100 million in India at the hands o the British; 36 million Aricans who perished in transit during the transatlantic slave trade (in addition to those who perished as slaves once they arrived); one million in Algeria killed by the French; hundreds o thousands in Indonesia killed by the Dutch; and millions more who cannot be easily counted.10

But brutal physical violence was not the only option. Early on, colonisers realised they needed to be able to deploy other orms o extermination that eliminated not just individual lives, but also the

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

economic and social alternatives to colonialism (which in itsel entailed the extermination o lie, but at a slower rate). One strategy was the imposition o agricultural monocultures that were highly protable or the coloniser but destroyed the ability o the colonised to eed themselves. Think o the Dutch investment in coee production in the East Indies, which went rom a harvest o one hundred pounds (45 kilograms) in 1711 to twelve million pounds (5.4 million kilograms) in 1723.11 Or think o the colonial sugar trade, which created great poverty and misery in the Caribbean while contributing a signicant 5 per cent to the British gross domestic product at its peak during the eighteenth century (without slavery, sugar would just have been too expensive or most British people to consume).12

Another strategy o (economic) extermination was the throttling o business opportunities through the fooding o markets with cheap goods that eliminated homegrown industries. An example o this is the British cotton trade, which inundated global markets with cheap machine-made textiles that destroyed the liestyles and livelihoods o domestic cultivators, spinners and weavers in colonies such as India, not to mention the devastating human cost paid by plantation slaves in America.13 Throughout the colonial world, instructions like the ollowing (sent rom London to the governor o Quebec in 1763) were issued with the goal o retarding local industry: 'it is Our Express Will and Pleasure, that you do not, upon any Pretence whatever . . . give your Assent to any Law or Laws or setting up any Manuactures . . . which are hurtul and prejudicial to this Kingdom.'14

The monopolistic and anti-competitive practices o Big Tech are also having disruptive eects. The scale on which they operate cannot be ignored: i as late as 1945 one in three people on the planet was living under colonial rule, today, around one in three people on the planet has a Facebook account, and almost everyone uses search engines o some sort. The contexts and impacts are obviously di erent, but this resemblance in scale means that companies like Meta – which now owns Facebook, Instagram and Whats App – or OpenAI have a lot o power over the lives o a lot o people. Meta's power, many have argued, has contributed to the spread o misin ormation and hate amidst genocidal violence (like in Myanmar), health crises (anti-vaccine disinormation) and political intererence (the Cambridge Analytica scandal). Meanwhile, Sam Altman, the CEO o OpenAI, believes that the opportunity to solve humanity's problems with Articial Intelligence is so appealing that it is worth the risk o destroying the world as we know it.15 In other words, i AI ends up massively disrupting social values and institutions, as many experts claim could happen, Altman thinks it will be worth paying this price because o the problems AI will solve in the process. But others are not so sure this is a good bargain, which is why they are asking questions about where Big Tech's new power to determine what is relevant, normal, acceptable or true is heading.

Forms o economic and cultural extermination will, necessarily, take time to unold, but they are the potential consequences o a change we can already see: a major shit in power relations that fows rom the capture o virtual territories. Meanwhile, a very di erent story is being told about data, told with a much more positive twist. And here too there is a historical parallel. Colonialism has always required a strong civilising mission, an imposed worldview that dismissed all alternatives and rendered invisible all contributions emanating rom the colonised. This worldview allowed the colonisers to control not just bodies, but hearts and minds as well. In the past, Christianity and Western science were the cornerstones o this civilising mission. They delineated the path towards the salvation o colonised souls and promised them a share in humanity's scientic progress, provided they remained within their assigned roles.

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

As an example o a historic corporation engaged in a civilising mission, consider the New England Company, also known as the Society or the Propagation o the Gospel in New England. Founded in 1649, the company engaged in the 'education' o the 'infdel' populations in British colonies, carried out through Anglican missionary work, the establishment o schools and the translation o the Christian bible into native languages. Its frst president was the scientist Robert Boyle, best known or the law carrying his name that describes the relationship between the pressure and volume o a gas, but less well known or his theories on the nature o skin colour and the origins o blackness. While Boyle advocated or better treatment o slaves who converted to Christianity, he and his contemporaries never rejected slavery wholesale, and as members o the Royal Society they beneftted directly rom the slave trade through that institution's investment in Royal Arican Company stock.16

Big Tech too has a civilising mission that is mixed up with its technologies and business goals. Part o this civilising mission continues to revolve around Western science: network science, data science, computer science, and so on. The other part no longer revolves around Christianity, but around parallel sublime notions like the convenience that will supposedly make all our lives easier, the connectivity that apparently will bring new orms o community, and the new orms o science and Artifcial Intelligence associated with machines that purportedly can solve problems better than humans. It's not as i some o these dreams are not becoming real or a select ew; it's just that they risk becoming nightmares or everyone else in the orm o lost livelihoods, new orms o exploited labour and the loss o control over vital personal data.

Civilising missions, economic motives, the exercise o power and the introduction o specifc technologies have been deeply intermingled throughout the history o colonialism, but always with an uneven impact that avours some but not others. We saw this already in our example o the telegraph in southern Arica.

#### Data Grab

Another example was the introduction o the electrical grid to India throughout the Madras Presidency in the early twentieth century. Electricity was considered a triumph o Western science over the 'devil o darkness', and while it was initially used exclusively to improve the lives o white people as a display o cultural superiority, its application was eventually extended to the rest o the population as a kind o advertisement or the supposed benefts o colonialism. It powered cinemas, illuminated public spaces, propelled tramcars and provided energy to places like hospitals – all while generating income or British companies. But beyond these comorts, amusements and public services, electricity also served to run the lighthouses that guided ships carrying colonial goods, powered weapon actories and electrifed prison barbed ences that kept the population in check, extended the operating hours o oces and printing presses carrying out the coloniser's administrative work, increased revenue by accelerating industrial and agricultural production, and provided the backbone or communication and transportation networks that guaranteed the smooth unctioning o the empire. In other words, behind the civilising mission o this 'git' rom the coloniser, electricity was instrumental in sustaining the core business o colonialism,17 which was anything but peaceul (100 million Indians were exterminated during British rule, as we mentioned earlier).

Replace 'electricity' with 'data' and, while the specifcs are di erent, some elements o the story remain eerily similar. Ways o processing data are also heralded as scientifc achievements, a git that promises convenience, connectivity and new orms o intelligence. But look under the surace o this civilising git, and you will fnd that it also brings new orms o surveillance (through acial recognition or workplace monitoring), discrimination (when algorithms deny or control access to services based on people's profles) and exploitation (when gig workers' wages are continuously adjusted downwards, or instance).

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

A discussion o the colonial legacy o Western science will be a recurring theme throughout the book, and this is a touchy subject. To point out the ways in which Western science has been used to justiy social and environmental harms might come across as a wholesale dismissal o the benets and contributions o science, which are many (not least to monitor and model the harms that humanity is currently doing to our environment and, i we can nd them, monitor potential solutions). In no way is our argument anti-science, nor do we wish to an the fames o science denialism. But that doesn't exempt us rom acing head-on the important critiques that colonised peoples have made o the ways in which Western science was used during and ater colonialism to control and exploit the natural and social realms. In act, it is only by looking at contemporary science through this colonial lens that we see these continuities, which go back to the origins o modernity generally and o modern science. That is all the more vital when this problematic legacy continues to shape developments like data science and AI, which have huge impacts on our present and our uture. It is exactly as the Cameroonian philosopher Achille Mbembe has said: 'Our era is attempting to bring back into ashion the old myth that the West alone has a monopoly on the uture.'18

#### Terms and Conditions

Our new colonial reality entails new processes o data extraction that are changing education, healthcare, agriculture, policing, the way we talk to each other and the way we judge each other, and many other areas besides. Which is why this latest stage in colonialism's development requires a new term: data colonialism, a social order in which the continuous extraction o data rom our lives generates massive wealth and inequality on a global scale.

#### Data Grab

This new social order comes with a new social contract, based on the premise that the data 'exhaust' we generate through our online interactions should be given to corporations or ree. Why? Because, we are told, the data we generate can only be processed or rened using vast computing and storage capacities that we do not have at our disposal. Humanity's progress, the story goes, depends on that surrender o data. And we are told that this surrender is happening with our ull consent, because ater all, didn't we click the 'I accept' button when installing those apps on our phones or using those platorms?

Let's pause or a moment and consider what happens when we click 'I accept'. Or rather, what happened i you clicked 'accept' in 2007 under Google Chrome's Terms o Service (TOS) agreement. Most people don't read the Terms o Service in detail. So it's worth setting out some o what you would have agreed to back in 2007 when installing the web browser:

You give Google a perpetual, irrevocable, worldwide, royalty-ree, and non-exclusive license to reproduce, adapt, modiy, translate, publish, publicly perorm, publicly display and distribute any Content which you submit, post or display on or through, the Services.19

More recent versions are not worded as rapaciously. They don't need to be! Because, without most o us ever having read those original terms and conditions, we have reorganised our lives around whatever rules Google imposes on us. Google's new rules can aford to be worded more sotly because the extraction o data is already well under way – blunt language is no longer necessary. In any event, on July 2023 Google updated its privacy policy to indicate that it reserves the right to 'use publicly available inormation to help train Google's AI models and build products and eatures like

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

Google Translate, Bard, and Cloud AI capabilities.'20 This means that if any of us post anything online publicly, Google has unilaterally decided it can use it for training its AI models, without asking anyone for permission.

Now, compare Chrome's original TOS to a much older document, the Requerimiento (which in Spanish means the 'Requirement' or 'Demand') written in 1513, also quite early in the rst colonial landgrab. This was a document read by the Spanish conquistadors as they entered a village or city in the Americas in the middle of the night to wreak havoc and get their pillaging under way. They read this document in Spanish . . . to indigenous or First Nation21 peoples who did not speak Spanish! No amount of translation would probably have been enough to explain the dierences in worldviews to indigenous peoples as they heard these words being uttered:

But, if you do not [submit to Spanish rule], I certify to you that, with the help of God, we shall powerfully enter into your country, and shall make war against you in all ways and manners that we can, and shall subject you to the yoke and obedience of the Church and of their Highnesses; we shall take you and your wives and your children, and shall make slaves of them, and as such shall sell and dispose of them as their Highnesses may command; and we shall take away your goods, and shall do you all the mischief and damage that we can . . .22

We are not suggesting that what followed the reading of the Requerimiento is the same as what follows our installation of Chrome. Google won't enslave us or our children, and certainly the cost of rejecting Chrome's terms is not extermination. But the point here is to highlight the tricks and threats necessary to perform such initial acts o dispossession. Incomprehensible language must be used, because extraction requires that we don't ully understand the terms under which it is being carried out. By the time we do, it is too late.

The Spaniards, and all subsequent colonisers, counted on this initial ailure to understand what was behind the act o dispossession. They knew the Requerimiento was a pretence, a eel-good excuse to legitimise in legal, political and religious terms the atrocities they were about to commit. Indigenous people quickly sensed it, too. They would ee instead o waiting around to hear the proclamation; they had to be captured and tied to trees to make them sit through its reading, or they had to be enticed with cheap gits. In one recorded instance, a chie o the Cenú people insisted that the whole thing be explained to him, and when he fnally managed to grasp the basic meaning, he said that a Pope who was giving away land that was not his must be a drunk, and a king who accepted this git must be a lunatic. Bartolomé de Las Casas, a priest sympathetic to the indigenous people's cause, said that he didn't know whether to laugh or to cry upon hearing the Requerimiento.

#### Raw Materials

Perhaps one day we too will wonder whether to laugh or cry when reading the TOS o our present-day apps which carry much o Google Chrome's original intent. In the meantime, the dispossession o our data proceeds largely unabated. While it is the Big Tech companies that get the critical headlines, the data grab is not just down to a ew rogue companies: it is happening at every scale, sometimes in dark corners and sometimes in plain sight.

Take or example Lasso, a leading marketer in the US health sector who you almost certainly have never heard o. Personal health data is widely assumed to be legally protected, but Lasso has ound a way to oer a number o products or marketers who want

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

to reach customers interested in healthcare, including Blueprint™, Connect™, and Triggers™. While Lasso, as one would expect, says it is compliant with US health data regulations, its ambitions or Blueprint™ are striking. To quote rom its webpages:

Lasso Blueprint enables marketers to create high-value audiences composed o health providers and consumers based on diagnoses, medications, procedures, insurance data, demographic information, and much more. The product provides audience counts in real-time . . . audiences can also be dynamically rereshed on a weekly basis with the latest real-world data to ensure you [the marketer] never miss an opportunity to engage with your targets.24

So, even i your health data remains strictly anonymised, it is collected in bundles which are then used to uniquely target you as a consumer with a particular medical history. It hardly matters whether your name is on the data package. All this relies on sophisticated data processing by Xandr, a company that was acquired or US \$1 billion by Microsot in 2022, and operates ar rom news headlines.

Data capture through surveillance or the purpose o marketing or algorithmic proling was the biggest problem we had to worry about until recently. But the vertiginous evolution o AI in the last ew years has proven that data colonialism is unleashing efects that may transorm how we think or create – or, more specically, how we allow machines to do thinking or creating or us. Consider what is happening in the art, culture and media sectors with the arrival o natural language generation and generative AI tools. These programs are mimicking our creative endeavours using Articial Intelligence algorithms, with increasing degrees o authenticity. Given a ew text instructions, AI tools like ChatGPT, DALL-E, Deep Dream or Deep Mind (the rst two owned by OpenAI, the other two by Google) can generate text, images, sound or human speech that not only seem like they were generated by a human, but can imitate a specic author like Jane Austen, a painter like Salvador Dalí, or a musician like Fela Kuti.

For all this to be possible – or the AI to copy what Austen, Dalí or Kuti read, paint or sound like – it needs to learn what other artists, or indeed all o us, read, paint or sound like. In other words, it needs to analyse not only Austen's novels, but other novelists' works too, as well as what you have written; it needs to analyse not only Kuti's voice, but your voice too. It can etch them rom repositories that may contain the videos we upload to social media platorms, or the voice messages we leave on our riends' phones.

Some people may not think the AI generation o derivatives that mimic someone amous or non-amous are that big a deal. They can be seen as an entertaining gimmick, or perhaps a useul work tool (imagine being able to edit a voice or video message without having to re-record it). But questions about authenticity, about the value o original work, about our ability to recognise altered records, and about who controls the power to perorm these eats are worthy o our consideration in these early days o Generative AI.

And there's a bigger point too: that our collective cultural and social products now serve as the extracted raw material on which AI relies. For example, Google's MusicLM sotware, which can generate melodies based on instructions like 'Meditative song, calming and soothing, with futes and guitars,' was trained on 280,000 hours o music. Did Google pay to license all o the music it used or this purpose? Not likely. This is probably the reason why it decided to delay the release o this tool or a while. I the generated music were to sound too much like the source material rom which it derived, this would open the door to potential lawsuits.25 But eventually Google released the program ree o charge, like most o

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

its products. In its nal orm, the AI will not comply with requests to copy specic artists or vocals, allowing Google to avoid potential charges o copyright inringement. But there is still a corporation expropriating our cultural production as source material to train a machine to do the work o humans, because machines will be able to do the work more quickly and cheaply.26

From internet searches to cloud services to generative AI, Google doesn't need to charge us to use its products, because we are the source material for its products. Allowing the public to use its tools or ree (as a way to 'empower the creative process', in the case o MusicLM)27 cannot conceal the nature and scale o this data 'heist', as Naomi Klein has called it.28

Whether hidden in dark corners or not, these are extensive acts o appropriation. We are talking about the capturing and monetisation, through data, o our collective activities, our interactions with each other across time and space, our shared resources. The 'cool' actor o 'generative AI' is basically a device to distract rom this.

Welcome to data colonialism. It is happening everywhere. It is an appropriation o resources on a truly colonial scale. A data grab that will change the course o history, just as the original colonial landgrab did ve centuries ago.

#### Reading the Present through a Colonial Lens

Comparisons between the old colonialism and the new colonialism are worth exploring, but to compare is not necessarily to equate. History is too complex and interwoven or that. There is thereore no almanac we can ofer you that would dispense simple one-toone comparisons between, say, the Dutch Verenigde Oostindische Compagnie and Alphabet-Google. How could history repeat itsel in such a simple way? Instead, this book will ofer you a lens through which to see that today's data grab really is a continuation o the colonial landgrab that reorganised the control o the world's resources starting ve centuries ago, putting the opportunities or exploiting them in the hands o the ew. This lens enables us to identiy six important similarities between historical colonialism and data colonialism.

First, and most undamentally, both systems are ounded on an appropriation o the world's resources, treating them as 'just there' or the taking. Historically it was the territories, natural resources and labour o the Americas, Arica and much o Asia and the Pacic that were plundered. In the contemporary era, it is human lie, extracted in the orm o data, that is being appropriated.

Second, that appropriation always serves a wider purpose: to build a new social and economic order that locks in that landgrab o resources. Whether this took centuries to develop (like in historical colonialism) or mere decades (like in data colonialism), the goal has always been to construct a new way o ordering all aspects o lie to benet the coloniser by locking in a orm o extraction.

Third, this colonial order and global landgrab o resources is the joint work o states and corporations. In the past, colonial states were monarchies, and the companies licensed by them (like the British South Arica Company) were special-purpose vehicles created or colonial extraction. Today, corporations have played a much more important role in building complex alliances with nation-states and their governments, as we can see in the example o China and India, but also in diferent ways in the US and Europe. But the act remains that both historical and data colonialism represent state-corporate partnerships.

Fourth, colonialism has always had a disastrous efect on the physical environment, and continues to do so. While global warming began its ascent during industrial capitalism, it was colonialism that set the stage or this by raming the natural world as something cheap and disposable whose only value was the prot that could be extracted rom it. Some orms o environmental devastation are as

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

old as colonialism, like the large-scale mining o precious materials which the electronics sector still relies on. Others, while more recent, have strong links to earlier periods o colonialism: think o the devastation enacted today by industry sectors like Big Oil and Big Agriculture, which rely on the control o original colonial territories. And others are entirely new eects o data colonialism; or example, energy and water consumption by data centres, carbon emissions associated with online shopping deliveries, or the increased number o satellites orbiting the planet.

Fith, and very importantly, colonialism has always generated deep inequalities that divide extractive elites rom large populations o exploited labour. In act, the very idea just mentioned that colonial land was 'just there' or the taking was based, rom the very start, on assuming that the people who were already living on colonised land were worth nothing. Data colonialism introduces new orms o inequality connected to the exploitation o data, which oten replay and deepen those older inequalities, including racial and gender discrimination. And all o the developments with data extraction that we'll describe in later chapters are played out in a highly unequal global economy and geopolitics that have been pro oundly shaped by colonialism.

Finally, the predatory acts o colonialism are so maniestly unjust that they have always had to be disguised by more positive 'civilising' narratives, or 'alibis'. In past centuries, those narratives were tied to Eurocentric notions o progress or Christian ideals o salvation. In the Big Tech era those narratives connect more closely to the values o a capitalist economy, while retaining echoes o earlier civilising missions. Can we imagine Facebook taking on the role o providing internet inrastructure to over thirty Arican countries without these sorts o narratives?29

Despite these six similarities, there is no denying that there are signifcant dierences between historical colonialism and data colonialism. We are, ater all, talking about data, not land, which is a diferent type o asset. While there are limits to the resources that can be extracted rom a single piece o land, the same data set can be exploited ad innitum by an unlimited number o parties, which is what economists mean by saying that data, unlike land, is a nonrival good.

And yet, while both historical colonialism and data colonialism involve acts o extraction on a vast scale, we must remember that extraction only marks the rst move, not the last one, in the building o a social and economic order, and that this order is shaped inevitably by the levels o organisational complexity typical o its time (without doubt, our economy and its organisational structures are massively more developed today than in the sixteenth or eighteenth centuries).

Rather than talking about data colonialism in terms o similar or diferent efects, it is thereore more accurate to talk about compounded ones. The proound injustices o historical colonialism (thet, slavery, discrimination), ar rom being superseded by the new injustices o data colonialism, are made worse by them. This is an important point, because our use o the terms 'historical colonialism' and 'data colonialism' might give the incorrect impression that, as authors, we believe one kind o colonialism is over and a new one beginning. However, when we say that data colonialism is a new orm o colonialism, we wish to convey a sense o evolution, not o spontaneous generation. Thus, data colonialism should be considered on a par with other terms like plantation colonialism or settler colonialism. All o them describe diferent yet overlapping aspects and impacts o a phenomenon with a long and ongoing history: colonialism.

Our emphasis on the dangers o data extractivism might also give the impression that we are against data altogether, and are denying there are ever good reasons or collecting and processing it. That is not our position. There are o course many valid reasons

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

to collect and use data, especially when the purpose is to understand or even change the world for the better. What we are against is the use of data for the exclusive purpose of generating prot without social validation and public control, especially when this is presented to us by corporations as a narrative of salvation and progress. But why think that all good uses of data must come attached to the extractive terms and conditions that we'll discuss throughout this book? It is not data, but those terms and conditions, which currently dene and shape the world of Big Tech and AI. We must contest and rethink those terms and conditions, and the lens of colonial history can help us do that.

#### Your Guide to the Book

We are aware that colonialism is a dicult subject. People come to this subject from dierent histories and standpoints, including different relations to the history and reality of colonialism. Because of this, we acknowledge that people will inevitably be more or less ready to hear our argument. We respect that, because as authors we too are dierently positioned in relation to colonialism, one of us having grown up in London and the other in Mexico.

In Chapter One ('A New Colonialism') we'll confront the doubts many people might have about using the lens of colonialism to explain what is happening with data today. In Chapter Two ('Data Territories') we'll lay out in more detail what sorts of data are being taken, how it is processed, and what sectors of life are being aected. We'll nd that it's pretty much every sector of the economy, society and government, which raises some important questions about how power relations in contemporary societies are being transformed. In Chapter Three ('Data's New Civilising Mission') we look in more detail at the stories we are told (about data extraction and how this benets us) that eectively disguise the realities o data colonialism. In Chapter Four ('The New Colonial Class') we'll ask who is seizing this data, surveying the sorts o corporations involved, the motivations o those who work or them, and the individual entrepreneurs who are working at the data rontier.

In the book's last two chapters we think about resistance. I the cruel and divisive legacy o historical colonialism still lives on, it is hard to imagine what it would take to resist the new stage o colonialism that is unolding. But imagination and resistance are connected. We can think more sharply about what resistance to data colonialism might look like by drawing, as we do in Chapter 5 ('Voices o Deance'), on inspirational gures who opposed historical colonialism in the past, as well as gures in the early history o computing who predicted the dangerous tendencies o today's digital world. Then, in Chapter 6 ('A Playbook or Resistance') we turn to the practicalities o actual resistance, drawing on examples that have been emerging around the world.

The world's inormation sources are today being carved up between corporations, oten with national governments in tow, just as were the lands o historical colonies. Although the outcomes will, as always in colonialism, be unequally distributed, this new data grab will afect the living conditions o everyone on the planet in one way or another. This data grab is happening right now. It is neither an accident, nor the work o a ew rogue capitalists. And it's a global process, even i the techniques and civilising missions vary in detail around the world.

Yes, data extraction oten seems highly technical and remote rom our daily lives. But in its efects, it's anything but remote, which is why we'll start each chapter with an individual story. While the people in these narratives are ctional (the impacts o data extraction are everywhere, but the individual stories tend not be recorded), they are composites that are always grounded in real acts.

#### FROM LANDGRAB TO DATA GRAB

Taken together, the book's chapters provide a map to make sense of this unfamiliar and disturbing new landscape of data extraction. At the end of our journey across this landscape, we'll look back and ask: can we really aord to do nothing by way of resistance?

#### CHAPTER 1

## A NEW COLONIALISM

AS A SINGLE mother in the US, Tracy cannot imagine what she would do without technology, and considers herself lucky to be living in our Digital Age. Her phone and laptop are with her day and night, and she uses free apps to schedule doctor appointments, order food and supplies, pay bills, receive money from her parents, coordinate childcare, communicate with her daughter's teachers, and so on. In the occasional moment of peace and quiet, she uses these apps to entertain herself and socialise with friends and family.

Tracy took a course in college where the professor mentioned some of the privacy threats that come with using these services, but she remembers thinking that she didn't have a problem with corporations collecting her data, because she was not doing anything wrong or worth hiding. Tracy is not a terrorist, so why would she care if a company records information about her in exchange for the opportunity to use their services for free? Sure, the ad-targeting could feel a bit like stalking (sometimes it seemed like her phone was listening to her and then showing her ads for things she might have mentioned in conversations). But this was all just part of our capitalist system which, while not perfect, was still the best option available.

Tracy's views began to change when the Supreme Court of the United States overturned Roe v. Wade, opening the door for US states to criminalise abortion. As someone who had had an abortion, Tracy thought this was an important issue, and she became concerned when she started reading about how some o the data collected by the apps and services she used could potentially be turned against her. Articles were telling her to delete her period-tracking app and stop using her ftness monitor. A riend mentioned that her smart water meter, installed in her apartment two years ago, could determine i she was pregnant – or stopped being pregnant – just by analysing the changes in her household's water consumption patterns. 'Think about it,' her riend told her: smart water meters can detect when and how oten the toilet is ushed. Since pregnant women tend to use the toilet more requently, a sudden increase in toilet use might indicate a pregnancy, while a sudden decrease might indicate an abortion has taken place. Tracy also knew her shopping apps could make a pretty accurate guess about what was happening in her uterus based on what she purchased, and she'd also read stories about women who claimed Facebook knew they were expecting beore they did.

Suddenly, Tracy went rom thinking o hersel as someone who was not doing anything wrong to eeling like someone whose data could be used to prove she had done something 'wrong', that is, ending a pregnancy. True, none o that had happened in the state where she lived yet, and hopeully it wouldn't come to pass. But she read on the news that Facebook gave police in the state o Nebraska access to a teen's direct messages to prosecute her or an abortion, and that lawmakers in Texas were working on a bill that would make it illegal to provide inormation on how to access abortion services. I the bill passed, some people eared social media platorms would start to censor posts about abortion.

Tracy ound hersel wondering what exactly she had consented to every time she clicked the 'I agree' button on the dozens o apps she had installed. What data was being collected about her, and

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

how could it be used? Why was it being collected, and who in the end would benet? She couldn't nd the answers to those questions easily, not even by googling. Sure, Google had just announced that they wouldn't record the location data o women visiting abortion clinics, but this implied that they were collecting data about all the other places women were visiting (she later read that, despites its promises, Google was still tracking visits to abortion clinics1

The act that these services were ree no longer seemed like such a good deal to Tracy, ater all. With only a tiny turn o the political dial, something previously considered normal could become illegal, and Tracy's data could suddenly implicate her.

Many o us know someone – or are someone – in a similar position to this person we are calling Tracy. But could we say that Tracy is a colonial subject? Slaves were colonial subjects. Oppressed and disenranchised people serving vast empires hundreds o years ago were colonial subjects. Tracy doesn't seem to be any o those things. In act, apart rom some data privacy concerns, she seems to be living a relatively comortable lie and enjoying the convenience aforded by digital devices. Yes, it's scary that her personal data could be used to target her in some way. But isn't it bad governments, not good corporations, who are to blame or that kind o persecution? Isn't it thereore irresponsible, even insulting, to suggest that Tracy is the victim o a new colonialism?

We're not claiming that digital platorms take us back to the brutal colonial lie o past centuries, with its continental-scale orced labour and inhuman living conditions.

Our argument is a diferent one: that our digital lives today, and the kind o relationships we have with the corporations which make those lives possible, involve undamental changes in power relations that can only be ully understood within the ramework o colonialism, and the proound change in the world's resource distribution that colonialism engendered.

#### No Capitalism without Colonialism

In order to see this unolding reality we need to look at the colonial past and the capitalist present in a dierent way. No one doubts that we live today under an arrangement or managing the world's resources called capitalism, an arrangement that organises economies through markets. By suggesting that a new colonialism is emerging in the midst o this order, we are establishing a new kind o relationship between colonialism and capitalism.

Beore going urther, it might be helpul to start with some basic defnitions o what colonialism and capitalism are. According to Wikipedia:

Colonialism is a practice or policy o control by one people or power over other people or areas, oten by establishing colonies and generally with the aim o economic dominance. In the process o colonisation, colonisers may impose their religion, language, economics, and other cultural practices. The oreign administrators rule the territory in pursuit o their interests, seeking to beneft rom the colonised region's people and resources.2

#### While Wikipedia's page or capitalism reads:

Capitalism is an economic system based on the private ownership o the means o production and their operation or proft . . . In a capitalist market economy, decision-making and investments are determined by owners o wealth, property, or ability to maneuver capital or production ability in capital and fnancial markets – whereas prices and the distribution o goods and services are mainly determined by competition in goods and services markets.3

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

Judging rom these denitions, it would seem that colonialism and capitalism are two completely diferent things.

I we ollow Wikipedia, colonialism is a practice (or policy), whereas capitalism is a system. More to the point, colonialism seems exploitative, taking things away rom people through orce, exerting power rom a distance to make sure, among other things, that the colonised accept and adopt the ways o thinking and acting o the coloniser. In contrast, capitalism doesn't seem as brutal. It's almost civilised, a matter o arranging resources and making better economic decisions. Yes, there still seems to be one group o people in control (the owners o the means o production), but there is no mention o violence or orce. Instead, we have markets where capitalists compete, which presumably prevents abuse and creates benets or the entire economy.

We know these denitions are incomplete and, depending on where you stand, controversial. But whether you accept their detail or not, they capture the common-sense view that colonialism was about the exploitative extraction o things, whereas capitalism is about the collaborative production o things. Those tenses ('is', 'was') are not accidental, because in the standard view o history, colonialism came before capitalism. In act, the standard view o history would tell us that the brutality o colonialism needed to end in order to make room or the more civilised mode o production that we all know as capitalism. We must question that standard view o history.

#### Capitalism Cannot Be Understood without Colonialism

There is no way to understand the capitalism that Tracy recognises as her daily reality without also understanding the role colonialism played and still plays in making it possible. We need to look beyond our immediate historical context and recall that the wealth generated in the mines and plantations o colonialism nanced the actories o early industrial capitalism.

#### Data Grab

Sometimes we can track the transormation o colonial wealth into capitalist resources quite directly and within one generation. Take the case o pro-slavery parliamentarian Richard Pennant, 1st Baron Penrhyn, who owned six sugar plantations and hundreds o slaves in Jamaica while being actively involved in the development o the Welsh slate industry. Or the case o Scottish entrepreneur John Pender, who made his ortune manuacturing goods rom cotton produced in slave plantations in the US, and then invested much o his wealth laying undersea telegraph cables connecting Britain to America and India. O course, the actual transer o wealth was oten more complicated than that, involving multiple parties, institutions and nations. But the act remains that colonialism greatly acilitated Europe's transition rom eudalism to capitalism.

How exactly? According to one estimate, in the last 300 years colonialism contributed to an increase o 14 to 78 per cent o the total average income or the coloniser nations. Furthermore, it was colonialism that allowed European protocapitalists (merchants, artisans, landlords, etc.) to clear peasants of the land, accumulate capital, increase production and hire wage workers quickly, all with the support o their respective states. This accumulation o wealth and power allowed Europeans to triumph over competing protocapitalists in Arica, Asia and China, cementing Europe's global dominance and allowing it to re-invest its wealth into additional colonial enterprises. Without the accumulated wealth o extraction, business entrepreneurs wouldn't have had the stable resources to own the means o production and so become capitalists.4

In any case, it makes no sense to think o colonialism as something primitive that had to be got out o the way beore capitalism could develop. Karl Marx wrote that 'the overseer's book o penalties replaces the slave-driver' under capitalism, but he was wrong.5 Slavery, a major way o organising labour on a global scale under colonialism, thrived during the period o industrial capitalism's

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

growth (the nineteenth century). This was, or example, the time o the massive expansion o slave plantations in the US South. Meanwhile, the UK's stance o outlawing slavery hardly prevented the UK economy (or example, the port cities o Bristol and Liverpool) rom beneftting hugely rom the economic outputs o slavery. Economists Thomas Piketty and Gabriel Zucman argue that in the nineteenth-century US, the value derived rom trading and owning slaves was more than the wealth derived rom actories and transport combined. Contemporary historians have increasingly come to realise that, ar rom being organisationally backward, colonial production methods were at the cutting edge o nineteenth-century accounting, fnancing and the 'rational' management o human bodies to produce economic output on the largest possible scale and at the lowest possible cost.6

We can take this argument even urther. There's no way the huge wealth concentration in the Global North today, including the US's dominant position in the global economy, can be understood apart rom the unequal historical legacy o nineteenth- and twentieth-century empires. That legacy is the source o the continuing uneven opportunities open to the world's national economies: the 'New International Division o Labour', which involves the shiting o low-price manuacturing out o much o the Global North into the Global South;7 and the whole structure o multilateral organisations like the World Bank and International Monetary Fund, dominated by the West, that manages the so-called 'development' process.8

The standard view o history interprets the past fve centuries o history through just one lens at a time, when in act we always need two, superimposed lenses: colonialism and capitalism. It is misleading to see colonialism and capitalism as discrete historical periods linked only through a process o historical evolution, with colonialism (whose brutality has ortunately ended) coming frst, and capitalism (under which we are happily living) coming later. Colonialism has been there all along, rst laying the oundations o a uture capitalism and then uelling an insurgent industrial capitalism in the growing global economy. Capitalism has always had a colonial gene within it. And that remains true, even though, in much o the world, there are no more imperial colonies (although the peoples o Puerto Rico, Palestine and American Samoa might have a diferent view on that).

To be clear, we are not saying that capitalism is just colonialism in disguise (or there really is such a thing as capitalism, and it has unique characteristics in terms o its economic and social organisation). Rather, we have to treat colonialism and capitalism as parallel and linked processes. In act, the system o prot generation that is capitalism always depended on the colonial landgrab that started two centuries beore capitalism. That colonial landgrab continued through the nineteenth and twentieth centuries (think o oil and the extraction o ever more rare minerals), and it continues today.9 Capitalism has a colonial dimension, not by accident, but by design.

I colonialism and capitalism – extractivism and production – go hand in hand, today's new methods o 'mining' data show that similar processes are continuing to unold. This is why writers who interpret what is happening with data exclusively in terms o the dynamics o capitalism cannot quite make sense o the scale and orce o the data extraction under way today. There are multiple theories that propose we are experiencing a new orm o postindustrial capitalism – data capitalism, inormational capitalism, platorm capitalism, or, as scholar Shoshana Zubof has made amous, surveillance capitalism.10 There is a lot that is useul in those analyses, as they help us see the particular ways in which, within capitalism, data is changing how we produce and consume things. But one crucial thing is missing rom these theories: a historically contextualised account o the sheer scale and seemingly unstoppable social and cultural orce o the data grab under way.

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

It is the huge transer o the world's extracted resources throughout colonialism's history that is the crucial reerence point or making sense o the global data grab. The landgrab o the world's data is not a recent aberration (a side-eect o technological development that has somehow gone wrong) as prominent critics such as Zubo seem to suggest,11 but a continuation o what is now a very old story o extraction. To ignore this and pretend that the great accumulation o wealth today by Big Tech doesn't have an extractivist undercurrent that mirrors earlier colonial landgrabs is to engage in historical amnesia. Acknowledging, as we must, that in many parts o the world capitalism still benefts rom the legacy o historic colonial dispossession should not prevent us rom seeing the new colonial landgrab that is under way beore our eyes.

Only thinking through colonialism and capitalism in this way enables us to see the startling possibility implied in Tracy's worries: Yes, there is a new colony, a new zone of extraction. Data colonialism exploits it. What it exploits is our lives as human beings. Wherever we are. It is the lives o all human beings that are potentially or the taking. And this capture is part o our devices and platorms working exactly as intended: it is not a result o rogue activity or something going wrong. Exploitation is a eature o the routine operation o our devices.

Does this mean that today's proound inequalities in who su ers most, inherited o course rom historic colonialism, no longer matter? Absolutely not. Does it mean that this new colonialism threatens something undamental to the lives o all human beings, whatever their race, class, gender or sexuality? Yes, it does. It is this strange combination – the continuation o colonialism's inherited inequalities and colonialism's acquisition o new tools that potentially aect human lie in radically new ways – that we must understand. And it is exactly this combination that the Cameroonian social theorist Achille Mbembe reers to provocatively, even or many counter-intuitively, as 'the Becoming Black o the world',12 a phrase we'll unpack in Chapter Five.

But what does this mean or our view o colonialism as an evolving historical phenomenon?

#### Colonialism, Like Capitalism, Keeps Mutating

I colonialism is still relevant to how we interpret contemporary capitalism's latest practices with data, what does that mean or how we understand colonialism as a long-term historical process?

Colonialism and capitalism keep changing and adapting, like most historical orces do. Social movements, scientifc understandings, cultural trends, religions . . . those things don't just 'begin' and 'end'. They are not static. They keep evolving and transorming. This is true o capitalism, and we argue that it is true o colonialism too.

The most obvious mutation in colonialism is that we've gone rom a landgrab to a data grab. This is a relatively recent development, but that doesn't mean it won't alter the organisation o human lie or a long time, just as the impact o the original colonial landgrab still reverberates today. The data grab is already changing how education, health and our workplaces are experienced in more and more parts o the world.

Another important mutation in colonialism is the dierent role that violence plays in the new iteration. Historic colonialism was brutal and inhuman. But as we saw in the last chapter, it also ound ways to operate without direct physical violence (mostly through cultural and economic violence). Which brings us to an important question: i physical violence is largely absent rom data colonialism, can we still call it colonialism?

Here we must keep in mind that, as we argued previously, colonialism is evolving in sync with the social relations that are part o capitalism. Centuries ago, colonisers had just two means at their disposal to grab the land and the gold: violence and lies. They oten used both, killing, pillaging and exchanging trinkets or gold. At

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

that time there were no alternative ways o achieving the colonial goal o grabbing resources at the scale, speed and proft margin the colonisers wanted, or colonisers and colonised had absolutely no pre-existing social relations with each other.

Today, ater fve centuries o colonialism and two centuries o capitalism, that kind o violence is no longer necessary, or rather, it has evolved and become more symbolic. Your dispossession (your loss o control over the data that aects you and the impacts this has on your ability to control the terms on which you work, get loans, educate your children, and so on) may be no less absolute, but no violence is needed to persuade you to click the box that says 'I agree to the terms and conditions' beore installing an app. That click alone, by virtue o the vast legal and practical inrastructure o capitalist social relations, is enough to plunge us into endless spirals o data extraction. In other words, today's orms o extraction are almost rictionless, although that doesn't mean their long-term repercussions are entirely non-violent, as indeed Tracy eared in terms o the pregnancy app's implications or her control over her own body. The key point is that, violence or no violence, a comparable colonial act o appropriation can still be perormed. This takes us to one more observation about the link between colonialism and capitalism.

#### Colonialism's Core Mission Stays the Same

I colonialism keeps evolving, we cannot expect its present permutations to be a mirror image o the colonial practices we've seen over the past 500 years. Indeed, it's pointless as well as oensive to suggest that using Facebook makes us slaves in the same way that plantation workers were enslaved. O course it doesn't, nor does it need to, or a colonial-scale extraction to be going on.

Lots o things have changed in fve centuries, and we would expect today's data colonialism to be distinctive in its contours rom its previous versions o colonial appropriation. We can best explain this by separating out the orm, content and unction o colonialism throughout history.

Because colonialism has developed over centuries, its orm and content have shited and adapted with the times. By form we mean the social and economic eatures o colonialism at any one moment. Think, or instance, o how planter colonialism (grabbing land to grow cash crops, while not necessarily living on that land) was very dierent rom settler colonialism (appropriating land to live on it). By content we mean the specifc ways that overall orm maniested itsel in dierent places and times. Think o how colonialism in Mexico by the Spaniards looked and elt very dierent rom colonialism in India by the British. All o these historical orms o colonialism are obviously very dierent, in turn, rom data colonialism. In no way do we want to deny those dierences.

But more important is the similarity, the thing that these orms o colonialism share, which makes them all orms o historically evolving colonialism. And that is the function – or, i you like, the core mission – o colonialism. The core act, the basic meaning o all historical types o colonialism, was to extract, to dispossess. That is why the image o the landgrab is still relevant to understand what's going on with data today, because colonialism always and everywhere means dispossessing others o what is theirs, and taking it regardless o their rights. Whatever the nationality o the coloniser, whatever the geographical location or time period, whatever the legal structure, colonialism has always been about colonisers taking something that does not belong to them through orceul means.

We are not merely saying that what's going on with data is like historical colonialism, or resembles it at a superfcial level. We are saying that, through our relations to digital platorms, a new and expanded colonial order really is being built. This new colonial order based on data extraction will dispossess almost everyone in the long run o the ability to control the judgements that are made

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

about them; and it will do this based on the data extracted rom their lives, just as Tracy ears.

All this is so ar happening without much outcry. A key reason is that we are ed narratives that disguise the reality o what's happening. One such narrative repeats a colonial view o resources as cheap.

#### The Colonial Economy of 'Cheap' Resources

Businesspeople oten say that the data they extract rom our time online is 'just there'. It's what we generate, eortlessly, as we move around online, and unless they (the corporations) put it to good use through careul data processing, it would go to waste. Wouldn't it be too bad i all that knowledge were lost, the data that might help solve most o humanity's problems? So why not let control o this vast 'data exhaust' pass to business or ree?

The echoes here o the arguments historical colonisers once used to justiy their taking o somebody else's land are uncanny. They argued that the land and natural resources they took were abundant, ree, and 'just there' or the taking – in other words, cheap. There was a whole 'new' world waiting to be developed by the coloniser, whose arrival was oten deemed sucient to justiy a declaration that the new territories were his and his alone. An old legal doctrine was conveniently revived to make sense o this: the land taken was said to be without owner (terra nullius, which in Latin means No One's Land).

Inconveniently or the coloniser, some o that land had people living on it. But rom their Eurocentric perspective, those people had no rights to the land, in part because they just weren't using it to its ull capacity (they weren't 'civilised' enough). Because the colonisers could be relied upon to make the land more productive (according to their defnition o productivity), they had an overriding right, they believed, to just take the land and do what they wanted with it.13 Later colonisers, particularly the English, developed the idea that colonised peoples were o a human type that was 'naturally servile', compared with the colonisers' supposed natural propensity to rule.14

In order to put those natural riches to good use, the colonisers realised they needed another important resource: human labour. Thus, the labour o the colonised came also to be portrayed by the coloniser as cheap and abundant – just like cheap nature. According to the coloniser's view o the world, some people were predestined to do that work, and this ate was mostly determined by their race. The labour o local or imported populations o colour in the colonies came at a low cost, at least i you excluded the costs o the violence that held it under control. Jan Pieterszoon Coen, a governor o the Dutch East Indies and the ounder o Batavia (now Jakarta), justied the Dutch exploitation o native populations this way: 'May not a man in Europe do what he likes with his cattle? Even so does the master do with his men'.15 In this way, cheap labour transormed cheap nature into a ready and abundant source o wealth.

Looking along the timeline o colonialism, we see a transition rom cheap nature to cheap labour to, now . . . cheap data.

Cheap data involves the same extractive rationality that we saw with cheap nature and cheap labour. Think o how much data we generate through our activities online, uploading and tagging content, liking posts, sharing things, posting our own content. All o this takes our time and efort, sometimes too much o it. But in the hands o the platorms, it arrives absolutely or ree, without payment. Such data is said to be abundant, without owner, and 'just there' or the taking. Yes, you and we might produce it individually, but by the time it is aggregated, it is considered an owner-less exhaust or by-product, over which we can no longer exercise any claims.

Like many other colonial resources, however, data needs processing, rening. That job is technology-intensive and requires

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

highly specialised tools. Which means only big and sophisticated corporations can do it. Our only job, it seems, is to generate the resource. We are told that this is what progress looks like, that our data is used for connecting us better, building better communities and fuelling the AI that will solve humanity's problems. But so far the real beneciaries are not us; they are the owners of the platforms and AI models that help maximise their prots.

#### Data and the Continuation of Colonial Violence by Other Means

We said that the colonial data grab of the twenty-rst-century economy can be eective without violence, and explained why that is. But that does not mean there is no violence associated with data colonialism. Far from it.

Yes, data colonialism exhibits dierent forms of violence from those encountered in the past. As we know, the inhabitants of the colonised lands were discarded or eliminated by the millions. But instead of the vicious physical brutality of historic colonialism, data colonialism makes possible novel forms of symbolic violence,16 such as those that come with discrimination, loss of opportunity and the classication of people into disadvantageous categories by AI and its algorithms. These forms of symbolic violence can mesh together to have long-term eects that are indeed physical. Being passed over for a job or being denied certain welfare benets by an automated decision system, while initially a symbolic act, can have very concrete and physical consequences for people's health and well-being.

In other words, data colonialism has its own distinctive forms of oppression. Violence in historical colonialism was organised through racial hierarchies, and its impacts were felt through brute force. Although violence in data colonialism may be less brutal, and its particular tools and techniques may be new, its impacts are still concentrated in certain populations, and they oten continue the legacy o racist, sexist and class violence that started with historical colonialism. Vulnerable populations within these categories are, ater all, the most likely to be subjected to more pervasive orms o surveillance at work or school, or rely on precarious orms o gig employment, or be subjected to algorithmic-promoted hate speech because o their identity or status.

Nor is algorithmic discrimination the only type o symbolic violence associated with data colonialism. As a social order, data colonialism also attacks and undermines alternative orms o thinking and o being. This is a complex point that will gradually become clear as the book's argument unolds. But our basic claim is that, as human habits become colonised by Big Tech and digital platorms, it becomes easy to orget that there once were other ways o being social beore extractivist platorms came along; to orget why it is we eel compelled to install apps on our phones, or why we don't question who is collecting the data generated by our smart cars or appliances. Ater all, everybody else is doing it! Nobody wants to eel let out, or be what evangelists o Big Tech call a miserablist (someone who only sees the negative side o these new technologies). But i we lose the habit o questioning the assumed benets o these products, we risk orgetting what the point o resisting the new order would be.

What then are the specic harms that data colonialism commits?

#### Why the Effects of Data Colonialism Will Never Be Evenly Distributed

Some people have more means to protect their privacy. Maybe they can aford an Apple device, which is more expensive but which harvests less data than other brands o devices (or at least keeps that data within the Apple corporation, instead o selling it to a third party). Or maybe they know how to install ad blockers or VPN services. Or maybe they are just the type o wealthier consumer who

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

tends to win out when ad-targeting produces good prices or them, or when algorithms make decisions that give them exactly the services they want . . . to put it crudely, the ones who employ gig workers, not gig workers themselves.

Needless to say, this inequality is nothing new. Historical colonialism wasn't just about making vast amounts o money, but about reinorcing which types o people had access to the world's resources and which didn't. The same impulses are exhibited in data colonialism. There is, and will continue to be, a data colonial elite, and its composition will be heavily shaped by the legacy o the historical colonial elite.

But even or those who do eel they benet overall rom our deal with Big Tech, it is important still to acknowledge that plat orms and algorithms are not as kind to everybody else. But who is this 'everybody else'? Who does not benet to the same extent as we do when participating in data colonialism? For the oreseeable uture, they will include the people who have always paid a heavier price during colonialism: the poor, people o colour, and women. Colonialism may keep evolving, but its unequal legacies and injustices go on being perpetuated.

This continuity makes even more sense when we remember another key eature o colonialism: social classication. Classication is a general eature o society, and doing it in a new way through automated decision-making potentially afects everyone. Since data is exactly a tool or discriminating between things and people, it is no surprise that bias and prejudice are replicated when data is collected and ed to a system that can make automated decisions about the welare o already vulnerable groups. Indeed, this kind o systematic discrimination, which allows one group o people to classiy and control the rest, is very much a colonial invention. Strategies o divide and conquer by colonisers have or centuries been partitioning societies along the lines o class, gender and race: rich above poor, men above women and gender non-conorming individuals, and white over everybody else (and also everybody else against each other, since hierarchies promote competition). This structure was and still is based around a gure at its apex: the superior rational authority o the (white, male) coloniser. The racial segregation behind Jim Crow laws in the US, behind apartheid in South Arica, or behind the British Empire's colour bar (perected in the colonies and then imported back to the centre o the empire), are all examples o this kind o hierarchy in which rich white men are at the top.

Class, gender and race have thus always been instrumental in creating the myth that human dierences need to be managed and ruled by a universal Western rationality. And data in some orm has or a long time been a part o this project, as it provides the means to quantiy and manage dierence: census data, surveillance data, demographic data.17 In this respect, once again, data colonialism renews the mission o historical colonialism through resh means – algorithmic decision-making, digital platorm design – that are even more subtle and harder to trace than beore. Data, ater all, always discriminates. You can't build a database o Xs, Ys and Zs, unless you have already decided that this is an X, and not a Y or a Z.18

#### The Colonial Continuities of Data Discrimination

Algorithmic discrimination based on data extraction is oten used against those struggling with poverty. This is already happening in rich countries, and it is likely to spread more widely as algorithmic decision-making and its techniques are increasingly imposed by corporations and governments. In her 2018 book, Automating Inequality, Virginia Eubanks analyses public assistance systems related to Medicaid, homelessness, ood assistance and assistance or at-risk children in the United States. In many o these systems, who gets access to the services is determined automatically by algorithms, whose operation is not transparent and seemingly arbitrary. Eubanks describes the punitive and harmul eects inficted on

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

those who are deemed ineligible or these benets. Class intersects here with race and gender, which brings to light the demeaning and excessive amount o data surveillance that is inficted on the poor (and more pronouncedly on poor Black women).

As a result, it is those at the bottom o the social pile with only a cheap phone and limited contract, who depend on that phone and its apps to get their next insecure job, who have literally no chance o renegotiating in any way the privacy-constraining conditions those apps and their employers impose upon them. All this may happen even against the best intentions o those devising the data systems, because o the wider social power inequalities in the midst o which they are used, and the huge inormation asymmetry built into the platorms themselves.19

This is also painully clear when we turn to gender, where the violence o extractivist platorms is just as harmul. The eects range rom the destructive infuence social media algorithms can have on teenage girls and their sense o their own bodies (an infuence plat orms like Facebook seem to be ully aware o, but which, according to the testimony o whistle-blower Frances Haugen to US Congress in October 2021,20 they reused to regulate in order to maximise prots), to the increased 'dystopian' workplace surveillance that women and minority workers endure,21 to the general harassment and bullying that women experience online because o platorms' ailure to put basic protections in place.

Take pornography. The connection with data colonialism might not be immediately apparent, but consider how datacation has allowed highly protable companies – Pornhub received around 4.5 billion visits per month in 202022 – to prioritise the monetisation o content over the welare o women.
