There is a standard practice on those sorts o platorms o allowing or the posting o abusive content (sometimes involving minors) anonymously, without the subject's consent, and without eective recourse or having it removed. There is also the predatory practice o deepake porn, using AI to realistically superimpose women's aces onto pornographic material without their consent.

But digital violence against women goes well beyond the nonconsensual dissemination o sexual content. Writing rom Mexico, a country where on average ten women are murdered every day, Grecia Macías identies the multiple ways in which women experience various orms o digital violence, including 'unauthorized access to one's devices, digital threats, smear campaigns, extortion, attacks on one's means o expression, identity thet, the manipulation o inormation, surveillance and stalking.'23 Data rom around the world provides plenty o examples: according to a study involving young women in Ghana, Kenya and Vietnam, 70 per cent o them report being exposed to censorship, verbal abuse, stalking, extortion and violence when using social media to seek healthrelated support or sharing health inormation;24 73 per cent o women journalists have experienced online violence; hal o young women in the UK have received unsolicited sexual images;25 and non-white women candidates in the US are twice as likely to be the targets o mis- or disinormation and our times more likely to be the victims o online abuse than white candidates.26 It is true that most o this violence is perpetrated by individual males (although when it comes to surveillance and censorship, the state is also a major aggressor). But as with the example o non-consensual sexual content, platorms denitely play a role in minimising or ignoring the risk that their technologies and business models represent or women (in a recent example, two women sued Apple, claiming that Air Tags made it easier or stalkers to harass them).27

These impacts are oten urther compounded when it comes to gender non-conorming people. In addition to the orms o violence mentioned above, they may be harmed by the very idea on which data-extracting corporations build, which is to x people into this category rather than another. This process is what the inormation scholar Anna Lauren Hofmann calls 'data violence', and it is

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

automatically meted out to those who don't accept being categorised in simple gender terms.28

For all of these reasons, it is important that, as Catherine D'Ignazio and Lauren F. Klein argue, the design, production and evaluation of digital technologies is informed by a data science aligned with feminist values and practices, which can help to both expose inequalities and change the distribution of power.29

Race, as has recently become very clear, is also a crucial factor in determining who benets, and who doesn't, from data colonialism. There are many examples of how people of colour (or POC) undergo algorithmic discrimination in everything from insurance rates to targeted advertising to court sentencing to police surveillance, and more. Some eects are more indirect. For instance, a recent Pro Publica report uncovered an algorithm helping landlords in the US set the highest possible rents on tenants, exacerbating the current housing crisis in a way that is likely to impact tenants dierently depending on their race.30

Consider another algorithm used widely by US hospitals and insurance companies to determine the level of healthcare that patients were assigned. In a 2019 study published in the journal Science, 31 researchers found that the algorithm, used to manage the care of over 200 million people, exhibited a racial bias. Apparently, when considering patients with similar ailments, the algorithm more frequently referred white patients than Black patients for additional healthcare. In short, Black people had to be sicker to receive the same healthcare than their white counterparts.

How could this happen? The algorithm assigned a risk score to a patient based on the amount of money the insurance company spent on them, and then recommended additional care based on that risk score. But because of the powerful background of institutional racism and Black people's resulting distrust towards the healthcare system, they consume less health resources than white people, which meant that simply relying on such data guaranteed them a lower risk score even when they were, in act, more sick than their white counterparts. The result was lower recommendations to Black people or additional care.

Are racist engineers designing these systems intentionally to behave in racist ways? Not necessarily. We have to remember that these systems don't operate in a vacuum. In order or an AI system to 'learn', it must be trained with lots o examples rom the real world. And i those examples reect the bias and discrimination that we fnd in our real world, guess what's going to happen. Predictions based on those examples will reproduce that underlying discrimination. Dierences in outcomes rom data-driven systems can thereore be not just a unction o preerence or intent, but o deeper social structures like racism.

The result, indisputably, is that data systems o all sorts reinorce stereotypes and institutionalised discrimination. Indeed, data scientists themselves have proved that narrow approaches to reorming AI Ethics miss the wider structural orces that shape the inputs to algorithmic decisions; as a result, we can't rely on algorithms themselves to generate justice.32 Furthermore, the act that the design and deployment o these very complex systems are generally in the hands o small numbers o privileged white people (itsel the longterm legacy o colonialism among other things) means that the voices o the rest o society tend not to be heard, at least until the damage has already been done.

That most o us do not even think o questioning the premises under which class, gender and racial discrimination operate in data systems is testament to the act that inherited colonial ways o viewing the world are frmly entrenched in our thinking. In addition, our unwavering belie in the rightness o data extraction and Big Tech's supposedly superior rationality leads us to trust whatever civilising mission the computer scientists put orward to 'improve' our lives, overriding our doubts.

It is not that data itsel is bad – how could it be? We need data and inormation about disease, about the stars, about the ora and

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

auna that surround us, about the damage we collectively do to the planet. But the issue is how data is extracted rom what, rom whom, and on what terms.

It makes no sense to be against the idea o data in the abstract. But a social order or extracting data rom human lie under very particular conditions that benet the ew and not the many is another matter entirely: it is data colonialism.

#### The Colonial Roots of AI

The social structures that today make the continuous extraction o data rom our lives a comortable and even natural reality have been in development or hundreds o years. The business models and scientic principles endorsed by Big Tech and AI seem to have emerged quickly and out o nowhere, but they are rmly rooted in processes that are much older and well established. As we began to see in the earlier discussion o cheap data, colonial impulses do not simply disappear one day: they get recongured and reocussed on new processes.

To start tracing these continuities, we need to recognise that Western science, as well as capitalism, co-evolved side by side with colonialism. This is a subject that has lled volumes, so here we will simply point out that the need to manage colonies during colonialism was satised by the development o orms o knowledge that made the management o those colonies more efcient. And these orms o knowledge – that is, modern Western sciences such as botany, geology, anthropology andzoology –resulted in technological breakthroughs that allowed corporations to rule the world. Let's take a closer look at how this process unolded.

According to Steven Harris, the operation o long-distance corporations in colonialism required substantial investments in science.33 Enterprises such as the Casa de la Contratación de las Indias or the East India Company were tasked with managing colonies remotely, across great distances. Mexico had to be managed all the way rom Madrid, and Delhi rom London, at a time when the handwritten or printed word was still the main means o communication. So new tools had to be developed by scientists and inventors employed by these corporations to make the long-distance management o colonies more efcient.

While the standard view o history encourages us to think o science as the outcome o contributions by individual male geniuses (Galileo, Bacon, Newton, etc.), Harris shows that colonialism itsel helped to introduce a kind o Big Science developed by a collection o lesser-known scientists. Those contributions had just as much impact as that o the scientic celebrities, but they get a lot less attention. A global network o scientists and practitioners worked in concert or the benet o their employers or institutions. Much o this went on in the colonies. For example, between 1717 and 1738 Spain imported 45 tons a year o medicinal plants rom the Americas, a number that increased to 155 tons a year between 1747 and 1778. The knowledge o how to use these plants came rom indigenous peoples, but was systematised and commercialised by apothecary missionaries in the colonies, as the name o the popular ever-controlling plant o the time, cinchona, known as Jesuits' bark in Europe, implies.34

By the end o the seventeenth century, these Big Sciences (medicinal botany, observational astronomy, geography, natural history, meteorology, navigation, etc.) had 'modernised' themselves into what today we would call distributed or virtual teams. There were Royal Societies or Academies or this or that discipline, which invented new technologies and tools, sometimes adapted rom or inspired by pre-Columbian inventions in medicine, architecture, astronomy, transportation, and so on (rom cable suspension bridges to syringes to oral contraceptives).35 They also generated vast amounts o data stored in reports, charts, maps and handbooks, and

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

curated great repositories o specimens like the botanical gardens o Amsterdam and Leiden, or the Royal Botanic Gardens at Kew in London (which developed new strains o coee, cocoa, maize and various ruits that were better suited to local diets).36

In other words, colonial expansion and administration was carried out by global corporations (a sort o Big Tech) employing hundreds o scientists and technicians (Big Science) who developed the intellectual and technological structures required or the political, military and cultural subjugation o the world, in the interest o proft making. This required the collection and analysis o vast amounts o inormation rom the colonies (Big Data), which became an important asset or their management.

Much o the inormation that was collected by the colonisers was applied in the surveillance and control o colonised peoples (Big Brother). This extended into the political management o populations too, through mechanisms like the construction o security ences to divide populations, the panopticon design o jails to place prisoners under constant observation, and various sorts o early orms o biometric data collection – all o which became commonplace in the colonies beore being implemented in European cities.37

It is thereore the long history o colonialism, not just the last ew decades o the Digital Revolution, that is the best starting point or understanding how these processes are converging. Already, centuries ago – long beore GAFA (Google, Apple, Facebook and Amazon), their Chinese equivalents BATX (Baidu, Alibaba, Tencent and Xiaomi) and the rest o the players that make up today's data colonial class – the cumulative inventions o Big Science throughout modernity allowed Big Tech to use Big Data to act as Big Brother. Which means that what's going on with data is not just capitalist business as usual, or even a problematic recent variant o capitalism which needs taming. Large-scale data extraction, whether to run platorms or uel generative AI, represents the continuation o a long-established way o organising the world's economies, societies and power relations on a prooundly unequal and exclusionary basis.38 Within this longer context, we can see clearly that the extraordinary scale and impact o today's new data extraction has, in act, a clear parallel in history: the start o historical colonialism.

#### The Resilience of Colonialism

This historical raming helps us see beyond the scandals and market uncertainties that Big Tech periodically conronts.

Take the recent claim that Big Tech's 'ree ride' might be coming to an end. Although Amazon, Apple, Facebook, Google and Microsot posted record prots during the pandemic – US \$1.4 trillion in 2021, or a 55 per cent increase rom a base that was already quite high39 – recent trends seem to suggest a slowdown, as well as a host o other problems or Big Tech.

This claim seems to be reinorced by evidence that data privacy rules are being regulated more efectively across the globe, curtailing extractivism to some extent. Europe's GDPR is a good example; in addition, 35 out o 50 states in the US are at least considering some orm o privacy regulations.40 Indeed, the stronghold that Meta (Facebook) and Alphabet (Google) have on the digital ads market seems to be diminishing, decreasing rom a 54.7 per cent share o the market in 2017 to a predicted 43.9 per cent by 2024.41 Still quite substantial, but a decrease nonetheless, and perhaps a sign o healthy competition. Furthermore, legal woes are starting to catch up with companies like Meta, who recently settled its Cambridge Analytica scandal or US \$725 million, but then sufered European rulings that challenged how it exploits user data or advertising.42 All major tech companies have pending lawsuits throughout the world in areas such as competition, privacy,

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

misinormation and disinormation, illegal and abusive conduct including sexual exploitation, and algorithmic discrimination.43

For start-ups, the situation has also recently become dicult, as venture capital dries up and orces them to enter into less avourable debt deals.44 All in all, some experts are reconsidering their ideas about seemingly endless high growth rates in this sector (although many o these companies remain extremely protable).45

Does this mean Big Tech will wither and disappear, and data colonialism will collapse beore it has even reached its ull scale? Not likely. One o their responses to this crisis has been simply to re workers. US-based tech companies laid of 131,000 workers in 2023 (as o March, with more expected in the rest o the year) and more than 93,000 in 2022,46 although many o those sacked by Big Tech companies are nding employment within the general economy.47 Ater Elon Musk acquired Twitter, 50 per cent o its workorce was laid of.48 This is a good reminder that as ortunes expand and decrease, it is workers, not shareholders, who bear the brunt o hardship. One need only look at the poverty-stricken slums that emerge around Amazon warehouses in places like Tijuana, Mexico, to get a visual reminder o this act: next to state-o-the-art Amazon acilities stand shacks built out o tin, cardboard and plastic, where workers live. A similar scene is replicated across communities where Amazon operates, although salaries and living conditions might be a bit better in the Global North. Amazon receives tax breaks and other incentives to construct their warehouses, on the premise that they generate new jobs. But the jobs that Amazon creates are poorly paid (with hourly rates o around US \$2.60 in Tijuana), and with less benets or workers than the jobs they requently eliminate.49

Another response to crisis might be simply to double-down, reinorcing current extractivist practices so that consumers and citizens, not just workers, pay the price. This is what Naomi Klein called 'disaster capitalism': the idea that corporations and governments take advantage o periods o natural or nancial disaster to impose more oppressive measures.50 The Covid pandemic is a good example o this dynamic. According to research conducted by the European Center or Not-or-Prot Law, the International Network o Civil Liberties Organizations and Privacy International, surveillance technologies were misused by governments during Covid emergency periods by enabling the abuse o personal data, the silencing o dissent, the repurposing o counter-terrorism measures to track civilians and the extension o these measures beyond the pandemic.51 These our trends were accompanied by a th one: the increasing inuential role o the private sector as a provider o these extractivist technologies.52

The lesson is that, whatever economic slumps the tech sector might experience along the way, they – in partnership with governments – will probably nd ways to externalise the threat and pass it on to the public, oten in the orm o amplied data extractivism practices that afect workers and consumers. That is why the current economic downturn probably won't spell the end o data colonialism. Nor will the collapse o large social media platorms like Meta, or even the end o surveillance capitalism that some people predict (we ourselves are sceptical),53 mean the end o data colonialism, because, as we will show, data colonialism is much wider than either o them.

#### We Need Not Be Passive Victims

This is only the rst chapter o the book, but the problems posed by data colonialism may already seem insurmountable. What to do but throw up our hands in horror and then go on just as beore? In any case, isn't our data already out there in the hands o corporations?

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

We get that reaction a lot. Ater all, i these are the same problems humanity has ailed to resolve in 500 years o colonialism, what are the chances we will succeed in resisting colonialism's new ormation? We will continue to detail the serious, sometimes devastating, impact o data colonialism over the next ew chapters, so there is still a lot more bad news to cover, unortunately. But we also want to give a sense o what the starting points or resisting this emerging social order might be.

Even at the beginning o historical colonialism, there were people who questioned it and resisted it. Colonial subjects have always had this power, a power that in the end led to overthrowing the political structures o colonialism, and so do we. The coloniser simply cannot exist without the colonised, so the latter have always had the power o reusal (even i it has come at a very high cost, including oten the ultimate sacrice o lie itsel). To begin exploring this resistive power, let's consider our own role in this system.

As colonialism developed, a Eurocentric worldview became enormously infuential, shaping the consciousness o the colonised and pushing them to believe that Western power was incontestable, that Western science was the only orm o reasoning capable o explaining the world, that Western religion was the only one capable o saving the soul, and that Western culture was the most advanced expression o humanity. Colonialism, in other words, gave the colonised a sense o ineriority that has taken – and continues to take – a lot o work and time to overcome.

Similarly, when it comes to the new data colonialism, it is easy to eel that we don't have the reedom or opportunity to do anything but accept this new world order; that we have no choice but to click a button that says 'I accept'. But while there is a lot o deception and exploitation at work in getting us to click that button, and some o us need to click that button just to earn a living, the act is that we are not helpless. Put simply, i we accept something, we can also un-accept it.

#### Data Grab

And that's or a clear reason. In the end, data colonialism needs us. Without our data, it does not work. Most data is not extracted without our consent in some orm, and we are thus participants in the act o extraction. True, stopping our consent may only help us against uture extraction, not the impacts o all the data previously extracted that aects us, but that does not mean Big Tech no longer needs our consent. Big Tech works by us not ully understanding the implication o being complicit in its extractivist systems, but or now we do remain complicit to various degrees, depending on our situation. In order or anything to change, it is important that we frst recognise the ull extent o our complicity in what is unolding.

When reviewing the history o colonialism, some talk about the role o the 'native inormant'. These were members o the colonised class who recognised early on who would be on the 'winning' side o history, and decided to align with them, acting as translators not just linguistically, but culturally. A good example is La Malinche (aka Malintzin, aka Doña Marina), the Nahua slave woman said to be gited to conquistador Hernán Cortés who acted as his translator, consort and advisor during the conquest o Mexico. While it is easy to portray such inormants as traitors, we cannot guess what their actual conditions and intentions were. They were probably just trying to survive the collapse o their world, and the emergence o a strange new one. In cultural lore (at least in Latin America), these inormants are seen as the native mothers who, along with the European athers, gave birth to a new hybrid race o people (not necessarily out o love, but out o violence).

But in data colonialism we are all informants. We literally translate our lives or the coloniser by conducting our social lives on their platorms. This is the disturbing realisation to which Tracy came ater some reection. However, that's not the whole story. One advantage we have at this point in history is that we already

#### A NEW COLONIALISM

know what the colonial system wants, and how it works. We have lots o examples o how colonialism can be resisted, and we can still resist i we are clear about what is really going on.

But frst, let's take a closer look at how this dispossession is unolding in our daily lives.

#### CHAPTER 2

### DATA TERRITORIES

NOW IN HIS forties, Michael had promised himself and his family to lose some weight and live a healthier life. So, when his employer offered to enrol him in a wellness programme and give him a free digital fitness tracker, he didn't think twice about it. The device looked cool and sleek on his wrist, and he had received many positive comments about it from his friends. People couldn't believe it was free. His doctor was pleased, and mentioned it would be a great opportunity to track those blood pressure issues to which, as a Black man, he should be paying more attention. Bring on the information revolution, he thought.

He soon found himself checking the device every few minutes to measure his exercise activities, sleeping habits, caloric burn rate, blood pressure, and more. He would get a bit annoyed when the device admonished him for not meeting his daily activity quotas. Work quotas, exercise quotas, quotas all the time! Although maybe that was a good thing, he thought. Helps keep me on track.

But Michael also started noticing some weird things. Why did he start seeing online ads for the healthy smoothie place he passed during his walks? Also, his employer announced a new initiative at work to combat stress, which data had shown was on the rise at his particular office branch. What data? Were they using data from the fitness monitors?1 Another time, after a night of heavy drinking (which he regretted), he noticed the device showed he had been burning a lot o calories. 'Oh yeah,' said his co-worker Fred, 'I noticed it does that, probably because drinking elevates your heart rate. So, it probably knows when you've had one too many.' That's a bit creepy, thought Michael. He also read in one o his Facebook groups that the particular tness monitor he was using was 'racist', meaning that the device (which uses a green light to measure blood fow) was not able to generate accurate readings in users who have dark skin, a design faw that the company had not shown much interest in addressing.

But that wasn't the hal o it. Somewhere in the terms o use, which he hadn't really read, Michael had given permission or all o his health data – in act all o his 'health-related' data – to be shared. This meant that, potentially, data collected by the tracker that had nothing to do with Michael's health (such as his location or activities) could be shared not only with his employer, but with marketing rms, the authorities, or even stolen by hackers.2

As Michael's story illustrates, it's not exactly news that there's something problematic with how data is collected and used. We oten rst hear o this via stories o how our data is not sae rom hackers. Think o the data leaks through poorly secured data storage: in 2017, a data breach at leading US credit broker Equiax exposed the records o 147 million US citizens and millions o others in the UK and Canada, which was just one o many data breaches across many countries in the past decade.3 But we also learn about the problems o data mining through stories about poorly designed algorithms that silently discriminate against us. Even mainstream business outlets like the Financial Times talk critically o the 'surveillance model' o Big Tech businesses: making money by tracking us and our data.

Are these just the teething troubles that any major new industry can expect to ace? Or perhaps the exploits o some rogue players that need reining in? Isn't the right way o handling this to avoid

#### DATA TERRITORIES

putting anything personal on Facebook, switch rom an Android to an Apple phone (i you can aford one), and trust in the government to bring in some reasonable privacy controls on Big Tech?

The answer is no. Such piecemeal measures miss the real point, which is that there is a much wider landgrab o data going on across societies today.

Historical colonialism was nothing without the control o new territory. It was this territorial control that made possible the extraction o new resources on a global scale and the creation o vast new global markets or the benet o the colonisers. The regions we now call South and Central America, the Caribbean, and North America seemed like a 'New World' to their European colonisers. But they were no newer than Europe was, and they were inhabited by First Nation peoples who generally had to be displaced or colonial processes o extraction to begin. There were multiple models or generating the labour needed to exploit those new territories at scale: the importing o slave labour to the plantations o the Caribbean and parts o South and North America or, alternatively, the sending o settlers rom Europe to live on the colonised land. With Britain's capture o India, a urther colonial model developed: absolute control o the terms o trade with India and the managing o the local economy through military orce. A combination o these diferent models spread to Asia, Arica and the Pacic. But whatever the model o labour relied upon, historical colonialism depended on the control o territory.

The volume o land acquired and controlled was extraordinary: at the height o their colonial power, Spain controlled 5.3 million square miles beyond its national borders and Great Britain 13.7 million square miles.4 The economic impact o the additional land that European countries were thus able to exploit was huge, although estimates vary: one scholar argues that it increased sixfold the land per capita o the European colonising nations.5 This territorial expansion was the basis not only or economic exploitation but or completely transorming the everyday lie o the controlled territories.

But data is not like land: you can't touch it and you can't walk around in it. It might thereore seem strange to talk about grabbing data in the way that colonisers once grabbed physical territory. Indeed, data can be endlessly copied and reused; it is in principle what economists call a 'non-rival good', so multiple parties can use it at the same time, making it seem even more remote rom physical things like land or bodies.

So, i data is today's new type o colonial resource, how can a 'territory' be created that is controllable in ways that ensure data's maximum extraction? In short, by writing code. More precisely, by writing the specifc code that builds new computer-accessed spaces and orces any interaction within those spaces to unold in ways that enable the continuous extraction o data about those interactions. We will call those spaces data territories.

In everyday lie, we know those data territories generally as digital 'platorms' – think Facebook and Amazon. But as we will see, there are many other types o data territories. The interace o a search engine like Google is another type o data territory, as are the interaces o countless 'smart' tools and devices. All o them give us access to a commercially controlled space rom which data can be exclusively and continuously extracted rom us. We are not just talking about single territories, but about networks o data territories: through sotware, and without any physical constraints, any data territory can be connected to countless others to create a vast archipelago o connected data territories. Multiply those connections still urther and the whole surace o social lie becomes covered many times over by relations o data extraction.

Even though data is not land, data territories can be controlled by power with a completeness that rivals, perhaps surpasses, that o the original colonial rulers. The seizure o data territories thereore becomes the start o a larger transormation, both economic and

#### DATA TERRITORIES

social, that builds on these new relations. Just as colonial territories imposed new laws on their inhabitants, so data territories exert their own 'laws' based on the code that keeps the platorm running.

#### When Society Becomes the Territory

How did data colonialism's new territorial capture begin? There was nothing quite as dramatic as the physical discovery o supposedly new continents. Data territories have their basis in sotware that is embedded deep within the devices we use and the non-physical 'spaces' (the apps or platorms or websites) that we access through those devices.

But the emergence o data territories was rapid and dramatic nonetheless. Together, they have opened a 'New World' o data extraction that oers not just data, but a completely new way o organising the relations that make up society, quite literally reorganising society or business. How? By directing our activities into social channels that are ully under businesses' control. Data might be a non-rival good, but what it captures – human lie – is defnitely a fnite resource, which, at root, is what makes this an exploitative process.

We, as consumers o digital services, certainly don't know much about the data-processing operations that go on behind the scenes in a data territory. But i we try to leave that territory, or do things within it that don't conorm to the territory's rules, all sorts o legal and practical constraints get put in our way. In that sense the data territory is defnitely bounded like a physical territory.

The steps through which such data territories came about map directly onto the frst three steps o the Four-X model mentioned in the Introduction: Explore, Exploit, Expand. Signs o the ourth, Exterminate, are also emerging, as we'll see later in the chapter. Let's continue our application o the Four-X model to connect the past o historical colonialism with the present o data colonialism.

#### Exploring New Worlds of Data Capture

Just as the original European explorers didn't know they were about to discover wholly new reserves to be exploited (Columbus believed he would nd new routes to the already-known India), so too the territories o data colonialism emerged by an indirect route.

It all started with a very basic and banal eature o computers: they capture data. To explain how computers capture data, we must look back over the internet history o the past 30 years rom the point o view o social transormation, not just technical evolution. Computers work by capturing organised sets o inormation and storing them, in dynamic and adjustable orm, such as the document that we produced by typing this chapter into our laptops.

Seems innocuous enough. But the US inormation and privacy scientist Philip Agre, writing in 1994, saw the deep social signi cance o this new type o inormation tool. For computers do not just capture inormation in the abstract: they capture it on their own terms. Unless I interact with my computer in very particular ways that comply with its specic rules, nothing gets done. Smile at the keyboard, and nothing happens; I have to move my ngers over the keyboard in particular ways to produce inputs that comply with sets o computer-based rules that Agre called the computer's 'grammar o action'.7 This means, in efect, that computers always impose their grammar o action – their particular template or how you interact with them – on whoever uses them, and that grammar always includes the capture o data. This is the root o the otenrepeated idea that 'code is law':8 platorm code, in efect, sets the laws o data territories.

In act, a computer can capture traces o any interaction, provided the interaction takes a orm that ts with the computer's grammar o action, that is, it is encoded in accordance with the computer's rules o use. So, i my laptop has a camera embedded in it and the camera is switched on, then my smile can be recorded

#### DATA TERRITORIES

and stored. But Agre had in mind much more than the capture o what individual people do on their own computers. He was writing at a time when computers were beginning to be connected ever more easily, in what we now know as the internet. The World Wide Web's design had also recently been announced, which would make particular types o captured inormation placed at particular computer-based locations (websites) searchable by other computers.

What Agre sensed right at the start o our digital era was that, i all computers become connected, then any computer could, in principle, track the contents captured by any other computer. The consequences o this or everyday lie would only grow as people started to use computers more and more across daily lie, and those computers began, all o them, to be connected to each other via the internet.

The year o Agre's article, 1994, was the frst ull year o operations o Netscape, the frst commercial browser. It was also the year when Lou Montulli, an employee at Netscape, invented the 'cookie'. The cookie was initially a device to save us having to keep inputting the same data to a particular website every time we visit it: it certainly is a nuisance when a retail website doesn't remember your address and phone number rom last time you visited. But advertisers soon realised the huge potential or data capture o a technique whereby your clicking on a website generates a cookie sent to your computer that then stores certain inormation (on your computer, but accessible to a distant computer associated with the website). This was the start o all the tracking mechanisms that marketers placed on the web and on our phones that have recently caused a backlash rom Apple and others.

In the early 1990s, when Agre was writing, no one yet knew the intensely commercial path that the internet and World Wide Web would take. But already by the late 1990s, a completely new type o online society was coming into view that would be dominated by commercial corporations and their imperatives for prot. To achieve this, all that was needed was for individuals to live much of their lives on, and through, connected computers, and for businesses to take advantage of the traces stored on those individuals' computers. Two things now so banal that we can't imagine life arranged any other way, but here we have the origin of today's data territories. Data colonialism's phase of exploration had begun.

#### Exploiting Data Territories

Far-sighted legal and communications experts were already warning in the late 1990s that the long-term result of allowing private corporations to manage computer connections on a large scale would be a new structure of power with negative implications for democracy and basic values like privacy. Why? Because a networked computer space dominated by commercial imperatives would set no limit on how computers can track each other's information for commercial advantage; it would set no limit on human beings' vulnerability to continuous data extraction.9 Those experts sensed that the whole of everyday space could be transformed into what, looking back, we can see as data territories.

The story of how this possibility became real has been told many times. You will have lived parts of that story yourself. It took a lot of machinery. One piece of machinery was the Google search algorithm, which quickly became not just the world's dominant search engine, but also the source of personal data that fuelled Google's enormous advertising business. Google had realised that the information about us it had accumulated in order to serve us with more relevant search results was potentially of huge value in predicting other things about us, not just what we searched for. But behind this and similar machinery lay a completely new type of economic exploitation.

Few described this opportunity as clearly as Google's own chief economist, Hal Varian. Google's data territory is by no means the

#### DATA TERRITORIES

only one that matters, but we mention Varian because, in a paper or a dry academic conerence, he laid bare what we can now see as the exploitative inner logic o the data territory: how it enables corporations to use data to exclusively track and infuence their customers. This territory (even the very idea o it) simply had not existed until two decades or so earlier.

Varian's description started with something very simple: there was 'now a computer in the middle o virtually every transaction'.10 A connected computer, o course, since a computer without an internet connection was o no use to Google. What ollowed were certain new social processes that Varian thought important:

- Data extraction and analysis
- Personalisation and customisation
- Continuous experiments
- New kinds o contracts due to better monitoring o what customers do11

Data extraction is obvious: that's the basic thing that, as we've seen, connected computers can do. Personalisation is also amiliar: ad companies and social media platorms constantly tell us that by gathering more data about us, they can make their services more personally relevant to us.

But what about experiments? By 'experiments' Varian meant the possibility that platorms could use their control over computer-based transactions to systematically try out what pricing or other signals would be most eective. As to new contracts, Varian had spotted that, because every transaction conducted online was now directly observable, inormation streams that were beore out o business's reach suddenly became direct inputs to business. This changed the power relations between businesses and customers prooundly, because corporations could dictate interactions on their own terms (such as signing away some o your rights as an individual just by using a platorm) and, in that way, secure your presence in their territory, a territory or continuous data extraction.

Put all this together, and you had a completely new space o commercial power right at the heart o everyday lie. A new type o power relations had emerged based on the extraction o data.

Yet Varian was simply setting out the implications o the business model that his own company had long since pursued. Varian was one o those behind the design o Google's system or auctioning ads, in real time, by drawing on the precise data Google possessed about a user's likelihood to click on that specifc ad (they had, ater all, consistently been tracking what you searched or). Varian saw that every commercial transaction would over time come to operate on the basis o this new sort o tracking-based corporate power, that is, via exploiting data territory.

Varian also saw something else: that this new model o economic exploitation would have the potential to sweep away other orms o economic interaction that extracted less data. Here we see the seed o data colonialism's ourth stage (as per the Four-X model): Exterminate. We'll return to Exterminate as we unold the signs o how data colonialism is starting to override older models o economic and social interaction. For now, just consider Varian's seemingly benign example: the traditional insurance contract. What happens, he asks, when user behaviour can be continuously tracked by insurers?

When I rent a car, somewhere in the fne print there is a statement to the eect that I will operate the car in a sae manner. But how can they veriy that? There used to be no way, but now insurance companies can put vehicular monitoring systems in the car . . . They can use these systems to veriy whether or not you are ulflling your part o the contract. They get a lower accident rate and I get lower prices.12

#### DATA TERRITORIES

Sounds rather like institutionalised surveillance, and indeed that's what it is. And it has become the prevailing model today: 'usagebased' insurance is now booming, especially vehicle insurance. Yet Varian ignored the obvious consequence that the connectedness o the internet propels all o us into a space o continuous surveillance – not by the state, but by corporations – a space where all sorts o data about us can be used by insurers in new ways in data territories o insurers' own making.

Shouldn't saer drivers enjoy lower premiums that reect their driving more closely than through the old-style no-claims bonus? Sure, but every driver makes errors and has bad days. Such per ormance drops may have very dierent consequences in a data territory: whereas once you would have just berated yoursel and hoped no one noticed, i you have a smart insurance policy, you can be sure your car noticed and stored the data or the beneft o the insurer. How this will impact the evaluation o your driving will remain opaque to you.

#### The Expanding Data Colony

Data's modes o expansion are technical and sotware-based; they relentlessly expand the scale o data territories, in the process completely reorganising the spaces where we live and work. Three techniques have proved crucial.

The frst was to expand the range o computers we use in daily interactions. Remember Varian's point that 'there is a computer in every transaction'. Well, in the opening decades o the twenty-frst century, this became ever more the case, as more and more people carried small computers with them that could connect online: their phones. Since then, tablets have emerged as more easily portable versions o laptop and desktop personal computers. Ubiquitous computing has moved rom being the vision o specialised computer scientists to being an everyday reality, in richer countries at least.

The second technique was to create platorms: special-purpose spaces o computer-based interaction where pre-determined types o transactions and interactions can go on, while data is continuously extracted rom people. That's what Facebook is; that's what every app is. Platorms were already thriving when Varian wrote his paper, but they were only just beginning when Google made its rst moves towards monetising its own search data in the early years o the century. Platorms recongure how economic value gets extracted and how economic power works by creating sotware-based territories under the exclusive control o the platorm owner, a control exercised through the management o data.13 Not every attempt at platorm expansion will work: Meta's current attempts to promote participation in the Metaverse right now seem to be leaving investors, shareholders and even its own employees unimpressed,14 and no one knows at time o writing i Apple or Microsot will are any better. But that doesn't afect the general trend towards platorm economies, which are expanding the territories rom which data can be extracted in today's economies and societies.

There is also a third technique, which tries to extend the rst (computer access) in order to gain the advantages o the second (platorms). It adapts physical spaces which we have no choice but to occupy (like our homes or our workplaces) and converts them into data territories via apps that, as we use them, automatically gather data rom smart devices built into those spaces.

The Smart Home is basically a home recongured as a data territory. It is ull o Smart Appliances, which each operate as secondary data territories. Their smartness benets their producers more than us, since it enables them to sense how we interact with them and capture the resulting data or the corporation that installed them. We are talking here about the Internet o Things (or IoT), which has been hailed by business theorists as ushering in a new age o continuous connection. By contrast with the world in which advertisers tracked us only up to the moment they targeted us with an ad,

#### DATA TERRITORIES

Smart objects can monitor us continuously as we use them, building an ever-richer picture o our habits or marketers.15 In the Smart Home, avoiding being tracked is not an option: tracking is a basic eature o the data territory your home has become. Indeed, rent a property or buy a new home today and you may well be required to accept built-in Smart eatures, locking data territories into the larger order o home design.16

In the Smart Home nothing happens (no light is turned on, no ront door is opened) unless mediated by a data-extracting app. Amazon's 2022 purchase o Smart vacuum cleaner maker i Robot or US \$1.7 billion is a sign o where things are heading. Ater all, the Roomba (or at least the more expensive Roomba models sold by i Robot) literally can't work without turning the oorplan o your home into a data territory, rom which data can be systematically extracted and monitored by an external corporation. The data that gets captured need not be narrowly defned. Amazon's separate home robot, Astro, has the ability to gather data just like an automated security guard, checking exits and so on, and then link up with your smart door bell, Amazon's Ring.17 This is probably why i Robot's CEO announced ater the acquisition that 'the home o the uture is a robot'.18 In October 2018 Amazon fled a patent or Alexa that would allow it to track emotions based on personal biometric data, signalling greater ambitions.19 We'll come back to the Smart Home in Chapter Three, but who knows where the limits o Amazon's vision or its new data territories lie?

As in the early days o historical colonialism's territorial expansion, no one can be sure yet how much value will be extracted rom such data: our argument does not depend, or example, on there being a thriving market or our Roomba data, or even on such data being shown on Amazon's balance sheet. It's the move to create data territories rom which value can potentially be extracted that matters. That is the colonial move. The result is to transorm something personal and intimate (the space o everyday lie) into a terrain or exploitation by external corporations, making everything we do potentially legible to business.2 Even our vacuum cleaners can leak images o us in intimate spaces to the world, as a reporter or MIT Tech Review discovered.21 And even our own ridge can now be hacked. Indeed a whole new area called 'IoT orensics' has emerged, involving experts in extracting data rom our devices.22

What will happen i we go on reorganising our homes and other spaces to become more reliant on devices, services and systems that transorm them into data territories? The result, over time, will be societies that are also data colonies: networks o myriad data territories, each governed by the special power that data territories bestow on their owners. Business thinkers already celebrate this trend, without unpacking its social implications. 'Sensor data is here to stay', writes leading Big Data expert Thomas Davenport. The Internet o Things, together with Cloud Computing, Big Data and AI creates, according to Thomas Siebel, a 'Digital Transormation' that will revolutionise human knowledge and business.23

Common to all this is a vision so bold that, at rst, it seems barely credible, except, that is, through a colonial lens. It's the idea that human lie itsel – the space o our lives – is now 'just there' or commercial use and exploitation in the orm o data, a territory where business can 'write contracts', as Varian put it. O course, this can't happen without major eorts o technological construction. The new colonial territories have to be explored (by writing the right code); and once identied, they must be expanded and exploited. In short, you have to build the 'data pipeline' as Varian once wrote, adopting without irony the language o one o historic colonialism's core assets.24 Where is this pipeline plugged? Right into the fow o our daily lie.

Data colonialism's process o relentless territorial expansion into daily lie oten comes disguised in the orm o apparent benets or even gits (recall the 'git' o electricity discussed in the Introduction). Not or nothing did Amazon decide early on to give away or

#### DATA TERRITORIES

ree its smart assistant, Alexa. But it's important to see through that oer and ocus on what, in a long-term historical perspective, is achieved by us accepting these gits. We need to look, in other words, at the overall 'deal' o today's new colonialism. Its terms become clearer once we explore the implications o these new data territories or social power more generally.

#### New Data Relations Mean New Power Relations

Every data territory, as part o its role o maximising data extraction, draws us into a new type o social relation that we call data relations: relations between people and corporations or governments ocussed entirely on maximising data extraction. Data relations work whether we know anything about data or not.

The rst eature o data relations, considered rom a social perspective, is asymmetry. Colonial relations historically were always asymmetrical: when colonial territories were acquired, the result was not ree-fowing trade between colonisers and colonised, but oneway extraction o gold, silver, sugar, tea, and so on. When today you join a digital platorm, the fow o data is equally asymmetrical: while the platorm can see everything you do, you the user can see very little o what the platorm does. Indeed, your data is analysed endlessly to 'personalise' your relationship to the platorm. Perhaps you don't care about this, because that's exactly what you want (you do want Tik Tok videos that are what someone like you would want to see). But considered as a power relation, this is undeniably asymmetrical. It has taken years o social protest and legal battles to learn even a little about what platorms like Facebook and Instagram themselves do behind the scenes. Without brave whistle-blowers like Instagram data engineer Frances Haugen and Facebook data scientist Sophie Zhang, we would know even less.

In eect, data territories operate through a orm o asymmetrical surveillance power. Such power seems less strange when we remember how such relations were a eature o colonial social lie rom common public spaces to slave plantations. Colonies were rarely democracies, and colonial powers ceded subjects only enough power to maintain legitimacy or their resource extraction.5

Data relations are not only asymmetrical; they involve (and this is their second colonial eature) new orms o power and control. Historical colonialism also extended the range o techniques whereby some humans control others: skin branding, the ngerprint and the security pass were all related to processes o colonial government. But because data – unlike land, minerals and crops – is itsel a orm o inormation, data colonies have some special possibilities.

Take Google once again. As Shoshana Zubo (author o The Age of Surveillance Capitalism) explained, the vast amounts o data Google amassed by tracking how we search enabled it to predict what users will do next with considerable accuracy. Such predictions have value to other businesses who also want to know what we will do next (such as advertisers). Money can be made rom this, or example, by selling advertising based on the assumed value o those predictions. But the absolute control o a platorm territory allows its designers to go even urther and, as Varian noted, experiment on us.

What might this mean in practice? Well, platorm owners can nudge people in a certain direction and test out i they indeed ollow a particular path; i not, then nudge them dierently and track the new outcome, until you gure out the most eective nudging methods or a certain category o users. Multiply this millions o times across a platorm and you get what legal scholar Karen Yeung called the 'hypernudge': a space o such pervasive infuence that it becomes hard to know whether our actions there are ree at all anymore.6

Far rom slowing down, this process is accelerating. Tik Tok would never put it this way, but the skill o its algorithm is to convince us that what it selects or us to view is exactly what we would

#### DATA TERRITORIES

have wanted to see, even without Tik Tok.2 Meanwhile, Facebook, under pressure since Apple in 2021 gave i Phone users the right to say no to being tracked by third-party advertisers, is now using its own AI to oer an experimental ad-testing service to those same advertisers, called Advantage+.28

We are not saying that platorm owners literally want to govern every aspect o our lives, as an authoritarian government might. But the territory o the digital platorm allows business to manage our actions in very signicant ways or their own ends. Businesses call this process optimisation: optimising behaviour on the platorm to ensure the best possible outcome or the platorm's business goals. The drive towards optimisation characterises not just platorms and apps, but all areas o the internet where data is continuously captured or commercial ends. One thing that platorms always want to optimise is the amount o data they extract. It is not enough or a platorm or app to occasionally monitor what we do; it must monitor us all the time. It is not enough or it to monitor just a ew dimensions o our actions: it must monitor all relevant dimensions.29 The possibility o 'N = all' in the jargon fows directly rom the colonial nature o the data territory: nothing can happen there except what has been tracked as data.

Continuing with our review o the colonial eatures o data territories, their third key eature is an absence o the limitations that would normally apply to how power is exercised in a physical territory.

A data territory has no limits in terms o size. Whereas physical land and its resources are intrinsically limited by the surace o the planet, the territories under any platorm owner's control can go on being created without limit, provided people can be persuaded to interact with them. In act, expansion need not involve an entrepreneur creating new apps, but merely building links between existing platorms and apps. There is no limit to how one platorm can link to other platorms or apps, or alternatively insert its own links into open online territory. Almost the whole internet is now completely 'platormised':30 that is, eectively governed as data territories. You may notice this when big platorms act like gatekeepers to websites or smaller platorms, and you are asked to sign up via your Facebook or Google ID, or in China via your We Chat ID, ensuring that the data rom you using that new platorm is combined with your Facebook, Google or We Chat data, to those corporations' beneft. Underlying this may be more complex arrangements: Big Tech companies oering various data-related services to the smaller websites and platorms, which encourage them to build the large platorms into their own design.31

While 'platormisation' may sound like an innocuous descriptive term, looked at rom the perspective o colonial history, it signals a transormation o open online space into urther data territories or control and extraction.

Indeed, some platorms' territory is now so large that they are almost impossible to escape. I you live in China, you can do almost everything – buying ood, getting loans, accessing government websites, checking i you can leave the house under the latest health restrictions – while staying on the We Chat platorm. All the while, your dependence on We Chat and on We Chat's absolute power over your We Chat ID grows. There's an impact on business too: other businesses simply must maintain their presence on We Chat, i they want to remain in touch with you. Indeed, the Chinese government itsel has special 'programs' on We Chat, interaces that enable users to deal directly with government via the app. Perhaps this is the sort o one-stop app that Elon Musk has in mind when he talks elliptically o the super-app Project X. Whatever Musk's plans, China's super-apps oer us a glimpse o an all-encompassing economic, social and administrative power that even colonial power rarely attained, with the possible exception o Britain's East India Company.

Another way in which data territories are unconstrained is depth. There is no limit to the 'depth' at which data can be extracted:

#### DATA TERRITORIES

as long as people are willing to go on interacting with the platorm, the only limit is the code that platorm designers write and the number o categories o data collection it installs.

This has huge implications or daily lie. One is work, where, when tasks are translated into platorm ormats, managers are able to watch and track literally every level and aspect o the duties they assign to workers, including the time to the second it takes to per orm those tasks. Another is the human body. We may have become used to the tracking o everything we do online, but we are much less used to the tracking o the human body. Facial recognition is controversial almost everywhere, but this is just the start o body tracking. A new industry is emerging around the tracking and analysis o how people talk, whether online or via their phones. Call Miner is a specialist company in the area o voice processing and its head has spoken o his vision 'to empower organizations to extract meaningul and actionable intelligence rom their customers' conversations'.32

Data territories and their data outputs are also not limited in terms o their transerability. Whatever data is gathered via the data territory, its benefts can be transerred, shared or sold to others without restriction. Just as I can use inormation about today's weather at the very same time that you or the next person are using exactly the same inormation, so too can marketers with the dataset that defnes you as a shopper. This is not to say that businesses don't sometimes create artifcial scarcity around 'their' data. But rom the perspective o platorm users, it means that unless rules are written to stop this, they basically write a blank cheque when they give up data to any one platorm. They have no control over where in uture that data might move.

These eatures o data territories, while they might seem merely technical, amount to a redesign o social relations, or at least those social relations we perorm inside data territories (already an awul lot). In those territories, we enter – seemingly voluntarily – into a series o asymmetric power relations, deeper and more pervasive than we knew beore platorms were invented, through which businesses can optimise their goals without limitation. At the core o these new social relations is a new colonial reality: the capture o social territory on a vast scale or the control and benet o the ew.

Yet, as we'll see urther in Chapter Three, this remarkable trans ormation o social lie is presented to us – the users – as just businesses providing what we always wanted: more convenience, more social connection, and all in the interests o science, or at least 'knowing' us better. However, the engineers know such processes are anything but natural, even i they rarely own up to this. Which is why, when Andrew Bosworth, a leading Facebook engineer and now Meta's chie technology ofcer, admitted that 'the natural state o the world is not connected', he got a lot o attention in the tech industry.33 People o course have always socialised and organised things together. But doing so by connecting to Facebook, and in ways that suit Facebook's business model, ar rom being natural, ts perectly into a larger process o territorial capture.

#### Data, AI and the Environment

For data to be valuable, it needs to be processed, which consumes energy.

Our platorm use and networking unctioning relies on data processing by huge banks o computers (so-called 'data centres') which use electricity and generate heat, and so require cooling, as well as large amounts o land. Those processing costs get even higher or the most intensive data processing, such as large-scale AI. Aware o this, a number o Big Tech companies have green ambitions, with Apple aiming to be carbon neutral and Microsot carbon negative by 2030. But the environmental costs o our digital lie are still generally downplayed in public discussions.34

Consider data centres. They are distributed across both the Global North and the Global South. Most o us don't think about

#### DATA TERRITORIES

them, but their impact on the physical environment is starting to make the news. In west London, a mixed residential area where until a ew years ago one o us lived, the building o much-needed new homes will be constrained at least until 2035 by the lack o electricity supply. Why? Because the area's vast expansion o data centres means there will not be 'sucient capacity or a new [electricity] connection'. Globally, it is estimated that data centres will use between 3 per cent and 13 per cent o all electricity by the year 2030, compared to the 1 per cent they used in 2010.35

It gets worse. High electricity use in data servers causes overheating, which needs to be cooled down by using water – resh water, it turns out. Just a month ater that frst announcement, Londoners learned that Thames Water, the city's water supplier, was concerned that its water supplies were getting dangerously depleted. One reason: data centres. A UK parliamentary investigation on the long-term implications o data centres on scarce water resources has been demanded.36 And London is only one o 15 data centre hotspots across the world, not even the astest-growing (Mumbai, Shanghai and Northern Virginia are outpacing it).37

The vast power usage required to 'mine' digital currencies is another area o headlong development with ar too little consideration o environmental costs. 'Mining' here does not mean the physical excavation o resources in the ground, as in historic colonialism, but instead the use o vast computing power to validate the authenticity o each newly minted currency unit: or example, one Bitcoin. Bitcoin is just one cryptocurrency, currently in fnancial decline. But its 'mining' in 2020 used up more electricity than that consumed by Austria or Portugal, two medium-sized wealthy nations.38 From the point o view o environmental costs, then, Bitcoin might as well be historical colonial loot.

Yet all o these consequences o computing and data or the natural environment are obscured by the abstract or metaphorical language used to describe them. For example, the 'cloud' on which our data is stored is not a reserve o water that can help sustain earthly resources, but a vast network o data centres that likely depletes them.39 And this is only the data processing.

There are also the environmental costs o the physical devices on which we interact with data territories and data territories interact with us. All computing devices depend on chips which are generally made o silicon, phosphorus and many other minerals. Raw materials or these devices generally come rom Arica, Asia and Latin America, with 36 per cent o the Earth's tin and 15 per cent o its silver going into electronics manuacturing.40 Our devices also depend or their unctioning on rare metals (such as cobalt and lithium) that are oten mined in difcult and violent conditions in Arica and Latin America.41

Some o those raw materials require vast amounts o other natural resources to mine them. For example, lithium (used also in the production o batteries) is extracted, under one method, by pumping vast amounts o water to the surace in salt lakes, which then evaporates. In Chile's Atacama Desert this has caused water shortages or the Lickan Antay First Nation people who have lived or centuries on the land where mining goes on.42

At the other end o the process, there is the environmental waste into which our electronic devices turn when, all too requently, they become obsolescent: each person on the planet is estimated to have generated 7.3 kilograms o electronic waste in 2019, according to the Global E-Waste Monitor.43 Even though most o this waste is generated by the Global North, most o it ends up being disposed o in the Global South, with detrimental impacts to human health, including those who have to process it.

There is much more to say about the environmental costs o data territories, the colonial struggle or control over the resources that make our digital communications possible, and about the hidden environmental strategies o Big Tech companies to secure

#### DATA TERRITORIES

their power (or example by multiplying satellites that crowd Earth's thermosphere, or building cables that criss-cross our oceans).44 But we hope the bigger picture is becoming clear. I the expansion o data territories represents a takeover o social lie, the physical inrastructure that makes this takeover possible itself imposes major costs on the natural environment, especially in the Global South. This repeats a colonial history o the devastation o 'cheap nature', concentrated particularly in what Naomi Klein calls 'zones o extraction'.45

#### There's a Data Grab Happening (Very) Near You

Data territories can be installed in any area o lie and exploit any number o inormational dimensions.

One data territory which you may be amiliar with is the one into which advertisers try to entice you when they track wherever you go, pulling together all the resulting data so that at any moment they can direct a relevant ad or other inormation to pop up on your screen. Such personalised marketing was an early sign o the tracking possibilities that Philip Agre noted three decades ago, but now it is acing some challenges. Not everyone in marketing thinks it has proven efective. Do you click on every ad that pops up on your screen, or even 5 per cent o them? In addition, some powerul business orces, like Apple (as already mentioned) and Google, have started outlawing the cookie-based tracking on which advertisers have relied. Sounds altruistic and privacyconscious, although maybe Apple's and Google's goal is to boost the value o their own advertising and advertising-related data. It seems hardly a coincidence that a year ater it orced advertisers to get explicit consent rom i Phone users or tracking them, Apple announced a doubling o its own digital advertising workorce.46 Some people, encouraged by this blocking o the tracking without consent that underlay Facebook's long-time business model, have seen in these developments the end o surveillance capitalism.47 But data colonialism operates in many more spaces than targeted advertising.

Let's take personal fnance, or example. Quite apart rom the data scares involving online credit agencies, there has been a massive growth o algorithmically operated personal fnance platorms and apps. These apps are certainly more convenient than waiting endlessly or a ace-to-ace appointment with a bank manager; you can do it all rom your phone. But they depend not just on you supplying some initial data, but on you agreeing to the app accessing all sorts o data streams rom you and third parties continuously, beore and ater the loan, to generate your constantly updated credit rating.48

Advertising and personal fnance are just the tip o the data iceberg. Three other areas, basic to our quality o lie, where you might not expect data extraction to be rie, but where in act it is well under way, are agriculture, education and health.

#### All the Data That's Fit to Eat

Today, in many parts o the world, the tractor you see in a feld is likely not only to have a computer built into it, but to be connected to systems or online data analysis in the cloud.

There is a large-scale drive towards 'precision agriculture', where US equipment maker John Deere and US Ag Tech corporation Monsanto (now part o the German Bayer Group) are big players. Although armers are still ree to drive and walk around their felds, their work may be largely prescribed by the sotware management programs that come with their equipment. For this purpose, genetically modifed seeds count as equipment. To quote the FAQ o Monsanto's Technology Stewardship Agreement, 'any grower who wishes to purchase and plant seed that contains Monsanto Technology must have a valid TSA [Term o Service Agreement]'.49

#### DATA TERRITORIES

This vision o precision agriculture is transorming animal arming too. China has been trying to expand its pig production or some years to meet huge global demand. Industrial-scale arming was happening without AI, o course, but the Chinese Big Tech giant Alibaba's Cloud business saw an opportunity. It oered its proprietary ET Agricultural Brain platorm to monitor the progress o millions o pigs in huge industrial arms. First, however, it was necessary to train the algorithm to categorise pigs' behaviour and state o health correctly; or that, there are so-called 'digital towns' where migrant rural workers sit at computers all day perorming such labour.50 This is just one o many areas o ood production in which Alibaba is involved.51

A new vision o ood production is emerging that converges around data analytics, which Monsanto in 2013 called 'agriculture's next major growth rontier'.52Farmers contribute considerable labour, uploading and veriying images o their soil and weeds in order to train the algorithm. In many cases this is a one-way ow o inormation; the data collected by a John Deere tractor, or instance, is not accessible by the armer who drives it.53 Indeed, the armer is locked in to John Deere's repair services, with no independent right to repair: a orm o territorial capture that has recently been challenged by legislation in the US state o Colorado, which parallels challenges to Apple's decades o blocking owners' independent right to repair their devices.54 In the process, a huge amount o data and power accumulates with the large corporations, while armers become ever more dependent on management sotware such as Bayer/Monsanto's Field View platorm (created originally by The Climate Corporation). As you'd expect, such services are targeted mainly at the largest arms, but they are resetting the whole ood production industry. And there's another big player that benefts: the agricultural insurance business which can now use satellite imagery along with the new data platorms to monitor risks across large arms on an ongoing 'personalised' basis.55 Just as Hal Varian predicted or the humble car insurance contract.

Farmers' relationship to their land, which they have managed or generations, is being transormed as a result o AI-driven plat orms. This extends to the smallest scale: grass. The Grassometer, a device produced by ormer Irish company Monord, measures grass growth but a lot else besides. Via GPS and sensors, it maps the ground and measures grass height to bring 'precision arming to the grassland armer'. Its ounder proclaimed that 'data is the new soil: it's as important or the arm o the uture as the tractor is today'.56

Although these developments have still much urther to go, we can already see the territorial push to transorm the old colonial territory o land into a new asset where data control is o primary importance. The physical territory o the arm and feld is becoming a hybrid data territory.

#### The Classroom as Data Territory

Some o the largest players in Educational Technology (Ed Tech) are amiliar names. Google Apps or Education are relied upon in many classrooms around the world, while Microsot is also a huge player. Pearson, the world's leading education provider (who ormed a major partnership with IBM in 2016), has a vision o a 'digital ocean', a boundless space where educational data is continuously generated rom the Ed Tech-enabled classroom, just as social data is continuously generated rom people's use o social media plat orms.57 The global Ed Tech market was valued at over US \$250 billion in 2021, with predictions it will at least double by 2027.58

A core part o that value is data, data that can work well with the new large-scale AI services. As Pearson chie executive Andy Bird put it when asked i ChatGPT was a threat to its business, 'we are the owners o some very pure, rich data sets: when you start to input them into generative AI models, you get better outputs.' 59 Far

#### DATA TERRITORIES

rom undermining a large Ed Tech player like Pearson, ChatGPT and generative AI enables it to extract even more value rom its private data hoards.

Little o this is currently being regulated by governments. In act, the education data sector is growing with the encouragement o governments in many countries. Brazil and Nigeria are examples o growing national Ed Tech sectors that are emerging with government support.60 The Covid-19 pandemic gave a huge push to this. In South Korea, or example, all teaching went online at the start o the pandemic, with Korea's Educational Broadcasting System (EBS) playing a key role, but EBS in turn had deals with third-party commercial data extractors like Oracle's Blue Kai.61

Indeed, the whole climate o Ed Tech's expansion has been set by multilateral organisations (that is, transnational organisations unded by multiple governments) that we might have hoped to be more critical o business values. However, rom the World Bank and UN down, multilateral organisations seem to have allen captive to the cheerleaders o the world's unolding data grab.

Audrey Azoulay, the director-general o UNESCO, claimed in 2019 that 'education will be prooundly transormed by AI'. A UNESCO report rom the same year talked o 'preparing learners to thrive in an AI-saturated uture'. This ts with a broader business rhetoric that sees AI as automatically positive. The EU's High-Level Expert Group on AI stated that 'AI is . . . a promising means to increase human ourishing, thereby enhancing individual and societal well-being and the common good, as well as bringing progress and innovation'.62 But that rather depends on how AI is implemented, doesn't it? I AI were really under the control o those impacted by it, that would be one thing, but under the rules o the data colony, the impacts are entirely diferent.

Public-private data partnerships are becoming the deault way orward or education, as Big Tech seeks to make deals with governments, especially those that don't have much money to spend in education. Microsot's Arika is one such public-private partnership. Google Cloud is also ofering higher-education support in eight Arican countries, while Facebook has recently developed an education app called Sabee, again targeted at Arica.

The lack o barriers to commercial values in the education space was celebrated in the 2021 report by Microsot's Education division called Understanding the New Learning Landscape:

Retail stores using AI can determine within 2h[ours] the impact on purchasing behaviors in response to moving the shel location o a product. Education systems should be able to see the impact on student well-being and learning o changes in teaching strategies, tools and programs just as quickly.63

This sounds like Pearson's idea o the digital ocean. But the retail comparison raises a key question: is what needs attention in education as easily measurable as the rise in purchases when snacks are moved rom one aisle to another? And what i it isn't? What happens when education's participants – teachers, parents, children – are regularly exposed to the asymmetrical power relations o the data colony?

An important recent report by the UK's Digital Futures Commission and 5Rights Foundation raised the alarm. It examined the data policies o two prominent Ed Tech platorms, Google Education and Class Dojo, intensively used in UK schools (with total UK downloads in 2021 in excess o two million).

Translating the report's diplomatic language only a little, our problems emerged clearly. Opacity: it's very hard to know what these platorms are doing with the data they collected. Expansionism: the platorms constantly blur the boundaries between school and home data, between core and 'additional' services. Unaccountability: the platorms have huge power, but their accountability to

#### DATA TERRITORIES

schools, parents and children is as yet unclear. And uncertain compliance: because they are so opaque, we don't know whether those platforms are complying with existing legal requirements on data (there have already been legal challenges concerning this, for example in Holland).64 Nowhere in a school that operates Google Classroom or Class Dojo does there seem to be much scope for individual teachers to have a say in all this. How could they, when the contracts with Google Classroom or Class Dojo are negotiated at school level or even higher up the decision-chain? Parents have even less of a say, unless they are willing to withdraw their child from the school. And in case you didn't notice, the voice of the child, whose data is being harvested and archived, is completely absent here.65

Around the same time as the Digital Futures Commission investigation, the international organisation Human Rights Watch was surveying 163 Ed Tech platforms across nine countries. They found that government-backed Ed Tech during the pandemic involved multiple risks of data being transferred without consent to third parties, including in major markets like Brazil.66

Meanwhile, educational research in the digital classroom is proceeding apace. One area is eye-tracking. The hope is to build, as researchers put it, 'scalable attention-aware systems'.67 Such systems would pay particular attention to a specic type of eye-movement that is evidence of 'mind-wandering'; a practice researchers say is worrying because it 'involves internal thoughts'.

Perhaps not coincidentally, behavioural management to control children's eye movements is catching on as a teaching practice. Some UK teachers, with support from the UK government, are championing an approach called SLANT. The words for which this acronym stands say it all: Sit up, Listen, Ask & answer, No talking, Track speaker.68
