No one would pretend that, before the growth of Ed Tech, the classroom was an ideal space. It had many problems, particularly in low-income areas. But what we are saying is that, once you convert a classroom into a data territory, many older constraints on power disappear. Data territories, whether or teaching or any other purpose, are spaces o absolute rule that operate on scales and at depths unknown to the ace-to-ace teacher. This may be attractive to the rulers o large societies that need to plug huge skills gaps. Empires are being built in the Ed Tech sector based on aggressive marketing and continuous data extraction, like the Indian teaching app Byju's, which has raised more than US \$22 billion in less than a decade, including rom Silicon Valley (the Zuckerberg/Chan Foundation) and China (Tencent).69 But Byju's is just one example o a vast global wave o corporate data extraction rom schools and other educational institutions.70

Privacy and educational advocates are deeply concerned about a world where 'children's lives are lled with surveillance' to suit corporations who think that 'the more data the better'.71 We must think about the long-term consequences or education itsel: will it be organised increasingly around skills that are easy or Ed Tech to measure and monetise?72

#### My Health Data and Me

As we saw in Michael's story, there is no more intimate data or each o us than our health data. Used improperly or stored incompetently, and it could make the diference between lie and death, or at least cause great inconvenience. That's why it is news when health data gets moved around without consent, as when Deep Mind, an Alphabet subsidiary, collaborated with the Royal Free London NHS Foundation Trust and got access to large patient datasets without those patients being told.73 It is even worse when health data – let alone mental-health data – is shared with social media companies, even i unwittingly, as happened with mental health startup Cerebral in March 2023.74

It's good thereore that many countries have laws that give special protection to health data, or example the European Union's

#### DATA TERRITORIES

General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR). But there is some health data that is so sensitive – or example, an individual's genome – that philosophers argue that special rules are needed or how it is stored and accessed.75 This is the controversial territory into which US gene-tracing apps like 23and Me have stumbled with what many regard as inadequate privacy protections.76 It is a risk also in the area o mental-health data, a growing sector which has raised more than US \$8 billion since 2020.77

The controversy over individual genetic data is just one small part o the landscape o data exploitation related to health. Societies and communities do need health data to protect us against disease. As the Covid-19 pandemic reminded us, disease spreads without regard or borders, and data about where and how it is spreading is essential to help us fnd cures or design treatments. Indeed, given the maniest inequalities o healthcare, a persuasive case can be made or more intensive gathering o national and international health data in anonymised orm.78

But everything depends on how health data is gathered, stored and put to use – and by whom. Given that health data is individually sensitive, ew would disagree with the principle o protecting its anonymity, outside times when it is being directly used to treat a patient. But, as in many other areas, experts worry that health data can be de-anonymised when it is combined with other data sources, and that's beore we even consider whether the personal devices that gather this data are secure against hacking.79

There is also the question o the 'what'. Not only does the worry about anonymity intensiy when we move rom health data to the much larger and completely unregulated area o healthrelated data, but it is hard to know what health-related data is. Probably anything a commercial actor fnds relevant to someone's health. But relevant or what purposes? An insurer providing a health policy would certainly like to know what we eat, how much we exercise and what we drink. Perhaps data relating to our mental health too, and any conditions that might aect it (marital unhappiness, or example), since mental health issues generally end up aecting physical health in the long run. And visions o 'precision medicine' encompass even individual genetic data.0 That really is a lot o data! According to one estimate, the health Internet o Things market will be worth at least US \$300 billion by 2025.1

In the past decade, a huge industry has emerged to exploit both our health-related and health data, with only the second being legally regulated. Countless app developers and device suppliers are competing to extract health-related data via our smart devices (Fitbit and Apple Watch being the best-known examples). This expansion is not limited to the Global North; India is now the world's biggest market or smart watches.2 Big Tech companies are moving into the health sector, and capitalising on their Big Data, AI and cloud-computing resources: Amazon Health, Google Health, Microsot Cloud or Healthcare. Amazon's proposed purchase o US primary care provider One Medical or US \$3.7 billion positions it well as a potentially powerul player in the 'value-based care' market. This market rewards companies or patients' wellness, not just treatments delivered. But how will that be measured except by continuous data extraction?

A corporate move in November 2019 brought these two dierent worlds together and attracted a lot o attention: Google's purchase o Fitbit or US \$2.1 billion. Why so much money? The assumption in the fnancial and tech press was that it was based on the value to Google o Fitbit's data and, even more important, Fitbit's potential as an expanding portal or health-data collection. As the Wall Street Journal reported, 'Fitbit . . . cuts out the middleman': a doctor or hospital are no longer needed or data to be contributed directly via Fitbit to a health-data corporation. When reporting the buyout, the newspaper could not resist a colonial metaphor: 'health services remain an open rontier.'3

#### DATA TERRITORIES

As with education, the issues are larger than what happens to this or that piece o data. They concern the shiting balance o power in a health sector increasingly organised around the power that corporate access to ever larger health-data territories gives. This is the prize at which Big Tech players like Amazon, Apple and Google are aiming.84

The power play is however complicated by the role played by third-party users, such as insurers and employers. As Michael (in our opening story) realised, insurers are very interested in healthrelated data. And the employers who subsidise their workers' health insurance are themselves interested in the interest insurers take, because they need to keep down the cost o the policies they subsidise. So employers want their employees to wear the tracking devices that enable insurers to get a continuous stream o health data (in the way, or example, that UK insurer Vitality Lie gets data directly rom Apple Watch). These major commercial pressures are in tension with the important principles o protecting health data's anonymity and limiting the ow o data to minimise risks o de-anonymisation. The more companies interested in making prot rom data enter the health industries, the greater the tension.

Meanwhile, the risks rom losing protection o one's health data are very unequally distributed. Those who are poor and vulnerable will not be able to pay or legal action to secure protection o their data. On a global scale, countries in the Global South will have huge diculties in securing protection against the ree movement o health data when negotiating with the Big Tech corporations rom the Global North. Big Tech's investments in health cover not just IT inrastructure, but direct healthcare, where they can ofer themselves as solutions on the historically uneven distribution o health resources. Take the Chinese Big Tech platorm Tencent, which is becoming a huge player in the health sector under the banner o 'precision medicine'. Tencent is expanding in AI-driven diagnosis and in direct healthcare provision via its Smart Hospital programme, with its social media platorm We Chat being a key link.85 Meanwhile, Chinese corporation BGI has emerged during the pandemic as a leader in Covid-19 testing and 'personalised medicine'; and in August 2022, Byte Dance, Tik Tok's parent company, bought a major Chinese hospital company or US \$1.5 billion.86

We need a new principle to strike a balance between individual needs or data protection and the social imperative to share some health data or the common good. A recent Commission on Governing Health Futures sponsored by the Financial Times and the Lancet ramed this in terms o 'data solidarity'. They defne it as 'simultaneously protecting individual rights, promoting the public good potential o such data, and building a culture o data justice and equity'.87 We doubt whether this principle will be taken up by the international NGOs or national governments who set policy or the health-data sector. Fortunately, there are innovative social organisations like Hippo AI in Germany that are developing a di erent model or managing health data that builds on this principle o solidarity.88 Without it, there is little to stop Big Tech players rom urther exploiting this health-data 'rontier'.

#### Data Territories and the Transformation of Work

The data territories o Ag Tech, Ed Tech and Health Tech may still seem distant rom many people's lives, although they are a very pressing reality or those at the receiving end o these innovations. But there's one dimension o society where the innovations o data colonialism are already having a direct impact on people right across society. This is work and, in particular, the work o lower-status workers.

Work is being reconfgured by exactly the same mechanism we have argued is at the heart o data colonialism: the creation o new territories rom which data can be continuously extracted and

#### DATA TERRITORIES

exploited. Low-skill work in particular is being transormed by digital platorms and interaces o various sorts into orms o data territory under the absolute control o managers. As a result, whole ways o working are under threat.

There is nothing remotely surprising about capitalist or colonial employers grabbing all the inormation they can about their workers. As Karl Marx noted, surveillance is one o the basic tools o capitalist management,89 while the unremitting surveillance and counting o bodies was a key eature o the pre-capitalist slave plantation and other sites o colonial exploitation.90 So 'the quantifed worker' (in legal scholar Ieoma Ajunwa's phrase) is not new.91 But the question is: how much worse are things getting through the new orces o data colonialism, which are making ocussed and continuous data surveillance so much easier? The answer is: much worse.

In the most simple case, merely using your work computer can expose you to continuous surveillance by your employer, whether they are a thousand miles away or in the same building. So-called 'bossware' (platorms like Teramind, Veriato Vision and Clever-Control) locks workers into data territories rom which there is no escape. It can track your every keystroke, look or certain keywords (which the employer may want to see being used), check in on you via screenshots o your desktop or video/audio recordings taken at intervals, or monitor the programs you are using to make sure you are not wasting time. This kind o system can also monitor work emails and your chat messages. The Covid-19 pandemic intensifed the use o such techniques. According to one survey, 60 per cent o US employers were using bossware to monitor workers in 2021 (the fgure was much higher in some industries like marketing and IT); another survey report expects 70 per cent o large US employers to be doing so by 2025.92

There are however other more subtle orms o computer-based surveillance that should not be orgotten. Take precarious low-paid workers, such as cleaners or domestic workers who are generally women. I their next job is just an SMS text or Whats App message away, having a phone connected to signal and Wi-Fi can well be the diference between getting work or not working at all (regularly missing that connection will be noticed by the employing platorm and could result in getting kicked of).93

A great deal o low-paid work today is perormed in data territories. The enhanced data-driven surveillance that results rom this matters all the more, because o the proound reorganisation o work over the past our decades in many countries that goes ar beyond the orces o data colonialism. Management experts now talk o the 'ssured workplace', as supply chains have lengthened and large corporations looked to reduce their direct employer responsibilities. As we would expect, the legacy o earlier colonialism shapes how this works in practice, with much o the lower-paid work being passed on to companies in the Global South.94 More and more people now work urther down the supply chain in precarious circumstances, where guaranteeing a steady supply o work means that a small company needs to comply with very specic standards or quality, timing and the like. Who imposes those standards? You guessed it: the large company at the top o the supply chain, such as Apple, Walmart or Tesco. And how are those standards monitored? Through the ow o data up and down the supply chain. We enter here the area o logistics, an area oten ignored in discussions about Big Tech surveillance that ocus mainly on social media.

The delivery warehouses in the logistics industry are a good example. We all want our Amazon package to arrive on time, but how oten do we think about the individual human beings who are continuously tracked when we click on the icon 'track your package'? Here the territories or data extraction are generated not by interactive platorms that we play with in our leisure time, but by corporate IT systems. The systems that monitor how things and

#### DATA TERRITORIES

workers move around warehouses can be extraordinarily intrusive. The process o 'voice picking' (short or 'voice-directed order picking') involves using precise GPS readings o bodies and objects to monitor workers' movements and coordination rom moment to moment. Its implications were noted more than a decade ago by the UK's GMB Union as a risk to workers' health, because o the unlimited intensity and depth o its surveillance, which pushes workers in a way that results in increased accidents and injuries.95 On-the-body data collection leaves absolutely no space or negotiation, but even the seemingly less intrusive task o scanning itsel or the workow system is used without mercy: as Garfeld Hylton, who works at Amazon's Coventry, UK, warehouse said, 'It's called "scanner adherence" – you have to be scanning every minute to show a constant rapid scan'. Otherwise, he said, a laptop-wielding manager comes to berate you.96 But employees have no redress i the surveillance systems that monitor them get things wrong. Labour unions, among others, are increasingly concerned by this.97

On-the-body data collection is one thing, but the tracker need not be fxed to the body itsel; it is enough or it to be fxed to the machine or vehicle that the worker is responsible or controlling. Truck-driving has been completely transormed by the insertion o devices into cabs which record how the lorry is driven, and report back to the employer. Electronic Logging Devices (ELDs) are now virtually obligatory in all US trucks. The change in power relations achieved by simply inserting such a device in a truck cab is pro ound, transorming the truck driver, as the US sociologist Karen Levy notes, rom the sole story-teller o his time on the road to being just one inormation source or corporate databases among others.98 The impact on drivers' sense o sel-worth was proound. As one driver that responded to a regulatory consultation put it, 'I'm not going to work under conditions where I'm treated like a child, a child who doesn't have enough [sense] to know when to go to bed and when to get up.'99

Trucking rms, Levy reports, were also relying on data sources ar rom the truck cab; they were monitoring their drivers' social media use. Social media, ater all, provide evidence to an employer o potential employability, or an employee's continuing tness.100 In the process, a key boundary between the worker's public and private lie gets overridden. But the new rules o work-based data territory have potentially an even more dramatic impact: they can transorm whole work sectors and even create new types o work that were not possible beore, all through the fow o data.

#### The Gig Economy as Data Territory

We come here to the so-called 'gig economy': online task platorms such as Amazon Turk, Remo Tasks and Upwork, driver platorms such as Uber and Didi (China), and ood delivery platorms such as Deliveroo (UK) and Meituan (China). The leading Big Tech companies even have their own task platorms (Google's Raterhub and Microsot's UHRS or Universal Human Relevance System). A more descriptive term or such tasks would be 'on-demand' work. The percentage o people getting work this way remains relatively low (under 10 per cent), at least in rich economies such as the USA and UK (although according to other estimates, reelancers will soon account or about hal o the US workorce).101 Outside the West, reliable gures are harder to come by. But the percentage is growing ast. In the on-demand work economy, platorms are the arena where work and tasks get distributed and monitored. As a result, they literally transorm whole business sectors or create new ones. As leading Indian digital rights campaigners Anita Gurumurthy and Nandini Chami put it, platorms don't just dominate the market, they 'become the market'.102

The owners o gig apps requently claim their platorms benet society. So, or example, transportation and ride-sharing platorms claim they improve driver wages and reduce the environmental impacts o driving, even though evidence may point to the

#### DATA TERRITORIES

opposite.103 Meanwhile, the real advantages o gig platorms for managers derive rom the dismantling o traditional employment patterns in the fssured workplace. Not only is the role o large-scale employers increasingly ragmented across long supply chains, but all sorts o employers (rom the largest Big Tech platorm to small companies lower down the supply chain) are increasingly outsourcing work they cannot do in-house. They can do this by recruiting on-demand workers across the world on a task-by-task basis who they reach via platorms such as Mturk, Cloudactory, Crowd Flower and Lead Genius. These platorms give businesses access to large pools o workers, oten in the Global South (or example, Colombia, India, the Philippines), who must perorm precise tasks in fxed times and to fxed standards. This is where Uber drivers' photo-IDs get verifed when their appearance changes and triggers a query rom the system; and this is where small items o fnancial data get checked so that businesses can process it. On-demand platorms cover a wide spectrum o blue-collar and white-collar work.104

The basic eatures o such work ollow rom the properties o data territories explained earlier. Managers have always had considerable, sometimes even tyrannical, power over workspaces, but on-demand work platorms are designed to make sure that everything that goes on there is captured. The platorm owner can change the rules rom moment to moment as they please, including by denying a worker access to the platorm, or arbitrarily paying them less.105

Ending someone's ability to work on a platorm is now as simple as changing a ew letters and numbers in a database. Literally, because workers or on-demand platorms aren't known by their names and aces, only by strings o letters and numbers.106 And potentially legally, because platorms like Uber or a long time have been denying that they actually employ anyone, rather than just managing a database. Fortunately, law courts in various countries have begun to challenge this. Uber's denial o their employer status has now, in countries like the UK, been overturned; things are moving in that direction in the US too.107 But this doesn't alter the pattern of power relations across data territories that the authors of a study on shopping apps call 'algorithmic despotism'.108

Such asymmetrical power relations now govern work for hundreds of millions of workers across the world. Systems of rule that are almost completely opaque aord very little, if any, dignity to their invisible workers. When gig platforms can lower the wages of workers who accept jobs quickly (because it is taken as evidence that they are more desperate), and raise the wages of workers who take longer to accept jobs (because they are not as desperate, and need enticement), we come to see what the system is designed to do: maximise prot at all cost, even by punishing the most vulnerable.109 The result is to deepen the alienation of lower-status workers in society.

We are becoming ever more aware of the 'algorithmic cruelty' of work shaped by distant platforms that operate far from the human realities of workers' lives, and possibly with no human involvement at all.110 So far it is mainly academic work and journalism from China and elsewhere that has reected on what this is doing to the living conditions of workers. But British director Ken Loach's lm Sorry We Missed You (2019) stands out as a rare exception to this near silence. It tells the story of Ricky, a driver, who is encouraged to give up a secure position with a delivery company and provide his services to a delivery platform which gives him no security and sets no limits to the disruptions that its data-driven tasks will impose on his family and mental health. The result is a nervous breakdown: an individual's whole way of life gets destroyed by the violence of automated exploitation.

Nor is dependence on gig economy work evenly distributed in either the Global North or the Global South: population groups who suered from historical colonialism (including racial/ethnic minorities and immigrants) tend to make up higher percentages of 'ghost workers' (as gig workers are sometimes known) than other

#### DATA TERRITORIES

populations, at least in the US.111 The detailed reality o the gig economy is thereore, to a signicant degree, colonial. But so too is its overall organisation. Local ways o managing resources (oten o course exploitative, but at least locally negotiable) are being replaced by large-scale global systems o management that have a new social reality at their core, the data territory under the absolute control o its creator.

#### Global Inequality, Redux

One thing is very clear: the inequalities o the old colonial order live on. We see it in the geographic location o the world's largest plat orms and cloud service companies, or in the ownership o the undersea cables that transmit the signals we experience as the internet.112 Or in the political relations that enable Big Tech companies based in the Global North to extract avours rom the governments o the Global South. Or in the structure o global trade agreements that today impose the Global North's terms or data extraction on the rest o the world.113 Yes, China is a newcomer to the colonial landscape, but, as arguably the world's oldest empire, it is behaving in no less a colonial way than the West in the era o Big Data.

As a result, the worst impacts o data colonialism are likely to be elt most painully in the Global South and among those in the Global North whose orebears were on the losing side o historical colonialism. Tracing the intersections between the eects o historical colonialism and data colonialism will sometimes be hard.

It is as i two rivers, data colonialism and historic colonialism – one newly ormed and the other very old – were running side by side. For now, we need to track both river fows and see how over time they will merge. But it is the new river's input to which we need to pay the most urgent attention, not because in the end it will matter more (who can say?), but because right now it is intensiying the fow o colonial history.

'I don't get it. The manager who interviewed me seemed to really like me. Can't he override the algorithm?' Angela asked Phil.

'I'm not even supposed to be discussing this with you, Angela. Our company doesn't like us talking about these details. But this company that interviewed you probably uses a similar system to the one we use, Hire Plus AI.'

## 'So you are telling me the AI didn't like me?'

'It's like this,' said Phil. 'The Hire Plus AI system scans your application materials, and also looks at your interview video, analysing your acial movements, word choice, etc. It's supposed to be very air. It's better than being judged by a biased human being, trust me!'1

'But I have excellent qualications, reerences . . . and I tried to be very positive during the interview,' Angela interjected.
