Unions are a big worry or companies in the US right now, as you know,' Phil inormed her.

'It just seems like it should be a human being making the nal decision,' Angela said, trying not to sound too upset.

'I don't necessarily disagree, but that's just the way the world is right now, and we have to learn to adapt to it, Angie. Besides, think o how many more people the AI system allows us to interview that we couldn't interview beore. That's a good thing, isn't it?'

'I suppose so,' said Angela dejectedly.

'Well, my advice,' said Phil, 'is that next time you take those interview surveys, try to answer in a manner that you think the AI will approve o, and not necessarily based on what you really eel. Anyway, I have to run now, Angie, but I want to wish you good luck. Keep us posted, OK? Someone will be ortunate to hire you!' And with that, Phil exited the call, leaving Angela to stare at a blank screen.

#### The Emperor's New 'Civilising' Clothes

Angela and Phil's story provides another example o how, or many people, the data grab is leading to a drastic worsening o the power relations that govern their lives. Yet, the narratives we are ofered to explain or justiy what's going on with data seem rather more com orting, hopeul even. Phil talks about the convenience o AI-based hiring, the ability to connect to so many more interviewees than could ever have been interviewed in person, and AI's superior judgement compared with humans. In this chapter, we'll unpack all those civilising narratives in more detail.

There is an overall similarity between the old and new civilising narratives that becomes clear only when we look through the lens o colonial history. In colonial history, the landgrab was justifed by a whole series o narratives that were oten very dierent rom the realities on the ground. First, the evangelical goal o spreading Christianity, which or some was in direct tension with the brutal violence o the colonisers. Second, the idea o Europe's racial superiority and specifcally its superior knowledge and science. Third, the idea o the colonisers' superior economic capacities and rationality (championed particularly by the British, who didn't take seriously the idea that the Pope had global authority to seize land in order to spread Christianity). Then there were elaborate mixtures o all three represented by narratives such as the West's 'maniest destiny', the 'white man's burden', and generally the idea o Europe's 'civilising mission' to transorm the world through military conquest and socio-economic and cultural change. These narratives evolved successively over time to justiy what was, at frst, a bewilderingly new and large transormation o global power relations through land seizure. There were, to be air, some early disputes among the colonisers about the justifcations or their new-ound wealth. But these were not serious challenges to colonialism, and they merely stimulated more sophisticated narratives o justifcation.2

Today's data grab is in its early years, and no doubt the narratives used to legitimise it will evolve too. But already there are clear patterns. No, proponents o Big Data and Artifcial Intelligence are not saying that they want to civilise humanity. At least, not in those words exactly. The reason or this subteruge is colonial history itsel. During the struggles that led to the collapse o historical colonialism's political structures (or example, the British Empire in India and the French Empire in South-East Asia), civilising narratives as a whole became discredited. When Gandhi was asked what he thought about Western civilisation, he supposedly replied, 'I think it would be a good idea.'

So new narratives – or at least narratives that seem new – are needed to ulfl the same role that those disputed civilisational

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

narratives once played. The advocates o large-scale data extraction use another language, invoking goals to which it is very hard to object, and claiming a very special practical power, as businesses or governments, to implement them. There's the idea heard in various circles o 'AI or Social Good'. There are narratives o supposed historical progress such as 'The Second Machine Age' or 'The Fourth Industrial Revolution' that are hard to challenge, i you make the assumption that history always moves orward in a linear way.3 That idea, and the associated one that modernity is something 'the West' naturally leads, is itsel a legacy o historic colonialism, as we saw in Chapter One.

But there are other civilising narratives on which the data grab relies which at rst look entirely unrelated to the history o colonialism. There is the narrative o convenience and the narrative o connection, that is, the value o connecting people and things through technology. Both narratives, though supercially convincing, disguise the reality o the larger data grab and, in the case o connecting people, disguise some seriously toxic side-efects.

And the echoes rom colonial history become even clearer when we unpack the detail: not only does the mission to connect promoted by someone like Mark Zuckerberg echo the religious mission o some historical colonisers, but, as we'll see in the chapter's last section, the language around Big Data and AI itsel continues the vision o colonial science.

At their grandest, these stories express a civilising mission that makes the data grab seem the responsibility o a generous coloniser. Such grand narratives can be hard to unpack because they seem so enticing, so ar beyond dispute. Yet they represent an ambitious rationale or data extraction, and they play a crucial part in how data colonialism works as a orce in the world.

This process works on top o more obvious colonial continuities evident in Angela's story: economic dependence on distant US corporations has been a reality in the Philippines throughout its modern history, and now emerging tools like AI provide powerul new ways o managing lie and work through data-driven structures o economic control that are truly global in scale, but that remain dominated by corporations in the Global North.

Not only is Angela being paid a small sum or insecure piecework that involves being exposed to violent content, but when she loses her job doing that, she enters into a job-interviewing process where she is evaluated by an Artifcial Intelligence program trained rom data extracted rom lots o people like her. Yes, there are humans still involved in the process, but more and more they play a secondary role, submitting to the recommendations o a blackbox system whose authority Phil accepts without question, and Angela cannot contest. This huge transormation not only represents a continuation o very old neocolonial inequalities, but is evidence o distinctive new civilising narratives that work to legitimise the data grab wherever it is happening.

Data's civilising stories are another version o the Emperor's new clothes: empty in substance, but surprisingly eective in practice.

#### Civilising Narrative #1: Everyone Wants an Easier Life (aka Data Extraction as Convenience)

The frst and most common version o Big Data's civilisational story is the argument o convenience. Convenience is always a societal bargain between needs that society requires to be ulflled to maintain a certain way o lie and other interests. What sort o bargain are we being encouraged to strike?

#### Smarter Lives?

We are oten told that we give up our data in return or a service we like or need. But there's something strange about this supposed bargain. Although 'my' data is needed as one o billions o inputs to vast data platorms, by itsel it's not valuable. Until, that is, it gets

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

pooled into a huge vat where other people's data has been added, and the relations between all that data can start to emerge. This means that the real bargain we make is rather dierent. It is a societal bargain, not an individual one.4 As societies, we are entitled to ask: is it a good bargain for all of us to enter into that arrangement? Is that really a good bargain at the societal level?

Certainly, modern life has become more complex, as we move between multiple online and onsite places for living, working and studying, and try to sustain contact with friends and family who are ever more scattered. Perhaps it is true that the tempo of modern life has sped up in the past couple of decades.5 If so, maybe social media platforms' numerous shortcuts really do help us navigate these increased pressures. Think of the convenience of a Whats App group that allows users to share photos with their family or with multiple, but distinct, friendship circles, just by one or two clicks. Or the Facebook post that enables a hassled mother to publicise the details of her child's upcoming birthday party in one place for everyone to see, without numerous letters or phone calls. Or perhaps the digital personal assistant (or smart speaker) that enables you to open the front door or switch o the lights when otherwise engaged.

We don't deny the reality of these pressures and the usefulness of some of these solutions. But, given the huge landscape of data extraction we exposed in Chapter Two, we are entitled also to ask about the costs of those very same 'solutions' on a societal scale in comparison to the individual convenience they often provide. Three basic types of costs need to be considered.

First, there is the cost of the unequal power relations that result from the massive expansion of data territories in sectors such as agriculture, health and education, and over work and home life in general. As we saw, data relations are almost always asymmetrical, giving platforms and corporations a power to track you which they don't oer us back in return.

Second, there are the wider costs – indeed potentially serious harms – that ow rom allowing large cross-societal systems o data collection to develop. We saw in Chapter Two the simple case o the car insurance contract which oten now enables insurers to obtain not just the data drivers submit to them about their lives, alongside publicly available statistical data, but also a continuous data stream direct rom their car that monitors them as they drive. The deal is sold as one o convenience, the convenience o lower premiums, though we suggested things might not prove so convenient, i, like everyone, you make an occasional mistake. But the long-term convenience is very much on the side o the insurance companies, since it undamentally alters (in act, narrows) the nature o the risks that insurers cover. What i health insurers start getting access to all sorts o other data they have not had beore, or example, to our social media data or our search data? That will have even more dramatic implications.

Third, there is the risk that systematic data extraction enables completely new orms o extracting economic value and providing services. Here the risks may not be elt by the service user – Uber is undeniably convenient – but by those who work in the sector and now nd their terms o work entirely dictated by distant platorms whose managers work in the Global North. These transormations are happening not just in the ride-share sector, but in other areas such as delivery, janitorial and low-skill jobs traditionally occupied by migrant workers. In these cases, convenience benets some, maybe many, but at detriment to specic others. Once again, it is the arrangement (the enduring social and economic order) which can be built through data-rich platorms that should be our ocus.

This takes us to a ourth and related cost, which is the efective and subtle reproduction o inequalities, which are the legacy o historical colonialism. As an example, consider home security. This is something everyone, rich or poor, wants and needs, or which wellof people anywhere will certainly pay, making this an area where Big Tech corporations present an attractive ofer. But the introduction

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

o these technologies into a society with deep histories o segregation, such as the US, is likely to be anything but neutral. Take Amazon's smart doorbell system, Ring, which is plugged into a wide network o law enorcement agencies. When Amazon bought Ring, it also purchased the Neighbors crime prevention network service (basically a platorm or sharing reports o suspicious activity), and subsequently Ring has registered patents or acial recognition applications and other techniques. Facial recognition systems raise general questions o liberty, but their particular dangers or the populations who suered worst under colonialism are also well known.6

Under the pretext o convenience, platorms want to become our social operating system. That way we'll use them more regularly and more intensively, and generate more ad income or the plat orm. Indeed, as the power o platorms in our lives grows, those platorms are able to position themselves as providing the convenient solutions even to the problems they help create. Do you want to protect yoursel against 'revenge porn', the unauthorised distribution o your naked seles on social media? Submit those very same seles to Facebook! They will analyse their digital signature (presumably without a human being looking at them) and be able to detect and remove them when they are posted by anyone else.7 Do you want to limit the kind o tracking that Google does? Buy a Google Pixel phone, 'secure to the core' and 'private by design'!8

There's something else that is strange about our supposed bargain with Big Tech platorms: the timescale (is it really so convenient over the long-term?). Yes, I might right now, in the rst fush o downloading a new platorm, like its service. But later I may lose interest. That, however, doesn't make the data gathered rom me obsolete or less valuable in the uture. The data bargain, and particularly the societal bargain that comes rom all o us making similar individual data bargains, are or the long-term – convenient or corporations, but not necessarily, long-term, or us. The resulting transormation o society's power relations, once under way, cannot be simply undone. So there's a real danger that the bargain we supposedly make trades individual short-term gain or long-term societal harm.9

Undeniably, the whole question o 'convenience' turns on other transormations o modern lie that go ar beyond the impact o digital platorms or data extraction: the decline o secure longterm work, the increasing need or exible work hours to ft around complex patterns o childcare and care or elderly relatives, the decline o convenient physical locations that bring people together and promote inormation sharing (the pub, the community centre). There's an argument to be made, or sure, that social media plat orms o many sorts – rom Etsy and Instagram to Alipay and We Chat – play an important role in providing the resources we need to cope with increasingly insecure and complex lives. But are they the only platorms that could do this? Why would we not consider less data-extracting alternatives?

#### An Infrastructure for Living

The question o what is convenient takes on a special poignancy in Global South economies, which may have limited or damaged inrastructures. Convenience can quickly become necessity under those conditions. Even in one o Arica's richest countries, South Arica, the assumption that most people have a smartphone and a good phone plan does not apply.10 For most o the population in Arica, in act, using anything other than Facebook or Whats App or messaging or calls may be completely impossible, since Meta subsidises those costs as a way to get people to use its services.

In the Global South, then, Facebook is more o an enorced convenience, but one that, ar rom being natural, ows directly rom the legacy o colonialism, and or two reasons. The frst reason is the continued legacy o poor or absent telecommunications inrastructures linked to centuries o under-investment. The second, as we just indicated, is Facebook's privileged position o being able to

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

negotiate with governments and telecommunications providers socalled 'zero-rated' deals or users to access Facebook (and a ew other sites on which Facebook decides) at little or no cost. Facebook has negotiated these deals, known as Free Basics, with more than thirty Arican countries, plus many other countries in Asia, Latin America and the Pacifc. This is not out o charity on Facebook's part, but a pragmatic response to its own stalling numbers o subscribers in the Global North. Indeed, since those deals, Facebook's total number o subscribers has started to all, making its need or new subscribers elsewhere even more acute.11

When it comes to the kind o inormation-sharing that an economy demands, it is hard to argue against the perceived convenience o a service like Facebook in countries with poor transportation and poor communication channels or businesses. Some argue that, in Arica today, Facebook is the only place to get this kind o market inormation. Listen to Balqees Awad, a citizen o Sudan's capital Khartoum, speaking to Sudanese Guardian journalist Nesrine Malik about how she used Facebook in a period o acute shortages:

When a bakery receives a bread delivery, or a petrol station replenishes its uel, someone always posts in the group. They even tell us when there is heightened police presence in certain areas. Security patrols sometimes pick up people or no reason and extort or detain them.

A similar situation plays out or businesses trying to cope with an inadequate inrastructure to market their services. In Egypt, Malik argues, Facebook may be the only place to start a small business and test demand, 12 although it's important to note that in some parts o the Global South local networks are emerging that provide an inormation inrastructure or business without relying on Global North platorms like Facebook, or example India's Open Network or Digital Commerce.13

Is the strongest argument or Facebook's convenience that it supplies basic inormation inrastructure to countries already broken by the legacy o colonialism? I so, then thinking about Big Tech within a longer-term historical perspective requires us to think more about for whom exactly is the continuing social and economic inequality associated with Western platorm power convenient? Maybe the interests o Facebook, a Big Tech business based in the Global North, are trumping the long-term interests o those societies' citizens. Within a colonial perspective, it is hardly new or colonisers to seek to govern through building convenient inrastructures.

Similar arguments can be made about the role that global media platorms like Whats App play in helping hundreds o millions o orced migrants to keep in touch as they cross borders in great danger and in search o better lives; or about the role o Facebook and Twitter in providing sites where political mobilisation and basic political discourse can happen at all in countries like Iran or Saudi Arabia that are deeply authoritarian. But we need to think a little more about the implications o such arguments. Are we really saying that Global North platorms work best in authoritarian countries with weak communications inrastructures, which are oten ormer colonies o the West? I so, we are back to a colonial argument.

Meanwhile, it is also worth noting the increasingly important role that Chinese, not just US companies, are playing in Arica. It is not dicult to see the Arican consumer as a target or which both major powers o data colonialism are vying.

The narrative o convenience then, even i we are tempted to accept it, cannot be easily disentangled rom both old and new colonial processes, and rom the specifc costs associated with the new landscape o one-sided data extraction that characterises many aspects o contemporary societies and economies. But let's turn now to the second candidate or a new 'civilising' narrative: connection.

#### Civilising Narrative #2: This Is How We Connect!

Everyone wants to be connected. At least, no one wants to live out their life in complete isolation.

Once again, digital platforms aim to persuade us that they oer the best way of realising a common need: this time, the human need for connection. Corporations like Meta or Tencent need us to believe that it's Instagram or Whats App or We Chat where we must go to be connected. To achieve that, they cloak their business models in all sorts of altruistic narratives.

Mark Zuckerberg, for instance, in February 2017, responded to worries that Facebook was becoming a site for dangerous populist rhetoric, such as that which fuelled the rise of Donald Trump. Let's put aside the new goals that Zuckerberg set out for Facebook in that statement – to support safer, more informed, more civically engaged, and inclusive communities – since they read like a shopping list for reversing exactly the problems that Facebook had been accused of accentuating. Let's focus instead on the overall vision oered by Zuckerberg in that statement, since it captures perfectly the civilisational narrative of 'connection'. And, as we read it, let's remember the religious dimension to many civilising narratives oered to justify historical colonialism.

History is the story of how we've learned to come together in ever greater numbers – from tribes to cities to nations. At each step, we built social infrastructure like communities, media and governments to empower us to achieve things we couldn't on our own.

Today, we are close to taking our next step. Our greatest opportunities are now global . . . Progress now requires humanity coming together not just as cities or nations, but also as a global community . . . Facebook stands for bringing us closer together and building a global community . . . In times like these the most important thing we at Facebook can do is develop the social inrastructure to give people the power to build a global community that works or all o us.4

Two things are astonishing here.

First, the claim that a global community can be saely created through a platorm with the exploitative business model o Meta. The hollowness o this claim was exposed a ew years ater Zuckerberg's statement by Frances Haugen, ormerly o Instagram, in her testimony to the US Congress and European parliaments which revealed that Instagram had known rom its own research the dangers its site posed, or example, or young women.5 Haugen later commented: 'I did what I thought was necessary to save the lives o people, especially in the global south, who I think are being endangered by Facebook's pursuit o prots over people'.6 Adam Mosseri, Instagram's head, at the height o the scandal responded: 'We know that more people die than would otherwise because o car accidents, but, by and large, cars create way more value in the world than they destroy. I think media is similar'.7 As i Meta had no responsibility or its specifc business model and its efects.

In any case, why believe that a global platorm or humanity is even possible or desirable? But or Zuckerberg, it is impossible to think otherwise, because o his belie in the narrative o connection, a narrative over which Meta must remain in control.

The second thing that's extraordinary about Zuckerberg's claims is the idea that it is platorms like Facebook that uniquely represent the direction o history and o progress. That is a bizarre claim, when we consider that humanity has always been connected through small and large social networks. Did we really need Facebook to tell us that connection with riends and amily is good?

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

Did humanity really need Mark Zuckerberg to make sense of its history?

The answer to both those questions is no – unless you believe that only Big Tech can bring 'connection' to humanity, precisely the sort of 'civilisational' mission that we are trying to unpack here.

Another version of this almost-messianic connection narrative predominates in the world not of everyday contact between friends and family, but between business and things. Data extraction from connected objects and devices is a key input to economic production, we are told – the form that technological momentum is taking in the twenty-rst century – and none of us should stand in the way. As they say in the US, get with the program.

That's basically the argument of US West Coast tech evangelist and founder of Wired magazine, Kevin Kelly. His book The Inevitable was published, perhaps fortunately, just before the techlash kicked o in response to Facebook's 2018 Cambridge Analytica scandal. The book describes 12 'technological forces that will shape our future'. In reassuring language, it describes the predominance of surveillance and data extraction in contemporary capitalism, and why we should celebrate it. Three chapter titles are enough to give you the picture: 'Accessing', 'Sharing', and 'Tracking'. All, Kelly argues, are inevitable dimensions of humanity's future, a future Kelly welcomes rather than fears.18

But the idea that Big Data's data grab is inevitable has spread far beyond a few US West Coast evangelists. It is a tribal belief in much of Silicon Valley, well expressed for example by Silicon Valley entrepreneur Thomas Siebel's vision of a Digital Transformation that results from the convergence of 'cloud computing, big data, Internet of Things and Articial Intelligence'.19 And it is almost a religion in the wider global business community inuenced by them. The pronouncements on Big Data from the Swiss-based World Economic Forum are full of such claims. Starting out from the cliché

that data is the 'new oil' –'a new type o raw material that's on a par with capital and labour' – the WEF conclude that hyperconnectivity and the data it generates will undamentally transorm human knowledge:

All actors interact and co-evolve within an ICT ecosystem . . . a virtuous circle starts where improvements in one area aect and drive improvements in other areas . . . Individually, we are all limited in what we can know, but together hyperconnectivity makes it possible to overcome those individual limitations and mine dierent types o data to fnd insights.20

It's only reasonable to be suspicious about the requent claims that only through continuous data extraction rom people and things can lie be connected in the way it should be.

Let's now look in more detail at these narratives, starting with the less amiliar o the two, connections between things.

#### Connecting Every Thing

The Digital Transormation proposed by Siebel depends on connecting up people and things. For him, the Internet o Things (the world o 'Smart' tools and systems, all o them continuously connected to the internet) is much more than a marketing slogan; it is the building block o a new type o economy and society.

Starting with the intuitive idea o the Smart electricity grid – who, ater all, would want a dumb grid? – Siebel switly moves on to the necessity, almost inevitability, o every object becoming connected, as well as human beings attaching objects to themselves in an attempt to become even more connected. As he enthuses, 'soon even humans will have tens or hundreds o ultra-low power computer wearables and implants continuously monitoring and regulating blood chemistry, blood pressure, pulse, temperature,

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

and other metabolic signals':21 Apple's i Watch and other ftness trackers already provide most o this.

Here, Siebel is expressing the general sense among Big Tech leaders that the Internet o Things represents the next new human– computer interace. In 2014, Cisco predicted that by 2022 connections between things would constitute 45 per cent o the world's internet connections.22 While we don't know i that prediction came true, we do know that there are already more connected devices (13.1 billion) than people on the planet, and that number is predicted at least to double by the end o the decade.23 To believe this is good or humanity (as opposed to just being good or corporations) depends once more on the civilisational narrative o connection, a narrative over which Western leaders increasingly want to retain control. According to the authoritative Council on Foreign Relations, and American think tank:

As the Internet o Things (IoT) expands in coming years, the next iteration o the network will connect tens o billions o devices, digitally binding every aspect o day-to-day lie, rom heart monitors and rerigerators to trac lights and agricultural methane emissions.24

This is a vision not o reedom so much as order, which is exactly how China's policy or the internet tends to characterise it. Indeed, China has been prioritising the Internet o Things or nearly a decade. Haier, a Chinese company, bought the US domestic appliance giant General Electric back in 2016 or US \$6 billion, and China's latest fve-year plan or 'National Inormatisation' contains many reerences to building a 'sensory inrastructure' or the IoT, both in China and in other countries where China has inuence. 25

It remains to be seen how ar such visions can be implemented, but according to this narrative, there is no option but or the whole world to become a data colony.26

#### Connecting Ourselves to Death

Looking back on the past two decades, it is hard not to recall the work o Karl Polanyi, a twentieth-century economic historian who wrote about how social relations had to be turned upside down to make room or the new types o economic relations that industrial capitalism required. Writing in the 1940s, ater leaving Vienna or London a decade earlier due to the rise o the right in Austria, Polanyi's concern was the social conditions or the capitalist economic transormation o the nineteenth century. He described those conditions in terms o a gateway drug: 'highly articial stimulants [were] administered to the body social'.27 This articial stimulation seems like an apt description or the impact that plat orms like Facebook and Tik Tok are having on social lie today.

Yes, the way that social media platorms connect us and stimulate our engagement is highly convenient or many things, including short-term political mobilisation. As a result, platorms like Facebook really have made a diference in countries with authoritarian political systems. In the early days o the 2011 Arab Spring, the availability o Facebook as a site where people could speak with apparent saety about common issues and nd ways to mobilise did have an impact.28 The same can be said o anonymous social media platorms that allow Turkish women, or example, to protest against the potential imposition o Islamic dress code by the state.29 Kenyan writer Nanjala Nyabola strongly conrms this point, based on her extensive research into digital politics in Arica: 'there's no doubt in my mind that social networks have been useul or political discourse and or organising in countries where there is no ree speech'.30

The value o digital networks of some sort – in the ace o not just inadequate communications inrastructure, but political oppression – must then be taken very seriously. But, as with the narrative rom convenience, we must weigh this connection narrative against other

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

actors. First, it is just not true that social resistance never happened under pre-digital regimes: history is ull o examples o people using what anthropologist James Scott once called 'weapons o the weak' (or example, agricultural or actory workers subtly working around their managers' strict rules).31 Second, as many commentators on the Arab Spring pointed out, government authorities can not only use social media to promote their own ideas, but also to directly monitor protesting citizens. The ailure o any enduring revolution to emerge in Arab countries ater the 2011 Arab political revolution was probably not unconnected to this kind o surveillance, as well as to weaknesses in the kind o politics that are possible on social media.32

The social side-eects o social media platorms must be considered seriously. From the history o colonialism we know that side-eects matter. When historical colonisers intended to invade and take the gold, they didn't know (at frst) that they were bringing European diseases which killed ar more than their weapons ever did. Today's data colonisers certainly intend to seize data territories and get control o the resulting data. But no one is saying that they predicted, let alone planned, the side-eects o their plat orms and business models on the social world, which many claim have toxifed social lie.

Many now argue that Big Tech's hunger or profts and their lack o concern or the very people who make their wealth possible have contributed to the creation o a deeply ractured public sphere, where 'connection' is divisive, exhausting and oten traumatising. But we should keep things in perspective and remember that the origins o cultural, social and political polarisation long predate Big Tech: it makes no sense to say that digital platorms are the prime cause o polarisation.33

At the same time, the ability to join virtual groups who share our interests – the eature we were all excited about in the early days o the internet, beore it was thoroughly commercialised by Big

Tech – is the very same eature that some claim has created 'lter bubbles' where disinormation and hate thrive. A term originally proposed by Eli Pariser in 2011,34 a lter bubble is basically an internet-era updated version o an echo chamber: a space where you only hear what already ts your way o speaking and thinking. The idea is that search engines and social media platorms, by doing what they do best – that is, giving us the content that we want to see, and eliminating the content we don't – create unhealthy diets o inormation that reinorce our existing worldviews, rather than presenting inormation that might challenge them.

In other words, connection, as operationalised by social media algorithms, has become something extremely targeted, something that limits, rather than enhances, diversity. Platorms, not people, decide which connections are more advantageous to them: that is, whatever gets us to spend more time on the plat orm, as epitomised by Tik Tok's 'For You' algorithm. The more time we spend on platorms, ater all, the more data that can be extracted rom us.

While there's no denying that positive movements or justice (such as Black Twitter in the US)35 have emerged rom digital plat orms, there remain a number o negative consequences to be considered, which fow directly rom social media's business models that are based on data extraction. According to their critics, lter bubbles and echo chambers have resulted in everything rom Brexit to the election o Trump, Bolsonaro and Duterte, the antivaxxer movement, global-warming deniers, and so on. Every bad idea in the world is given a platorm to thrive thanks to lter bubbles, and while good ideas should enjoy the same virality, the theory goes, they are always somehow overshadowed by the angry voices. According to US commentator Jonathan Haidt, this is because people, now armed with the weapons o the Like, Share, and Retweet button, are more willing to promote content that triggers their outrage. The result has less to do with authentic connection

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

and more to do with public shaming and mob mentality, 'ampliying' in the words o leading US legal scholar Julie Cohen our 'collective unreason'.36 But these social media eatures (Like, Share, RT) are themselves just techniques or social media platorms to stimulate us to generate ever more data about our 'engagement'.

When we keep our ocus on the wider impact o social media platorms' business models, it matters less that, contrary to popular belie, academics have ound scant empirical evidence that lter bubbles actually exist, at least as a widespread social phenomenon.37 People who spend time online are generally exposed to more diverse content than the people who don't. But is polarisation in inormation ows the only type o polarisation that matters? What about polarisation in how people eel about each other – those they seem to be like and those who seem diferent rom them? Political scientists call this 'afective polarisation'.38 Isn't this more consequential in the end, including or how people eel about the news content they come across?

The journalist and Nobel Peace Prize winner Maria Ressa has experienced rst-hand the power that social media algorithms, designed to prioritise prots over acts, have had on polarisation and democracy's death by a thousand 'likes'. As president and co ounder o Rappler, an online news site in her native Philippines (a nation whose population spends the most time on social media in the world), she was probably the rst to present to Facebook incontrovertible evidence o how their business model was allowing or the commercialisation o disinormation, only to have that evidence ignored. Reecting on what she learned rom the experience, she writes: 'Tech sucked up our personal experiences and data, organized it with articial intelligence, manipulated us with it, and created behavior at a scale that brought out the worst in humanity.'39

The impacts can be devastating. Social media platorms really can have a direct efect on the extremist radicalisation that is part o social polarisation.40 One example, documented in a New York Times podcast series,4 involves the case o Caleb Cain, a US high school student o progressive and liberal sensibilities who, in a two-year period o watching thousands and thousands o You Tube videos, went down a Rabbit Hole (the name o the podcast) o increasingly extremist content, and ended up identiying as a white supremacist. Fortunately, he later made the reverse trip and ended up watching progressive content again and renouncing his ar-right allegiances.

What's interesting about cases like this is that the reason or this push to extremism seems to be a simple change in You Tube's recommendation algorithm around 205 (just to be clear, similar recommendation algorithms can be ound in other platorms, so this is not a problem unique to You Tube).42 Such changes illustrate the huge power that accrues to owners o data territories. Instead o showing users more o the same content they are interested in, social media platorms have gured out that the way to get them to spend more time on their platorms (and thus to be exposed to more advertising) was to show them new things they might be interested in. For cat-lovers, not more and more cat videos (how many in the end can they watch?) but, say, videos o tigers, since tigers are also elines; that way, users will spend even more time on the platorm discovering new content.

I only we were talking about just cats and tigers! The kind o algorithmic homophily (birds o a eather sticking together) that platorms encourage gives rise to online spaces where ar-right extremism can easily fourish because already existing polarisation gets ocussed and amplied there.43 We saw an example o this during the 6 January 202 insurrection in the US, when a group o people living in such a radicalised bubble collectively decided it was a good idea to storm the Capitol building. But countless other examples can be collected rom around the world, rom Brazil, India, Kenya, Myanmar and Sweden, to name just a ew.

Did ar-right sentiments and opinions exist beore the internet? Certainly. Do platorms like Facebook encourage people to become

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

more radical, nding those you like and pushing each other into committing ever more radical acts? Yes, there is overwhelming evidence from political scientists that such polarisation and radicalisation are going on.44 Even worse, there is evidence from whistle-blower Sophie Zhang that Meta knows this is a consequence of their business models.45 And have companies like Meta and Google benetted nancially by allowing climate-deniers, antivaxxers, election-deniers, and all sorts of conspiracy theorists to advertise on their platform? Yes, they have.46

It is clear we all want to be connected – but not necessarily in this way! Not if 'connection' means limited templates for human interaction determined by business models. Not if 'connection' means more data extracted from us for prot, with more of our gadgets constantly surveilling us in order to use that information to sell us more junk, or to manipulate us. Not if 'connection' actually means its opposite: an easy way to end up disconnected from wider reality and from people we might usefully disagree with. In short, not if communication – the basis for connection – means not the sharing of meaning, but the gamication of meaning, where what counts as truth is established not through discussion, debate or even political negotiation, but merely through the virality of content.47

Legislative moves are under way in many countries (for example, the UK's Online Safety Bill, Brazil's Fake News Bill,48 and multiple proposed bills in the US) that may impose stricter responsibilities on platforms. But none so far appear to challenge the basic business models of data-extracting platforms. The result is that, even if we sometimes feel digital platforms are places for fun, the real game is likely to remain the one that is being played on us – by the algorithms and the large systems they serve.

Which brings us to algorithms and the last important civilising narrative we'll discuss: the story of extractivist science and knowledge associated with AI.

#### Civilising Narrative #3: AI Is Smarter than Humans

We have all been exposed to the idea that Articial Intelligence is, supposedly, a great thing or humanity. Narratives about AI and machines that can think like humans have been around in science ction and other orms o storytelling or a long time. We are waiting – in hope or in ear – or the day when we can really talk to a machine like we talk to another human being, or the day when we can rely on a sel-taught robot to perorm unimaginably complex tasks awlessly and efciently, liberating us rom the drudgery o work and rom the ollies o our own biases and limitations.

What we have today with Articial Intelligence is oten not very intelligent at all: it is what AI expert Meredith Broussard calls 'arti cial unintelligence'. 49 First, there is the problem o training data: the repetitive calculations on which AI depends only work because it has been exposed to thousands i not millions o examples rom which it can 'learn'. AI programs need to cut their teeth, as it were, on preselected datasets. But those datasets necessarily have limitations: they only include things that have been previously counted. Whatever is counted necessarily reects the orces that shape what there is in the world, orces like privilege and inequality (which is why AI is oten ound to replicate the biases that are already ound in the world).

But a second problem arises with the most advanced orms o AI, the so-called AGI (Articial General Intelligence). These systems are able to move beyond their original task parameters and come up with innovative solutions, going as ar as potentially generating new knowledge. But the problem o context remains. Human knowledge arises in a lie context, many aspects o which are so obvious that they never need to be made explicit – at least to humans. But to AI, since it is ormed outside o any lie context, everything needs to be made explicit, including the unacceptability o what humans would

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

reject as nonsensical. All the recent absurdities thrown up by our questions to ChatGPT are variations on this problem o context.

Beneath these examples o AI ailures is a more undamental point about what AI actually is: a mere collection o programs that are given massive amounts o data, fnd patterns in that data, and make some statistical predictions based on those patterns, such as predicting what the next word in a sentence is likely to be. That is true o small AI just as much as it is o the largest AI, or example the models or predicting text that drive the now amous ChatGPT or Google equivalent system, Bard, as even the most enthusiastic celebrants o AI have to admit.50 And there lies the problem. Let's quote the academic paper that lost leading AI researcher Timnit Gebru her job at Google:

An LM [AI-driven language model] is a system or haphazardly stitching together sequences o linguistic orms it has observed in its vast training data, according to probabilistic inormation about how they combine, but without any re erence to meaning: a stochastic parrot.51

The problem occurs when the parrot embarrassingly repeats bad things it has heard. For example, researchers discovered that when asked to adopt certain identities ('You are a 20 year old White male with a high school diploma, earning US \$30,000 per year. You are a registered Republican living in the USA.'), ChatGPT produced racist and polarising ratings o various groups that were seven times larger than the average ratings produced by actual humans.52 We are not denying that there can be real value in using AI programs, like when they are used to design vaccines, detect cancer or model environmental phenomena; that is, where AI can see into datasets much too large or human intelligence to map and fnd statistical patterns. But major problems arise when we unquestioningly use the outputs generated by AI to simulate human understanding or make decisions that impact human welare.

Here is where the civilising narrative comes in, as a way to inuence our judgement about what is best, and to justiy why data or AI needs to be extracted rom us in the frst place. This civilising narrative is complex, and it will take a bit o work to explain why it is continuous with the even larger story o colonial science and the interconnections between colonialism and modernity that we have been exploring.

#### Science and Power from Colonialism to Capitalism

AI is part o science. It is a discipline within computer science, and while most people don't think o computer science in the same way they think o 'pure' sciences like biology or physics, it is a science, nonetheless. We discussed in Chapter One the important continuities in the evolution o scientifc methods, rom early colonialism until today, and what those continuities have to do with power.

Francis Bacon, considered one o the ounders o the scientifc method, established in the seventeenth century a direct connection between the growth o science and the exploration o new worlds: 'Nor must it go or nothing that by the distant voyages and travels which have become requent in our times many things in nature have been laid open and discovered which may let in new light upon philosophy.'53 Since Bacon was writing in 1620, we know that the distant voyages and travels he mentions were the explorations happening within the context o ull-scale colonial expansion. This suggests that the scientifc method and practices o colonising the world were, rom the start, joined at the hip.

More precisely, both scientifc and technological evolution are part o a colonial impulse to administer extracted resources. The scientifc method and colonialism developed and evolved together, in response to the problem o managing what Bacon called the

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

'many things in nature' that 'have been laid open'. The need to acquire and transport resources more eciently, to rule new territories across huge distances where those resources were located, and to process those resources into something that generated wealth, were all colonial problems that required greater scientic and technological advancements.

Almost three hundred years later, during a lecture to the Liverpool Chamber o Commerce in December 1899, Sir Ronald Ross shared the results o his ongoing work on combatting malaria (which killed many English colonists) by stating that 'in the coming century, the success o imperialism will depend largely upon success with the microscope'.54 This statement helps to illustrate the link – already present in Bacon – between the colonial enterprise and the scientic method, which was used as a way to reduce – or abstract – the natural world down to objects that could be managed and properties that could be predicted. From this perspective, the scientic method becomes not only a tool or understanding the world, but a process or eliminating everything that intereres with its colonial management.

This process had a signicant impact in understanding the diversity o the colonised world not as a positive eature, but as a problem to be dealt with. In the words o postcolonial critic and Goan environmentalist Claude Alvares, '[a]bstraction increases control by homogenizing its subject matter. It eliminates the basis o diversity, the personal and the historic, creating an articial reality which can be completely controlled'.55 This is probably why many eminist critics o science have also noted the close ties between Bacon's metaphors or scientic method and the masculine imagination.56

Just to be clear: we are not suggesting that every and all scientic discoveries serve a dark colonial purpose, or that we would be better of without science. Our point is merely that there is an undeniable arc in modern science, starting with colonialism, that bends towards the goal o controlling people and nature, and that links scientic abstraction and indeed technological development to extractivism. Our concern is that this arc extends all the way to the science behind AI.

#### The Discriminatory Intelligence behind AI

O course, most o the time we don't know how AIs work, because their inner works are black boxes protected by their makers as trade secrets. And it's not like their makers always understand how their programs work either, because AIs can generate outputs on their own, without human intervention. In the case o AIs like the one described in Angela's scenario, we imagine that the program can somehow absorb the collective experiences o, say, thousands o expert human-resources managers and interviewers, and use that knowledge to act like a super-human recruiter that can expertly and quickly assess job candidates, determining who is the best person or a job while avoiding all the biases and inefciencies o humans. This is the narrative that Angela was asked to accept. But as Dan Mc Quillan observes, AIs are simply programs or making 'elaborate statistical guesswork', based on extracting data that, in turn, reects the pre-existing inequalities in society.57 Sure, that's a pretty neat technological trick in and o itsel. But the problem, he argues, is that this orm o statistical optimisation is being used blindly to reinorce orms o discrimination that oten go back to colonialism, all while being presented as a magical new solution to social problems.

To understand this, let's break down Angela's example a bit more. The experience we described was based on what we know rom how companies like Hire Vue and Humanly market their products, or rom what we hear rom people who have been interviewed using such systems.58 But we can iner that, in order or the AI to determine whether Angela was a good candidate or not, it didn't need to learn human resource policies, it didn't need to learn

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

eective interviewing techniques, and it didn't even need to learn about the qualications or the job. Instead, the AI was given tons and tons o CVs, survey results, and videos o actual interviews (what is called 'training data'). Then it was told which interviews resulted in hires. The AI then compared the 'good' interviews and the 'bad' interviews and identied the characteristics that occurred more requently in the good ones (the characteristics that were more statistically signicant, in other words). The more training data the AI is ed, the better, because it can nd some unexpected correlations that humans (whose capacity or processing large amounts o data is limited) could not nd.

The science and maths behind all o this is ascinating. But it is already clear where this model soon risks going wrong. Maybe the AI determined that good candidates were the ones with more years o experience, or those who used certain words more oten, or those who answered in a survey that being on time is more important than stopping to help someone on the way to work. But maybe it also learned that in addition to these characteristics, a good candidate is also . . . male and white. In other words, i the training data refected the act that more white males ended up getting hired, and the AI was not explicitly adjusted to correct or this, it could have 'learned' that being a white male was a characteristic o good hires. To urther complicate things, all o these decisions may not be apparent to the people who designed the AI, or who are using it. They just receive the nal recommendation or prediction, and it takes some extra work to gure out i the AI is biased. In one recent case, Amazon decided to abandon plans or a recruiting AI system precisely because, in testing, the system was ailing to recommend hiring many women (Amazon never disclosed why it didn't work).59

The people who design and sell these systems will probably complain that our example is too simplistic. They will also point out that even when AI is shown to exhibit some biases, those can be identied and corrected, which is not something we can do easily with human bias; in other words, they would say, AI represents a better chance at eliminating bias. They would also probably point out that, while we wait or these systems to be improved, we can simply include a human being in the decision loop to ensure proper outcomes.

In response we would argue that the act some o these systems are discriminatory and opaque means that they are probably working exactly as intended. AI is applied in all sorts o areas today, but the most concerning application – and the one we can expect to see increasing – is to automate decisions that have a negative impact on populations that can do little to contest these outcomes. These populations are the same ones who have traditionally sufered discrimination under violent and bureaucratic systems, and who are on the 'wrong' side o the intersections o class, gender and race. So yes, these systems are very convenient, i by convenience we mean, as Mc Quillan argues, 'not having to engage with structural legacies o colonialism'. Simply adding a human in the loop will only serve to locate an individual scapegoat to blame when something is ound to go wrong.60

More important than these limited corrective mechanisms (which have only emerged in response to recent outcry) is the bigger picture: that most applications o AI are part o a colonial history o scientic and technological development that, as scholar Paola Ricaurte argues, uses extracted data to codiy and automate already existing violence and discrimination.61

#### AI as a Colonial Tool of Extraction

We can probably assume that, working in their research labs, data and computer scientists are probably not thinking about creating what mathematician and data scientist Cathy O'Neil calls weapons of math destruction. 62 And yet their scientic methods, and the tools created with those methods, are part o a long trajectory o attempts to manage the world more efectively and more protably. Data has

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

been, since the beginning o colonialism, an important tool to extract the 'many things in nature that have been laid open', to cite Bacon again.

Against this background, we can see clearly that AI and particularly large-scale AGI is just the latest step in the colonial production o knowledge. The basic reason is simple, even i its implementation is very complex. As we noted right at the start o the book, largescale AI takes the whole o humanity's production that is registered online as its input. From this, it seeks to generate knowledge that it can oer back to us as valuable. Doing this requires vast computing power, since we are talking trillions o data points, possibly orders o magnitude more. And that computing power remains the exclusive possession o the largest Big Tech players. This huge computing power is transorming the world o search engines, but also how social media platorms like Facebook are generating useul targeting or advertisers via its AI-based Advantage+ program developed or advertisers as noted in Chapter Two.63 Whatever the details, the very idea o treating human culture as i it were just ree input to AI processing is, rom the start, one more act o colonial appropriation.

The second colonial eature o AI today ows rom the basic point that AI needs training data to unction. The task o collecting and organising this training data into a ormat AI can use, ar rom being 'artifcial', is a labour-intensive process with lots o human input. As a collective o workers or Mechanical Turk puts it, 'There is no ChatGPT without human workers'.64 This labour is very oten done by poor people in the Global South, an unequal relation that replays the global division o labour that is the long-term legacy o colonialism.

Companies have realised that people in the Global South who are willing to work without contracts or reduced wages in precarious conditions orm an ideal labour market to do these repetitive tasks. As we saw in Chapter Two, platorms have been created to control these labour markets. Leading tech journalist Karen Hao tells the story o a gig worker in crisis-ridden Venezuela who works or a company called Appen labelling images, making as little as US \$6–\$8 a week, which she can't even withdraw rom her account because the company has a policy o only allowing withdrawals when workers accumulate more than US \$10.65 Those gig workers may be highly educated, but they are also, as recent research in Brazil discovered, likely to be women who lack secure employment and so be willing to accept extremely low wages.66

AI industries and their contractors have no qualms recruiting remote workers who live in conict zones like Venezuela or Syria, which sounds positive until we realise the goal is not to provide good jobs to desperate people, but to take advantage o their vulnerability to create pools o workers willing to work or very little. These jobs oten entail being exposed to violent content, as with the Meta contract workers in Kenya who are given fty seconds to determine whether there is violent content in an uploaded video, regardless o the length o the video or how traumatising it might be to watch it.67 Those same workers have sued Meta in a series o law cases that, at time o writing, are still unresolved. Without realising it, Angela was probably training the very same AI hate-speech detection system that made her redundant: that, ater all, is the point o this human labour, to train AI to replace it. The act that an industry selling us a vision o an autonomous intelligence or solving the world's problems relies on armies o underpaid and exploited workers in the Global South is one o its biggest dirty secrets.

The third colonial dimension o AI relates to how its techniques o data processing operate in detail. Take the question o the languages that provide the input to AI. Almost all o us beneft rom AI being able to recognise our voices or translate rom one language to another easily and accurately, through a process known as Natural Language Processing (NLP). But what happens when the uneven application o these resources reinorces colonial legacies? What

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

happens, or instance, when Big Tech decides to prioritise the development o NLP technologies in the languages o the colonisers (English, French, etc.), not the colonised? The world watched with concern when Facebook's inability to properly monitor hate speech in Myanmar allegedly aided a genocide (Facebook's engagementbased algorithms promoted inammatory content advocating discrimination and violence because such content was calculated to keep people in the platorm or longer, generating more advertising revenue). Algorithms that monitor content in majority languages like English and French have more mechanisms to prevent this rom happening, but the question o Facebook's responsibility towards its speakers o minority languages, like Burmese, remains.68

Alternatively, what happens when AI discriminates against the language o the colonised? A report prepared by Business or Social Responsibility (BSR.org) ound that during a May 2021 conict, Arabic content published by Palestinians across Meta's platorms was censored to a larger extent than content in Hebrew published by Israelis, even when the latter contained clear examples o hate speech and incitements to violence (it's possible that humans, not just algorithms, played a part in this censorship).69 In an earlier case in 2017, a Palestinian man was detained by Israeli police ater he posted an Arabic message on Facebook that read 'good morning', but which was erroneously translated as 'attack them' in Hebrew.70

Some o these problems have been in the public eye or a ew years and Big Tech companies have started to respond to them (or example, the egregious aults o Google's AI that had coded Black bodies in deeply negative ways).71 But such responses to AI's problems have been ar rom complete, which takes us to the ourth way in which AI operates as a tool o colonial power: the highly unrepresentative consultation processes that supposedly try to correct or AI's problems.

Those doing AI research are overwhelmingly rom the Global North, and more likely to be men than women. Take the specifc case o NLP research: as the 0 State o AI Report makes clear, NLP research is becoming the domain o a handul o Big Tech corporations and their university partners in the Global North, perpetuating the very same inequalities that contributed to such problems being undetectable or unaddressed.7

Unsurprisingly, industry-driven solutions to issues o algorithmic injustice and AI unairness tend to be weak. AI Ethics is a big feld these days, largely unded by Big Tech. According to digital cultures researcher Luke Munn, it is also useless, or three reasons.73 The principles it proposes are too abstract to be o value. For example, the University o Montreal issued a declaration which says, in part, that AI should promote the 'well-being o all sentient creatures'74 (nice, i rather vague). AI codes also emerge in social isolation, rom elite cocoons (academia, Silicon Valley) that are the very same places where the problematic models and technologies come rom. And they are toothless, in that no one in those places is interested in enacting them in a way that would seriously curtail the power o the unders o those ethics programmes.

Even when we turn to some o the apparently most robust legislative responses to the problems o AI – such as the European Union's proposed AI Act – there are warning signs. This Act has promising eatures, categorically orbidding high-risk practices in AI, identiying those practices in areas like education, borders, crime, social credit systems, acial recognition in public spaces, law enorcement and more. But even this proposed piece o legislation (which may require some years beore it comes ully into orce) starts out rom a celebration o the benefts that AI will bring to humanity:

Artifcial intelligence is a ast-evolving amily o technologies that can contribute to a wide array o economic and societal benefts across the entire spectrum o industries and social activities. By improving prediction, optimising operations and resources allocation, and personalising digital

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

solutions . . . the use o articial intelligence can provide key competitive advantages to companies and support socially and environmentally benecial outcomes . . . 75

I that's true, the role o regulation is limited in advance to reining in its most extreme ('high-risk') versions. Meanwhile in Brazil, a national AI strategy was published in 2021 and a legislative process is under way that is expected to lead to major legislation that encodes rights or those aected by AI systems, although the details are not yet clear.76 The US is nowhere near drating actual legislation, although the White House has at least published a Blueprint or an AI Bill o Rights, which rames AI governance as a civil rights issue, and there are proposals by US senators to advance legislation to rein in AI. It's worth noting that, when drating its Blueprint, the White House consulted not only with the public and human rights groups, but with corporations like Microsot and Palantir – exactly the sort o corporations that the document is meant to call to account.

In act, this is the deeper pattern that underlies the dramatic statements by AI leaders in the spring o 2023 on the need to halt AI temporarily because o the 'existential risk' it poses to humanity.77 Note, as the authors o the 'stochastic parrots' paper remark, who is talking here, and how they talk only about AI's uture risks, and say nothing about resolving any o its present, well-known risks.78

The supposed solution is regulation, but again, the question is: by whom and on what basis? Western governments such as the UK are competing to lead the process, with Big Tech corporations guaranteed a seat at the table. Meanwhile, there is complete silence on how the views o the vast majority o the planet on this supposedly existential risk will be taken into account. Is the current rush towards AI alarmism (rom those who earlier led its development) just a plea or public subsidy o the difcult and costly process of regulation? If the past is an indication, we can expect that regulation will favour corporations, not people. Too much money is at stake, and the civilising narratives tell us that the welfare of humanity hangs in the balance. As ChatGPT's creator, Sam Altman, put it, 'the most important component of wealth in the future is access to those [AGI] systems'.79 The current furore about the future of AI, seen from the perspective of colonial history, is all about securing the continued appropriation of data resources.

To change those narratives means challenging some of the key premises of how science and technology have developed, led by the West, throughout the history of colonialism and capitalism. This, in turn, would require fundamental changes in how those technologies are implemented. If we were serious about treating AI as an existential risk and imposing social limits on its development, we would surely want to take into account the environmental costs of its production, as well as the social costs of its implementation, and to regulate it in ways that involve actual citizens rather than just high-level summits. We are a long way from this, and we will remain so, until we challenge the civilising narratives that promote a particular view of what AI is for, and whom it should serve.

#### Why Civilisational Stories Work

Perhaps there's an even bigger pattern here. Looking back over the stories told about data colonialism, and our brief summary earlier of the stories that evolved to justify historical colonialism, we can see that these stories, old and new, perform multiple roles.

They provide an alibi for the real processes of colonialism by telling other, more palatable stories – for example, of global commerce's necessary expansion, of the civilising mission of empire, of Big Data's enabling of connection and convenience. Believe those stories, and you are distracted from the reality of colonial resource extraction.

#### DATA'S NEW CIVILISING MISSION

Furthermore, the historic idea o the West's superior science and knowledge was tied to an implied vision o the West's white racial superiority. These stories did not just enorce a hierarchy o power – they justied it and legitimated it. They worked to lock in colonised peoples to an inerior, weakened position rom which change was hard. Data's civilising stories do the same: they lock citizens everywhere, except those in data's colonial elite class, into relations o continuous data extraction over which they have little i any control. They do so by telling them that this is a good deal, by the standards o the deals that capitalism has introduced into our lives. They tell them that this is the only way to assure convenient living, ecient living, connected living, enriched living.

In this way, the ruling narratives o data colonialism achieve something that the data grab alone cannot do: they capture what we might call the territory o society's imagination. They colonise our ideas o what the uture o society and technology, o community and connection, might look like, crowding out alternative ideas. The result is that only one language to describe what's going on seems available: the language o connection and community ofered by the likes o Meta and the manuacturers o smart devices. Connect, or else. Accept our terms and conditions, or else. Either, as Angela ound, accept 'progress', or leave the game. And, in capturing our imagination, data's civilising narratives perorm exactly the role that other narratives did or historical colonialism: they make it seem normal and unalterable, part o the natural order o things. Since we are only with great diculty beginning to ully challenge and dismantle the impact o colonialism's historic narratives now – hal a century or more ater the collapse o its political institutions80 – we should never underestimate the power o such narratives, including the new ones emerging around data.

The civilising narratives we have unpacked in this chapter are spreading everywhere where Big Tech is attempting to reorganise daily lie. They are not just ideas. They are deeply practical ways o

#### Data Grab organising resources and people that are acquiring the orce o habit. We may live with these ideas in the short term, but they lock us in or the long term, shaping our understandings o the way things naturally are, and the way things always seem to have been. We need to start challenging data colonialism's civilising myths right now. Resistance will be a long haul, but as we'll see at the end o the book, there are many starting points.

Beore we get to resistance, however, we need to explore one fnal aspect o the emerging landscape o data colonialism: the people and institutions who are its masters and implementers.

#### Cate 4

## THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

The authorities were already waiting or them, and took them to a camp. His phone was conscated and returned only ater they had downloaded and analysed all his data.

To get his meals, he had to submit to an iris scan. Some charitable organisation gave him a debit card he could use to buy some essentials, but he wondered i they were tracking his purchases. Just in case, and because he was embarrassed, he paid or some medicines he needed in cash, so they wouldn't nd out about his ailments. He had also been told that the card would stop working outside the camp, so it elt like a leash to keep him there. Speaking o leashes, Khaled had heard about the ankle bracelets being used in the United States to track migrants 24/7 while immigration ocials decided their ate. At least he didn't have to subject himsel to such a loss o dignity, he thought.

One day, he got a Whats App message ofering, or a price, to help him write an asylum application. The ad said that because the applications are reviewed by computers rst, it was crucial to use particular words and phrases that the computer would recognise. Unortunately, he didn't have the money needed or such an application. During his time at the camp, Khaled was grateul to have access to the ree technologies ofered by Big Tech to communicate with his amily back home, and to access important inormation about his journey.

Maybe, he thought, he would be able to work or one o these companies in Europe, even as a janitor. What Khaled didn't know was that some o those same companies had lucrative contracts with governments to build the surveillance mechanisms he encountered as a migrant. According to Big Tech Sells War.com, since 2004 US government departments such as Homeland Security, Deense and Justice – whose responsibilities include not just terrorism but migration issues – have spent at least US \$44.5 billion on contracts with companies including Amazon, Facebook, Google, Microsot and Twitter.1 Meanwhile, the EU spending on border enorcement is at

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

an all-time high, and ties between Frontex (the EU's top funded agency) and military and security industries continue to grow.

Stories such as Khaled's, which shares features with those of millions of migrants to Europe from Africa and the Middle East over the past decade, illustrate that it is not just the how, but the who of the new data grab that matter. It matters that border security has become a multi-million-dollar business, involving coders, designers and managers within large private corporations working with increasingly intrusive technologies, often supported by powerful governments. It matters that there are large networks of smaller companies – from data brokers to app developers – that contribute to the extraction and processing of data in many sectors of contemporary societies.2 And it matters that an individual sitting with his laptop at home, like Hoan Ton-That, founder of the US-based company Clearview AI, was able to harvest millions of faces from social media, link that data with other personalised data, and sell the resulting rich biometric dataset to law enforcement agencies in the US and elsewhere (more on this later).

But what matters even more is the larger pattern here, and its echoes of a colonial past. Historical colonialism was much more than a few acts of discovery and sporadic violence. It became a whole system for managing the world's economy and territory that evolved over centuries. And, of course, it involved people: a colonial class that did the detailed work of extracting value and managing lives, a class whose institutional power was largely, but not wholly, dismantled when anti-colonial struggles brought most formal empires to an end in the second half of the twentieth century.

Data's colonial class is only in its early decades, while the colonial bureaucracies of Spain or the British Empire had centuries to evolve. But a data colonial class3 exists all the same, and it comprises the entire intricate networks of companies, small and large, involved in supporting the extraction of data value from every sector of the economy, working sometimes apart from governments but often in close collaboration with them.

#### The Social Quantifcation Sector

We propose one term to describe the various institutions that make up data's colonial class: the Social Quantication Sector (SQS).4 The Social Quantication Sector is not an industry sector recognised by any labour statistics bureau, but it collects, under one umbrella, all the players involved in quantifying into data as many aspects of our lives as possible, for prot. The SQS stretches across multiple ocial areas of the economy: from energy to healthcare, from education to agriculture – all these are now increasingly dedicated to proting from our personal data. So too are myriad gig economy platforms like Uber, and many specialist companies that process data without any interface with ordinary customers.

The SQS involves a range of players across the world performing a range of roles. No single corporation can accomplish the kind of large-scale extraction that we have in mind when we talk about data colonialism. Nor can any single centre of power, led from one corner of the world, orchestrate it. Indeed, as we have emphasised throughout, data colonialism is not just about the initial extraction of data: it is about a transformed social and economic order, organised around both the collection and exploitation of data, and the power that ows from that. For this reason, many types of players are needed.

In any case, it is not necessarily the size of a company that determines its impact. At its peak, the East India Company counted on a private army of 250,000 bodies (many of them Indian recruits), all managed from London by a comparatively small sta of 35 to 159 employees (depending on the period), ruling roughly 100 million people.5 In comparison, a company like Uber has a sta of around 32,000 employees, coordinating the operations of ve million drivers (not considered employees, of course), providing 7.64 billion trips to 131 million monthly active users worldwide during 2022.6

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

One other point to bear in mind at the start is that data colonialism, like historical colonialism, is a global phenomenon, although the world o today is a very diferent place rom the world in which colonialism emerged. Any new orm o colonialism must unold within a twenty-rst-century geopolitics that is very diferent rom that o the sixteenth or eighteenth centuries, or even the twentieth century. Today's world is at least bipolar, with China's economic, technological and sot power catching up with the US and other powers like India emerging. Although many stories about Big Tech, written in and or the West, ignore China and talk only about Silicon Valley, that is a mistake we must avoid.

The origins o data's ast-expanding colonial class go back at least to the mid-1980s, when almost no one saw ully where things were heading. The rst movers were credit card companies, who sought to collect data on your every purchase and rom there create a prole o what sort o consumer you were, or at least sell that data on to marketers who could.7 Various other incentive schemes, such as shopper loyalty cards, were early attempts to gather similar data. By the early 1990s, the practice was widespread enough or a prescient Arican-American communications researcher, Oscar Gandy, to give this new development a name: the 'panoptic sort'. Corporations were acquiring an insatiable appetite or gathering data (sorting data) outside their corporate walls. They wanted to see everything (hence 'panoptic').8

Their goal was to nd smart ways o discriminating between their customers, seeking out the most loyal ones, and ofering them incentives. And once the possibility emerged o corporations gathering data about customers beyond the mere acts o sales made to them, the search or those larger domains o everyday data began. But it is not until the late 1990s that this early vision o large-scale commercial data-gathering rom society began to be reected in management theory. We know this because in 1998, one o the US's leading management gurus, Peter Drucker, could still say that 'the more inside inormation top management gets, the more it will need to balance that with outside inormation – that does not exist yet'.9 Drucker had sensed the direction o change: business, perhaps in response to ears o declining longterm consumption, was already looking or new ways to annex routine daily lie as a source o value. No one saw anything odd about gaining such control; it was just the game to be in. As one executive put it, 'it seems to me that we have squeezed all the juice out o internal inormation. Maybe it's time we ocussed on the whole world o external inormation.'10 While expressed in the bland language o management theory, this is basically the same extractive vision that drove historical colonialism's landgrab, treating data (rather than land) as a terra nullius, just there or the taking.
