Or at least potentially. The 'how' was still being worked out in the early 1990s. By the end o the decade (still years beore the rise o Big Tech), the visionary and troubled US entrepreneur Josh Harris had fgured out that the business model o the internet was based on extracting our data and then selling it back to us. Describing his experiment on surveillance, appropriately called Quiet: We Live in Public, he said: 'Everything is ree, except the video we capture o you. That we own . . . I'm going to sell you your lie back.'11

Fast orward nearly two decades, and a world where corporations routinely relied on a frehose o data rom their customers' lives had become normal, so much so that now more outlandish predictions began to be made: or example, one marketer's prediction in 2015 that 'by 2028 hal o Americans (and by 2054 nearly all Americans) will carry in their bodies device implants that communicate with retailers as they walk down the aisles'.12

This is the recent history o a deep social trend that sociologists call 'datafcation': making the whole world into a data source or business. Some commentators compare this to a great inrastructure project worthy o comparison with the French Enlightenment's Encyclopédie. Well, it's certainly big, and it's becoming the basis o some sort o social inrastructure. But it's also a landgrab, or more politely a 'productive appropriation' o what was just the ow o our everyday lives.13

Only recently has this move become publicly controversial, ollowing the so-called techlash triggered by the Cambridge Analytica scandal o 2018. But or today's management theorists, datacation remains just a sensible expansion o the inormation corporations can access, even i a proound one that some call 'a management revolution'. As the inventors o that term put it, 'each o us is now a walking data generator'.14 But they don't mention the mechanisms o data extraction that had to be in place to make it so; they don't mention the data grab. Maybe that's not surprising, since, rom the point o view o business, people always were in the way. As one corporate data expert put it, 'one o the major constraints to success with Big Data [is] the human actor'.15 The group o entities we call the SQS exists to overcome that human obstacle.

What types o corporations make up the Social Quantication Sector? Let's start with an overview o the larger corporate players that make up the core o data's new colonial class.

#### The Big Data Harvesters

The story o data extraction is commonly told in terms o our big US corporations: GAFA – Google (now Alphabet), Amazon, Facebook (now Meta) and Apple, usually with the addition o Microsot (making GAFAM).

To get a better sense o the diferent roles that GAFA members play, we can go back to the Four-X model or playing strategy video games: Explore, Expand, Exploit and Exterminate. The diferent phases o historical colonialism and the successive colonial powers that dominated them can be mapped onto these dimensions: Spain, the master o Explore, discovering the new territories that started the whole process; England, the master o Expand, steadily building an empire o linked territories that became the largest empire the world has seen; Holland, the master o Exploit, controlling global trading routes with fne precision; and, fnally, France and the US, masters o Exterminate, experts in the elimination o resistance and the systematic colonisation o the mind.

Each o these empires applied all our X's at the same time, o course, but this broad picture also helps us roughly map the X's and the colonisers o the past onto the corporations that currently constitute the leaders o data colonialism: Amazon, the Explorer o new territories o data extraction as the basis or online retail; Google and Apple, through their dominant search engine and branded hardware, respectively, Expanding vast empires o services and inrastructures; and Facebook, perhaps the most systematic Exploiter o every possibility or extracting proft rom people's time spent on its platorms. As to Exterminate, it is harder to map this more brutal dimension onto a single corporation or a single practice, but at the same time, as we have outlined, many o the practices o data colonialism do result in direct physical violence or in other orms o indirect or symbolic violence, including loss o opportunity, deteriorating wel are, or the eradication o alternative ways o thinking or being in the world.

The point is that, merely two to three decades rom its inception, data colonialism represents an evolving landscape. The way these corporations have succeeded in mastering the larger economic opportunities o data extraction deserves a closer look.

Starting in the mid-1990s, Amazon led the way in opening up the territory o e-commerce and online retail business. It now has a market capitalisation o US \$1.28 trillion.16 The company generates a large majority o its income (72 per cent) rom its online store platorm, advertising, and services to third-party sellers. 17

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

But Amazon is much more than just a store. Data rom tracking customer habits is key to its income, and Amazon has many ways o grabbing our data. In the home sector, it has built the world's leading digital personal assistant, Alexa, which is the market leader in the US (think o all the Echo devices, including Loop, Ring, Frames, Smart Oven, and the many third-party Smart devices that can be controlled through Alexa, all while capturing data about our habits).18 Amazon would love to take a look inside your phone, which is why it is piloting a program that ofers some 'lucky' users US \$2 a month to let the company monitor their mobile devices19 (you can rest assured the value that Amazon can derive rom this data is much more than a couple o dollars). Crucially also, Amazon is the world leader in the cloud computing business, with Amazon Web Services (AWS) capturing 32 per cent o the world market,20 making up 16 per cent o its total income. 21 Even the US Central Intelligence Agency (the CIA) is an AWS client, which speaks to the level o trust in its security protocols.

Google, now part o Alphabet, has a market capitalisation o US \$1.57 trillion. Since 2010, Google has consistently led the world's search engine market, with a share o 92 per cent in June 2022 across all platorms, including mobile.22 By this date, advertising comprised 81 per cent o Alphabet's income, with the bulk o it (58 per cent) coming rom advertising linked to search. But Alphabet has many other major business lines that grab data: it owns You-Tube, dominates geographical inormation (Google Maps), and has major investments in education, health, home personal assistants (Google Home), and more generally AI services. It also has a small but growing Cloud business.

Alphabet is increasingly ocussing, or the long-term, less on the data about us it can sell to advertisers and more on using its own vast stores o integrated data to ofer an ever-more personalised service to its users, what CEO Sundar Pichai has called 'each user['s] own individual Google'.23 Although some believe its main search engine and advertising business will be damaged by the revived competition rom the ChatGPT-enhanced search engine Bing that Microsot owns, this may underestimate Google's strategy o expanding its domination across multiple knowledge services basic to everyday lie.

Facebook (now part o Meta) is the member o the Social Quantication Sector most in the public eye, in part because o a series o eye-watering scandals, rom Cambridge Analytica in 2018 to the revelations o whistle-blower Frances Haugen in 2021, spotlighting how ruthlessly Facebook and the other parts o Meta (Instagram, Whats App) have been willing to exploit their privileged access to the so-called social graph o Meta's users (the mathematical patterns that capture the shape o its users' social networks). Whatever its troubles, the Facebook platorm is the world's largest social media platorm, with almost three billion users by April 2023 and a market capitalisation o US \$720 billion.24

Meta is, however, substantially less protable than Alphabet and Amazon, and its income is almost entirely dependent on advertising.25 That advertising prot took a major hit in 2022 when Apple changed i Phone's terms o operation to orce thirdparty advertisers to give its users the explicit right to opt out o being tracked, although by early 2023, there were signs Facebook was developing ways round this by utilising its own AI to produce a bespoke ad-targeting service based on its own exclusive data.26 Meta has also recently become the rst o GAFAM to ace major global competition, rom Tik Tok. And its plans to convince us all to migrate into its virtual Metaverse don't seem to be going very well, so ar.27 But it would be oolish to write of Meta just yet.

Then there's Apple, with a market capitalisation o US \$2.9 trillion. Apple is an outlier in the data colonial class, in part because o its strong public stand against generalised data collection. But this stand is grounded in its own highly distinctive business model.

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

Instead o generating predictive data about its customers, Apple makes money by locking customers into its very own data colony: Apple's universe o hardware devices whose operation and maintenance Apple exclusively controls. The strategy has paid o substantially: by early 2022, it had 62 per cent o the premium phone market (phones priced at more than US \$400).28 This generates its own guaranteed streams o revenue, which ow not just rom devices (Apple remains by ar the most proftable phone manuacturer in the world),29 but also increasingly rom what Apple calls 'services', which include the commission Apple charges to those who develop apps or the i Phone.30

Apple's recent use o its huge territorial power in the smartphone market just mentioned has laid the ground or Apple's own advertising business to grow unimpeded – based on the data Apple exclusively collects on its phone users, as well as the exclusive content that Apple produces, such as Apple TV and indeed its App Store!31 That data is used, or example, to target ads, and to recommend apps and games on the Apple Store, just as Google does on its Play Store. This has even led to a lawsuit, claiming that the company collects data without users' awareness, even when they select the 'do not track' options on their i Phones.32 Apple's relations with China are also notable. Not only are 95 per cent o i Phones still produced in China, but Apple is also, currently, the most proftable Big Tech company in China.33

#### China's Parallel Data Empire

The landscape o the world's large-scale data harvesters is highly concentrated, 34 but so ar we have only considered US corporations. China has built its own parallel empire o data extraction, the so-called BATX: Baidu, Alibaba, Tencent and Xiaomi. While these companies operate mainly (but not exclusively) in Asia, the emergence o Tik Tok (owned by Byte Dance, valued at approximately US \$220 billion in March 2023)35 suggests a shit to a more direct competition between US and China, the contours o which are not yet clear, given US threats o retaliation.36

Though not so well known in the West, the members o BATX match GAFA in range o market power, at least in China. Amazon's Chinese counterpart in online retail sales is Alibaba. Recent market turbulence reduced its market capitalisation to US \$236 billion. Like Amazon, the bulk o its income comes rom consumer retail.37 Unlike Amazon, however, it long ago moved into personal nance, creating China's most popular payment app, Alipay, which 93 per cent o Chinese online buyers say they use.38 It is worth noting that Alibaba has increasingly tense relations with the Chinese government, which have afected its standing in global stock markets.

Google's Chinese counterpart Baidu is also a powerul search engine or the Chinese market, launched in 2000. Like Alphabet, it has a large investment in AI, but it has also branched out into ridehailing services, launching a driverless ride-hailing service in mid-2022.39 It also has its own Tiangong platorm or smart IoT and the DuerOS voice assistant or the Chinese market.40 Its market capitalisation, however, is much lower than Alphabet's (US \$50.8 billion).

Facebook's closest counterpart in China is Tencent, which in 2011 developed the super-app We Chat discussed in Chapter Two. Tencent (market capitalisation o US \$444 billion) is certainly o comparable size to Meta. But like Alibaba, Tencent moved into personal nance early, and its payment app (We Chat Pay) is almost as popular among Chinese customers as Alipay.41 Tencent is much less dependent on advertising income than Meta.42 It is also now a key portal to access government services, which Facebook is not. Unsurprisingly, Tencent's relations with the Chinese government are also becoming more troubled, which has begun to afect its market capitalisation too.43

Meanwhile Apple has something o a counterpart in Xiaomi, although it is the least known o the BATX and very much smaller

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

in terms o market capitalisation (US \$35.7 billion). Yet it is the world's second largest shipper o smartphones ater Samsung.44 It is also a leading player in China's Smart Home device market, paralleling Amazon, and has a popular health-tracker called Mi Band that orms part o its Mi Fit platorm.

The inuence o Chinese Big Tech extends beyond the platorms that operate in China to its involvement in digital inrastructure building, or example across much o Arica. In this context, it's worth mentioning the little-known Chinese phone hardware company Transsion (market capitalisation US \$15.7 billion as o June 202345). Transsion is the largest phone supplier to the Arican continent. With production across multiple sites (India and various Arican countries) it has specialised in phones whose hardware, with multiple pre-set apps, does not require huge memory, and so ts better with the technology access issues that aect Arica. Transsion is becoming an increasingly important player in 'the datacation o everyday lie' in Arica, operating under the radar o the US or even the Chinese big players.46

#### The New Colonial Vision Distilled

Each corporation in the Social Quantication Sector has a particular vision o why the data grab makes sense or them and or the world. Consider Microsot, an oten-neglected player in the recent story o Big Tech, even though it has a market capitalisation o US \$2.5 trillion. Close to 1.5 billion people and businesses use Microsot's word-processing products and services worldwide,47 even i it is much harder to put an exact number on the hundreds o millions who use its Ofce 365 product. It is dominant in business networking, having bought Linked In in 2016 and the sotware developer platorm Git Hub in 2018. The company also plays a major role in the data inrastructure behind the most advanced techniques or personalised marketing through its ownership o Xandr, a leading data solutions provider or the marketing industry. And it is a major rival to Amazon in cloud services (with 23 per cent o the global market or cloud inrastructure),48 playing an important role in health data and Ed Tech. As one o the key investors in Open AI, the developer o ChatGPT, Microsot has recently been in a strong position to exploit AGI to augment its own search engine, Bing.

The company's current chie executive, Satya Nadella, is one o the most eloquent representatives o data's new colonial class. He is not, however, the standard aggressive corporate type. Nadella is a sel-refexive product o earlier generations' critiques o capitalism (he talks openly about his Marxist parents in India). And his management philosophy does sound inclusive, judging at least by his 2017 book Hit Refresh.

Nadella wanted rom early on to produce 'sotware that would change the world'. He started in a background role, working on the technical inrastructure o Microsot's cloud system. From his perspective, a fexible cloud that supports business's growing needs or data processing is a way o 'democratizing and personalizing technology'. 49

But the core o Nadella's vision should certainly make us pause. That vision is to create a world where data capture happens seamlessly in the background without obstruction. Inspired by novelist Neal Stephenson's concept o the Metaverse, Nadella talks o 'replacing reality' 'with a digital imitation'. These ideas, he adds, 'are now within sight'. Microsot's detailed ambitions or the Metaverse have recently been announced, and they rival Meta's in scope: those ambitions have driven its recent expansion in the games market (the controversial acquisition o game developer Activision Blizzard or almost US \$69 billion).

But perhaps it is in Nadella's description o Microsot's littleloved digital personal assistant Cortana that his thinking comes across most clearly. Cortana, he writes, is 'an agent that knows you deeply. It will know your context, your amily, your work. It will also know the world. It will be unbounded. And it will get smarter the more it is used. It will learn rom its interactions with all o your apps as well as rom your documents and emails.' That's a vision o total surveillance, unless you believe that the data gathered stays entirely under your control, which does rather stretch our credulity. And even i you do, what happens when similar business models are applied by other corporations?

#### The Wider Colonial Class

In data colonialism, each platorm or app is a potential data territory where, under the developer's sole sovereignty, data o certain sorts can be gathered, actions o certain types can be induced, and relevant predictions about the user can be made. The resulting inormation and profling can be held or the beneft o the developer, or sold or others to use. The only catch is that the platorm or app must fnd users to generate the data, or which they depend on app stores controlled by Apple, Google Samsung, or other SQS big players (these companies extract a substantial cut rom any sales made through their stores).

There is no limit to the number o platorms and apps that can be built on top o our online activities. It is getting hard to do anything in lie – buy a ticket, fnd somewhere to stay, fnd a recipe – without using a platorm or app. They are becoming an inrastructure or daily lie. Businesses can go on adding endless layers to this inrastructure, as long as people can be induced to use them.50 In principle, there is no limit to the extraction opportunities because, as explained in Chapter Two, data territories have no limits in physical space.

True, not all apps extract data rom us or their own beneft. We certainly hope our local bus and train apps don't, since they relay to us inormation about the public service we already pay or, although perhaps we should know better.51 The deault position is to extract data rom whatever customers do, no matter how trivial.

Needless to say, large numbers o coders, designers, managers and marketers are required to keep the system going. Each is a member o data's colonial class, the network o people who sustain and expand today's data grab. The result is an emerging bureaucracy that, in time, may parallel the bureaucracy o colonial administration that ran modern empires. Yes, given the market ownership o all the technologies that implement data colonialism, we would expect this administrative power to be more distributed than the bureaucracies o historical empires which – as or example in British East Arica – had a very close association with state power. But the core unction, to manage economic opportunities and control populations so as to ensure the optimisation o those opportunities, is basically the same.

#### The Data Scientist and Today's New Colonial Mission

All o this shows that there is obviously a human side to data colonialism, just as there was to historical colonialism: individuals attracted by the uncertain but maybe boundless riches to be gained rom new territories, individuals working together while believing in the same civilising narratives. Management theorists leading the Big Data revolution are more than happy to talk up the excitement o this work, calling the data scientist 'the sexiest job o the twenty rst century', or as another data scientist said: 'the uture belongs to the data scientist'.52

In one way, such claims cannot ail to be right. The controller o a data territory really does rule the uture o data extraction in that space. And i that space is increasingly interwoven with our daily lives, or with the inormation fows o a whole corporation, the uture-managing power o the data scientist can only grow. As one o them put it: 'When the company sees what can be created with data . . . you'll see data products appearing everywhere. That's how you know when you've won.'53 The horizon o data extraction stretches out like the territory that colonial administrators once surveyed: there to be captured and dominated.

This vision o control through data – through expanding territories o data extraction – is intoxicating. It is uelling a vision o

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

extended data grabs by 'intelligent corporations'54 that seek to control not just individual supply chains, but entire production and consumption cycles. International NGOs like the World Bank and the United Nations, ar rom oering values that challenge the data grab, have been enthusiastic partners in this. Their own development projects routinely impose on Global South populations heavy obligations to generate data. These are the same populations whose economic and social disadvantage were what 'development' was meant to alleviate in the rst place. But that always seems to be the case: some pay or 'development' more than others. I data power is a way o gaining social power through holding inormation, then it is also a new way or organisations based in the Global North to expand their power in the Global South.55

This global inequality is refected also, as in historical colonialism's governing class, in the power relations that shape who is part o the new Big Tech elites. Although data science oers a whole new technique o management power or data's colonial class, the opportunities to join that class are not equally distributed, any more than they were to join historical colonialism's bureaucracy. I someone is white, their chances o entering data's colonial class are much higher than i they are not: just 7 per cent and 8 per cent o jobs in US high-tech companies are held by Black and Latinx workers respectively, almost twice as bad as the representation in US industry generally.56 I someone is male, their chances are also much higher: in 2021 only 29 per cent o employees in tech companies worldwide were women.57 The emergence o data science as a pro ession and the costs required to train a data scientist (economic costs to the economy, and nancial costs privately borne by individuals and amilies) have ensured that the opportunities to enter data's new colonial class are not open to all. And this is not just a matter o the general inequalities that shape Western societies' distribution o work. In part, this is also because o the continuities between today's data sciences and the history o colonial science itsel that we discussed in Chapter One. Whatever the cause, the 'uture' that supposedly belongs to the data scientist is, to paraphrase William Gibson, not evenly distributed.

#### Who's Watching When You Drive?

As we've suggested, data extraction is not just something happening in social media or even retail marketing, but across all areas o lie where a computer has been inserted. As a result, the SQS is much larger than might frst appear.

To illustrate this, take car-driving and the insurance that goes with it, or many people a basic aspect o everyday lie, which is being transormed into a data territory (we'll stick to cars we drive; sel-driven cars are data territories rom the start).

Any new car today is likely to be Smart, that is, data-extracting. Cars have essentially become computers on wheels, and those computers collect a lot o data (it's very hard to buy a new car today that doesn't transmit data). We might appreciate the convenience o car systems that can help us drive or even drive on our behal, protecting us rom potential accidents by detecting threats and reacting to them aster than we can, or even just reminding us that we are due or an oil change. But cars these days collect – and more importantly, report – lots o inormation about us: how many passengers are travelling, whether their seatbelts are activated, whether the doors are unlocked, what the cabin temperature is set at and whether the windows are up or down, and even what music or entertainment programming we are listening to. Smart cars also record extensive trip data: location, speed, destination, when the brakes are applied and when the car accelerates, whether the headlights are on, and so on. All o this inormation may say something about us as drivers and people, and all o this inormation is transmitted wirelessly not to us or our edifcation, but to business.

As a result, cars are not just a mode o transport, but a large data market in their own right, which analysts predict will be worth

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

anything between US \$300 and \$800 billion by the end o the decade. Data extraction in the car sector involves basically three types o corporate players who manage the data territory that cars now represent.58

First, there are the data harvesters, the car manuacturers who install devices or extracting continuous data into the computerbased systems that operate the car. Those systems 'capture' data like any other computer has or the past three decades. In the industry, carmakers are known as OEMs (original equipment manuacturers).

Data is now the ocus o a number o economic alliances between OEMs and third parties. There are suppliers o navigation systems and internet-enabled inotainment systems such as Apple Car Play and Android Auto, and, i the car has a built-in phone connection, telecommunications operators. Then there are the specialist manuacturers who make installable tracking devices to collect even more data, the so-called 'atermarket' data gathered when, ater the car is sold, the owner agrees to have an additional data-tracking device installed. The Electronic Logging Devices (ELD) or trucks discussed in Chapter Two are an example o an 'atermarket' device. Finally, there are the Big Tech suppliers o voice-activated search unctions: yes, you guessed it – Google, Amazon and Apple. Their data extraction is so signicant that one car data specialist has called the car 'a browser's last rontier'.59

What happens to all this data? That's where a second member o the new colonial class steps in: the data aggregator. A whole specialist sector now exists to process the mountains o data generated by our cars, other than that already siphoned of by Big Tech providers. In the car industry these specialist data aggregators are called 'vehicle data hubs'.

Big players such as Lexis Nexis (based in the US) and Otonomo (based in Israel) are in this market. They perorm a role which has equivalents in many other sectors, such as, in marketing, large companies like Experian, who aggregate multiple sources o data and process it into a usable orm. In the background, Alphabet is playing an increasingly important role in setting the standards or smoother data extraction in the car sector.0 Much o the data gets anonymised or broader industry analysis, where individual identities are irrelevant. Similarly in the car sector, vehicle data hubs can generate anonymised, aggregated data or general use. But i the individual inputs to such larger datasets were ever combined with other data so as to identiy an individual, there are third parties interested in buying such data or sure (or example, advertisers might want to know your preerred cabin temperature beore they try to sell you a hot or cold beverage).

But data hubs also interace with a crucial third player in the car-data market who absolutely does not want anonymised data. This is the insurer. Insurance contracts are increasingly being turned inside out through data processing. The new 'usage-based' policies (sometimes called 'connected' policies) no longer protect us against one-o unknowable risks which might aect anyone at some point during their driving career. They are products tailored or particular policy holders and continually refned in the light o the data that is gathered directly rom a particular driver's car. Instead o having to spread general risks across a pool o drivers, insurers now have the option to take on much more narrowly defned risks tailored to precisely the sort o driver they observe each o us to be.1

Insurance contracts are now, in other words, surveillance contracts, although that is not how they are marketed. A blog by The Floow, a company acquired by vehicle data hub Otonomo to run such a policy, is quite explicit. It describes how it installs a device in the car to 'monitor [drivers'] habits and behaviors'. Note the word 'habits', which suggests there's plenty o larger-scale analysis done over and above the moment-to-moment tracking.2 It is not or nothing that one such policy, marketed by Progressive Insurance in the US, is named Snapshot.

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

The colonial dimensions o this new type o car insurance become even clearer when we look at how the same business opportunity plays out in the Global South. 'Personalised' car insurance is also proving attractive to insurance companies in Latin America, Arica and South-East Asia. Why? Because, as a group o Swiss political scientists recently ound, it provides a great new way or insurers to expand their clientele to populations whose risk actors, in insurers' perceptions at least, might otherwise be unattractive. This is accomplished by expanding the range o risk-relevant data insurers extract, not just rom the car (like GPS location data) but also rom the driver's personal details and social media accounts.

The researchers interviewed insurance proessionals and ound plenty o evidence o seemingly bland categories, like 'personal address', being used to hide implicit judgements about, or example, race. Your address, ater all, is rarely unconnected to your race, no matter where you live. Given that, it's hard to have condence that other categories such as 'likely to ail' will be used without subtle reerence to racial actors. What we can see here, in a particular racialised orm, is how the data grab is opening new types o markets where service providers no longer have to socialise risks, distributing them across a wide population. As the CEO o a Kenyan-based insurance platorm put it: 'we thought that it's not air to charge everyone the same 5 per cent because some people are better drivers than others. So now our regression model comes up with this percentage' (translation: i the data extracted by the company suggests you are likely to be a bad driver, the algorithm will also calculate how much more you will pay).63

The jury is still out on whether this trend in car data extraction will go on expanding, at least in the Global North. Some experts think car manuacturers, picking up on drivers' squeamishness, will start resisting insurers' claims or a complete data stream, with similar tensions emerging between carmakers and Big Tech corporations like Google.64 But, i you can aford a Tesla car and are interested in Tesla's insurance ofer, you won't have a choice. Tesla cars extract large amounts o data as they are driven,6 and the only car insurance Tesla oers is tailored to the data extracted about owners and their cars. Indeed, such data, rom an industry perspective, enables a new sort o 'value-added service': Tesla doesn't just collect data about you; it's happy to sell you back your data, or an additional ee, as interesting insights!66

Economists tend to see this new orm o data extraction as just what's needed to correct the inormation defcits that have held back the car insurance market until now.67 But that is to miss the wider extractivist transormation that is under way.

#### Serving the Algorithmic State

We have covered the range o commercial actors involved in the Social Quantifcation Sector. But where does the state and national government ft into this? There are probably just as many areas o data extractivism in the public sector as there are in the private sector. Indeed, the two are becoming increasingly intertwined.

It is generally assumed that the government must store some data about us (securely, we hope). But when the state uses data to target specifc populations without transparency or accountability, things get a lot messier. Consider the region o Catalonia, in northern Spain, where it has been revealed that the government maintains an extensive 'preventive' techno-social system to monitor the Muslim population. A ew misinterpreted Facebook posts, along with signs o involvement in anti-Islamophobic groups, can be enough to deny a citizenship application and expel the applicant. And while the system relies on more than just digital methods o surveillance, there is no question that technology plays a big part in the Catalan Protocol or Prevention, Detection and Intervention in Processes o Violent Extremism (PRODERAE in Spanish).68

This kind o surveillance extends to a nation's borders. Remember Khaled's story rom the beginning o this chapter? States are

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

increasingly relying on digital data to control the movement o migrants through what they call 'smart borders'. Unsurprisingly, the datacation o the border is not resulting in more humane treatment o migrants. According to United Nations High Commissioner on Reugees, at the end o 2022 there were 110 million reugees in the world eeing poverty, violence, human rights abuses or environmental destruction.69 For many o them, the attempt to cross a border entails an encounter with a multitude o technologies or extracting data, as we saw with Khaled.

Smart Borders – we came across that word 'Smart' in Chapter Three with the Smart Home, but this usage is unquestionably more sinister – are oten more lethal than 'low-tech' borders. By increasing the pervasiveness o surveillance, they push migrants to attempt crossings at more dangerous (but not as highly monitored) locations, which has resulted in higher death rates.70 In addition, Smart Borders are more prone to mismanagement, because the tools that make them smart are generally produced by the private sector, making regulation and accountability more dicult.

Many technologies are involved in Smart Borders: automated systems to review visa applications; autonomous surveillance technologies such as drones to monitor air, sea and land; intelligent video systems that can track and identiy humans; extensive biometrics (including acial recognition, ngerprints and iris scans) to control migrants at border crossings; the use o ankle bracelets or other location-tracking devices; and an extensive ramework or extracting, analysing and cross-reerencing data rom government records, medical and education records, phone location data, social media, and other sources o personal inormation.

Behind these vast eforts overseen by states are many SQS companies, including household names like Amazon and Microsot, whose cloud services are relied upon to store and process the data extracted. But the sector also involves other less well-known corporations who play a crucial role in processing data at the largest scale, such as Palantir, Salesorce and Lexis Nexis. These companies receive millions o dollars in government contracts, in complex arrangements, which leave it uncertain whether data collected or one purpose then gets used or another.

The colonial echoes o smart borders are striking. Data technologies o some sort have always played a role in the control o colonial borders. Indeed, that was why many techniques – rom passports to fngerprints to immunisation cards – were invented. And while it is not easy or some humans to bridge national divides, it is easy or technologies to do so; solutions applied at the border to track and surveil populations eventually fnd their way to the rest o the country, or to other countries. Think o the multiple uses o acial recognition technology or everyday policing in autocracies like Iran, where they were re-deployed to monitor women's acecoverings in September 2022.71 Borders have always been places where the nation-state's authority is violently enorced, within the wider logics o colonialism. The connected power and unlimited scope o today's technologies o data extraction have deepened the asymmetries involved.

#### Palantir's Data Territory

Controversial Silicon Valley entrepreneur Peter Thiel once remarked: 'My generation was promised colonies on the moon: instead we got Facebook'.72 Although Thiel was an early investor in Facebook, he soon ounded his own operation. We don't mean the payment plat orm Pay Pal (which he also ounded),73 but rather his role in setting up Palantir Technologies, a company which has a major role in supporting the contemporary state's border operations. This will be our case study o how data's colonial class is serving the state's wider goals o data management and territorial management.

Palantir is a US company quoted on the New York Stock Exchange (market capitalisation, US \$34.5 billion). It was ounded in 2003 and remains one o the most secretive Big Tech companies.

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

It operates a bit like the data aggregators we ound in the car sector or marketing: it doesn't collect data itsel but provides crucial data integration services to a range o external clients. Palantir's clients are mainly in the security, deence and intelligence sectors, but the company also provides services to corporations and organisations in healthcare, drug saety, and other areas. Its role in providing the inrastructure o the UK National Health Service's data processing has become increasingly controversial.74 But it is in relation to core government services that Palantir's greatest importance lies.

Governments gather lots o data when people cross borders. They also have access, directly or through their own indirect powers, to vast amounts o other data. But how to link these data sources and make sense o them? And how to do this systematically, continuously and in real time, to target an emerging threat supposedly posed by an individual crossing a border right now? This is where Palantir's services come in. Palantir does a lot more than aggregate heaps o data into larger datasets. Without getting too technical – and the details are very technical – Palantir works on three interconnected levels o increasing signifcance. To get a sense o how they work, let's imagine a government client or whom Palantir is consulting.

First, Palantir organises the multiple messy datasets that the government may have relevant to an area. In other words, it gets one database to talk to another database (connecting the Student and Exchange Visitor Inormation System to the Alien Criminal Response Inormation Management System, or instance). Government itsel may have no idea how to integrate them; indeed, they may think it impossible to get those datasets to talk to each other. But Palantir tidies up the inconsistencies, labels everything systematically, and generally turns that pile o zeros and ones into one very large and unctioning dataset. Incidentally that is exactly the advantage that some National Health Service (NHS) managers in the UK see in Palantir's core sotware, Foundry.75

Then Palantir goes deeper, looking or the underlying patterns that may be hidden in that vast terrain o the now organised data. Techies call this an 'ontology'. A more amiliar term or what Palantir creates at this stage is 'metadata', generating ever more subtle ways o labelling and ordering data to reveal deeper patterns across billions o data points.

The third level is where the real action is: that's when the deep patterns Palantir has discovered get mobilised to generate predictions, revealing this or that complex data pattern to be associated at high probability with a specic future ofence or risk. Armed with this prediction, Palantir supports the government to take preemptive action in individual cases, cutting of a person or a line o activity beore that predicted outcome ever materialises.

In this way, Palantir has become an active part o how governments actually govern. Much more than background support or the undeniably complex tasks o contemporary government, Palantir's systems enable decisions to be generated rom vast unstructured domains o data: data rom which decisions could never otherwise have been generated. Palantir nds patterns across multiple vast databases that have never beore talked to each other. And it creates new targets ('data objects' is the technical term) that have never beore been identied as targets; they only emerge as objects rom Palantir's multi-layered processing. Once such targets have been isolated, decision-making and planning becomes reorganised around them.76

For example, Palantir provided a tool to the US agency or Immigration and Customs Enorcement (ICE) that made it possible, across multiple databases, to identiy undocumented individuals relevant to potential investigations. The result was to target, arrest and deport amily members o children who crossed the US border alone and in need o sponsors, efectively preventing these children rom joining their relatives in the US. While Palantir claimed its

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

sotware was never used in deportation cases, at least 443 individuals were deported by ICE as a result o this particular operation.77

Palantir literally reconstructs the world on which government acts. In technical terms, according to one US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) ling, it ulls its clients' needs or 'generalizable platorms or modelling the world and making decisions'. But these are not one-o models that, once used, are put to one side. They are an emerging and permanent new inrastructure o government decision-making. Where? In the US certainly, but Palantir's activities in Europe have proved harder to track down, although it is known to be active in relation to security services in Denmark, France, the Netherlands and UK.78 Major crises are Palantir's riend: it advised the Greek and Dutch governments during the Covid-19 pandemic and the UK government in handling the Ukraine reugee crisis.79 While Palantir might blandly label this 'Data as a Service', echoing the better-known business product o 'Sotware as a Service', its real ambition was more clearly expressed in its SEC ling when it said it aimed to 'become the deault operating system or data across the US government'.

A recent exhaustive study o the language o Palantir's registered patents uncovered something even more concerning. An ambition to change what counts as knowledge or those in power, but also, in time, or those on whom that power is imposed. As the study's authors put it: 'Palantir imagines a world where its platorm might serve as a "shadow" universal knowledge graph or governments, industries, and organizations'. In other words, Palantir holds up a mirror to the social world and persuades powerul organisations and national governments to recognise their territory in it,80 a mirror that didn't exist beore Palantir came on the scene.

That mission is refected in the details (only starting to emerge as we write) about the role that Palantir is playing in supporting the Ukrainian army against Russian invaders. By bringing together data rom commercial satellites and secret intelligence sources, combined with on-the-ground sensors, Palantir and the Ukrainian army have built a system that allows commanders to remove the lack o situational awareness commonly known as the 'og-o-war'. This system is all the more powerul because the data can be processed by powerul AI agents halway around the world, and then ed back to the battlefeld in real time (using Musk's Starlink internet satellites). According to experts, the advantage this system provides is similar to the advantage nuclear weapons would give an army over another one that doesn't have them, and it is changing the way wars will be ought or decades.81

Palantir is just one prominent example o how the twin logics o corporate and governmental data extraction work together in the era o data colonialism. The upper echelons o the data colonial class collaborate to transorm what counts as 'actionable' knowledge in our societies. For the Ukrainians, this knowledge appears to be giving them an edge over their enemies (although we might worry about Palantir selling their technology to an army we don't support).82 For Khaled, the consequences o this knowledge are elt in the constraints under which he has to live.

At this point, barely twenty years into Palantir's history, we cannot tell where this will lead. It took the East India Company a century and a hal ater its ounding in 1599 beore it became the military quasi-state that we now remember.83 We should keep in mind where Palantir's corporate name came rom: 'Palantirs' were the all-seeing stones in Tolkien's Lord of the Rings that saw into events, both past and uture. The question is still: who has control over such power, and to what ends?

#### The Emerging Global Data State?

Palantir is only a spectacular version o a much broader transormation. Right across the global economy, Big Tech companies such as Microsot and Google are oering themselves as the inormation

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

inrastructure or business and governments. Without such largescale services or integrating data and storing it in the cloud, they claim, businesses would be unable to manage their relations with the world (Palantir itsel uses Google Cloud).

Far rom being a recent development, such links are part o a much longer, and largely hidden, history o state-corporate collaboration. In the US, Alphabet (Google's owner) rom early on benetted rom close links with the US Department o Deense and, as economist Mariana Mazzucato has shown, even the amously independent Apple relied on research done by government-unded scientists or the technical innovations that underlay the i Pod, i Phone and i Pad. Meanwhile, it is well known that it was DARPA (the research arm o the Department o Deense) whose unding started the internet itsel. 84 No surprise thereore that in December 2022, Alphabet was selected by the Pentagon to deliver a US \$9 billion cloud computing contract, alongside Amazon, Microsot and Oracle.85 And no surprise that DARPA unded the development o voice-activated interaces, which is the core innovation o the voice intelligence industry.86

Against this background, commentators in the West have no right to be surprised, in turn, that the Chinese government strongly supported its key platorms in their early years.87 Recent confict between the Chinese government and platorms such as Alibaba, Tencent and ride-hailing platorm Didi (which in June 2021 was pulled rom China's app store days beore its listing on the New York Stock Exchange) has demonstrated, in reverse, the signicance o the early political backing necessary to gather and process data in a society such as China. The Fourteenth Five-Year Plan, released in April 2021 by the Chinese Communist Party, names data or the rst time as one o the nation's core assets, suggesting that much greater state control is on its way.88

What is most striking about Big Tech's unolding in China, however, compared to the West, is the presence rom the start o a larger strategic vision that integrates 'consumer, corporate and government data' into one national asset.89 It's worth looking at China's policies or the development o its 'internet plus', Artifcial Intelligence and Social Credit System, which ortunately have been translated into English.

The policy document authorising the planning o the social credit system lays out the core o this vision. The Chinese government does not use terms like 'empowerment' or market 'reedom', so common in Silicon Valley. Instead, it talks more directly o 'a market improvement o the social and economic order'.90 Yes, order, which is undamentally a social goal, not obviously aimed at reedom. Yet the term 'order' also describes rather well many o the social transormations through data that we've unpacked in previous chapters.

At last, we see the wider logic that drives the expansion o data's seemingly disparate colonial class, wherever it is, and whether employed by corporations or governments. We are in the presence here o an extraordinary ambition: the ambition to know the world in new and enduring ways, and on that basis to exercise territorial power with an intensity not previously known in history. I corporations, as the main seizers o data territory, are the initial benefciary, we can be sure that governments will not want to be ar behind.

It is easy to be distracted by examples o authoritarian government overreach. We have seen much hand-wringing in Western media about China's Social Credit System, or about the Indian government's Aadhaar programme that requires biometric ID or even the most basic dealings with the state, resulting in what some call a 'welare-industrial complex', that is, an alliance o private corporations working with the state to extract commercial opportunities rom both welare system and conventional industrial development.91 But all governments, including democratic ones, are beneftting rom data-driven schemes across a wide range o their operations.

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

What they are benetting rom, very oten, are the seizures o data territory that corporate techniques everywhere make possible: that, ater all, was the key message o the Snowden revelations in 2013. So let's not all or the story o good corporation/bad government, let alone the idea o good democratic government using tech in a good way/bad authoritarian government using tech in a bad way. The very nature o government is being transormed everywhere by the new orm o control and knowledge that data territories make possible through a 'public-private surveillance partnership', in which old governing classes work closely with data's new colonial class to re-order power relations.92

There is an important continuity here with the histories o both colonialism and industrial capitalism. Government has always depended on the gathering o inormation about whatever territory it claims to govern, but the state's ability to gather inormation has varied hugely over the centuries. Government's data-gathering accelerated most in the nineteenth century, when colonial powers honed new surveillance techniques in their colonies such as the ngerprint and, in order to manage a home population sufering rom the disruptions o early industrialisation, rened statistical techniques.

Today governments are increasingly trying to integrate the commercial techniques o data analysis and algorithmic processing into their everyday decision-making. In many cases, they rely on the inrastructure provided by Big Tech (it is reported that 6,500 US government agencies are already using Amazon's cloud services93). Latin America is one region where such processes are expanding, as governments with ew resources look or quick routes to solve intractable social problems. In Argentina, or example, the government recently signed a contract with Microsot to use AI to create a 'Technology Platorm or Social Intervention' that predicts teenage pregnancy among lower-class women. 94 Horus is another Latin American social welare project, prominent in Argentina and Brazil, that seeks to cut welare waste through more intensive proling o welare recipients, once more driven by the AI services o Big Tech companies like Microsot.95

Meanwhile, as we mentioned earlier, the government o India has built the Aadhaar identication system as the inrastructure or social welare, and much else, to which 89 per cent o the population were signed up by mid-2019.96 Its early justication was to give many poor citizens a presence in government databases, which would make it easier or them to le claims under India's vast wel are system. But it has expanded into a much more comprehensive way o underpinning India's civic and economic activity, with some similarities to China's system. From the start, it was based on biometric data and a public-private partnership, generating serious privacy and surveillance concerns.97 Data in India, as in China, is proving an eective ocus or state-driven nationalism.98

By contrast, as the example o Estonia and its X-Road project demonstrates, it is extremely rare that a national government decides to run its services on the basis o a perectly conceivable alternative: that data fows should remain under the sole control o the citizens whose data is being used.99 So ar there is no evidence that Estonia's example is being copied by governments elsewhere!

#### Data's Lone Adventurers

The story o Big Tech, when seen the right way up, is really the story o data colonialism. The main characters are usually large entities (corporations, governments). But as with historical colonialism, individual adventurers play an important role too. They too can be conquerors or conquistadors, and engage in what one writer has called 'surveillance rontierism'.100

A curious example o this occurred when in May 2022 two powerul men shook hands on a deal that they hoped could potentially bring them great benets. One was Brazil's then-president Jair

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

Bolsonaro. The other was Elon Musk, president o Tesla and SpaceX, and now owner o Twitter. The handshake, sealed in a luxurious resort in Porto Feliz, signalled an intention or Musk's Starlight satellites to bring the internet to 19,000 isolated rural schools, and to monitor the environment in the Amazon rainorests.. It is doubt ul now the agreement will go orward under the administration o the new president o Brazil, Lula da Silva, but it casts a retrospective light on the ambitions o more than one type o adventurer. For the real purpose o this proposed deal, we might surmise, wasn't doing good to schoolchildren or the environment, but data extraction, plain and simple (meanwhile, Musk's Starlink system is already being used by illegal miners in the Amazon).101

We met another adventurer earlier in the chapter: Australian Hoan Ton-That, the brains behind Clearview AI, whose acial recognition app hit the headlines in January 2020.

Recall the app's basic recipe. First, scrape billions o images rom Facebook, You Tube, Venmo – in act anywhere we are expected to show our ace – and process them with high-level AI to distinguish those aces rom each other in ever more precise ways. Then use the resulting metadata to support someone else's decision process. For example, the decision processes o an entity who wants to know whether this ace, seen partially rom an odd angle, is the same ace that identifed itsel on that Facebook post. Finally, license the recipe to law enorcement agencies in the US and elsewhere, and you have a tool that the law could only dream o. There's no evidence incidentally that Facebook, You Tube or any other platorm anticipated this use o the billions o smiling aces they host, but then there was never anything in their platorms to stop it.

Ton-That saw nothing wrong at all with what he did. As an enterprising individual who, as he put it, 'grew up on the internet', Ton-That made exactly the same move that, on a smaller scale, Mark Zuckerberg perormed as a Harvard student when he downloaded and used without permission his emale peers' portraits to create

Facebook's precursor: Face Mash.2 The lineage is clear, since neither saw this as raising moral issues. As Ton-That put it, this was just 'the best use o the technology'.3 And yet, Ton-That (and Zuckerberg) were also making the classic colonial move: grab everything because it is just there, ready or the taking.

Let's put to one side the evidence uncovered by the Hufngton Post o overlaps, to put it politely, between Clearview AI's sta – indeed Ton-That's own social networks – and ar-right activists in the US.4 And let's put to one side, or a moment at least, the shocking implications o Ton-That's techniques or human reedom. Focus instead on a more basic point, that Ton-That's techniques are no more than an eective application o the core techniques o data colonialism: rst, capture a data territory, then rene it and generate new data ruits that you completely control, and nally sell access without concern or how this data might be used. Or in other words: Explore, Expand, Exploit and Exterminate.

In act, one o Palantir's patents describes something rather similar: a ace photo on a smartphone is sent to a server that recognises it by reerence to a huge database and then automatically links it to all the social media data associated with that ace and its owner. Because o Palantir's secrecy, we just can't know i it does anything like that, but we do know that Peter Thiel nanced Clearview's early operations to the tune o US \$2,, later converted into equity.5

There is absolutely no evidence, however, o Palantir's urther involvement with Ton-That's enterprise. Why would there need to be? The idea had its own momentum.

Clearview oered 3-day ree trials o its tool to police orces. Police quickly ound that Clearview's acial recognition tool drew on a much larger database and had a much more fexible algorithm than those supplied by, say, the state o Florida. Ater all, Ton-That had leveraged the vast data territory o Facebook and You Tube, to name a ew. In any case, Clearview never had to know its tool was

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

accurate, only that it produced results that were credible bases or law enorcement decisions. Clearview's own best claim in 2020 was that it ound matches at least 75 per cent o the time.106 No matter: that was enough to launch Clearview's business to global prominence.

Clearview's unauthorised plundering (we might call it 'pirating') o some o data colonialism's booty generated a panic in some quarters. Legal actions against its data sales to the private sector were taken (with nes imposed by a Greek regulator and the UK Inormation Commissioner's Oce), ollowed by an outright ban in the US state o Illinois. But that was negative reaction to its 2020 business. When we checked in again on Clearview over two years later, it had rebranded itsel with an impressive website, ull o reassuring headlines: 'Advancing Public Saety', 'Securing People Facilities and Commerce'. It claimed to be ranked number one or identiying aces in the 'US and the Western World', with an expanded database (20 billion+ aces) and improved accuracy (99 per cent+) 'or all demographics'. It boasted Ukraine's armed orces among its satised clients and a slew o positive media coverage. Ton-That's redemption rom colonial raider to security provider appeared complete.107

But the transition rom lone adventurer to a key player in military operations is no more accidental in the age o Big Data than it was when the Americas or India were captured. Yes, the geopolitical context is today very diferent. We are in the middle o an emerging new Cold War between the West and China and Russia. But what matters most is territorial expansion.

Sometimes, data colonialism's lone adventurers seem benign. Take Alex 'Sandy' Pentland, an MIT Proessor who in 2011 was named one o the world's seven most powerul data scientists by Tim O'Reilly, himsel the creator o the term 'Web 2.0'.

Pentland does not just do data science. He has developed a whole new version o social science to make sense o the sorts o data analysis he does. In his 05 book Social Physics, Pentland proposes to replace existing social science models with the search or 'statistical regularities within human movement and communication' that can generate 'a computational theory o behaviour'.08 The source is personal data, the 'digital breadcrumbs' each o us leave as we pass through the world. The result is basically network analysis: fnding patterns in our social interactions based in the structure o our social networks and the people we interact with.

The goal o all this data crunching? To develop a new sort o social knowledge, built rom a macro-understanding o how social networks operate and replicate. Pentland has applied this approach to work organisations: he developed a so-called 'sociometric badge' or measuring the 'culture' o organisations through their networks o human interactions, an idea taken up by Mc Donald's among others. But he has also applied it to social interactions more generally, and even to whole countries (his 'Data or Development' program collected mobile and demographic data or the whole o the Ivory Coast).09 Among Pentland's companies are Endor.com, a blockchain-based platorm that claims to make AI predictions widely accessible (the sorts o predictive modelling that many businesses now want),0 and Cogito, a leader in the AI-driven inerences that can be made rom people's voices. Although in recent work Pentland has ocussed on how data capital can be placed at the service o communities, it remains unclear whether Pentland is willing to question the imperative to extract data rom everyone and everything that lies at the heart o the new data science.

Whatever its arguable benefts, Pentland's entrepreneurship illustrates even more clearly than Ton-That's the emerging shape o a new science or the data colonial era. Just as botany and zoology once emerged in the course o colonising nations learning how to rule their new lands, so today we have 'social physics', the science that claims to map the phenomena o the data colony's vast archipelago.

#### We the Consumers

The geography o those responsible or colonialism has always been complicated. Ater all, the home populations o colonising nations such as Britain had a large stake in colonialism, even though they may have elt themselves ar rom the realities o what empire did in its 'distant' colonies. Colonialism uelled massive economic growth back home, as recent campaigns or more awareness o Britain's massive and long-continuing economic reliance on the slave trade have reminded us.113 Today, there is still exploitation at a distance linked to data colonialism: workers rom the Global South who do the underpaid manual labour o preparing materials to train AI models, or the labour o extracting the minerals needed or our electronic devices. But the geography appears very dierent rom that o historical colonialism, because its general target is everywhere: human lie (assuming it enters, willingly or not, into data relations).

How data colonialism aects people depends on where they are, their social status, what sort o work they do, and how each is linked to the longer histories o power, especially colonialism. Business elites and other elites the world over may be irked to hear that their photo was in a Clearview database and that their plat orm and browsing data are likely to be profled, but the net result o data extraction may just confrm their position among the elite. I, however, you are one o the many economically disadvantaged in the Global South or Global North, you will already know frsthand the orce o continuous data extraction and how it is used to judge you. You conront this orce when you try to get a loan, or access welare, or when your only possibilities o work depend on you submitting to the surveillance that comes with a job in the gig economy.

Beneath this complexity, there is one clear truth. That all o us, wherever we live, as users o the platorms and apps that increasingly make up daily lie, help sustain their regimes o data extraction. Our continued acquiescence to their terms o service is a key part o data colonialism's deal.

The question is oten posed to users in purely individualistic terms: my data is or is not at risk, the portability o my photos between platorms needs to be protected. This makes data colonialism seem like a trade-o that each o us can weigh up on an individual basis, or ourselves. But that's doubly misleading. First, as we noted in Chapter Three, the benefts or business come not rom us individually giving up our data, but rom the act that we all do, and rom the relations between data that ow rom this.114 Second, the transormation o power relations that is under way stems rom the act that all o us are changing our habits in parallel, so data colonialism cannot just be an individual issue. Our social relations with each other are increasingly being shaped by how data is extracted rom the lives we live together, as part o the emerging social order o data colonialism.

That said, our participation in this new social order doesn't make all o us members o data's colonial class. That status is reserved or those who exclusively beneft rom the exploitation o data territories, and membership o that class is skewed by the inequalities inherited rom historical colonialism. Not all old elites have managed to secure their place in data's ruling class (there's no evidence yet that, or example, landed elites in the US or UK are more likely to be data scientists or indeed investors in Big Tech), but they certainly helped to create an unequitable system that the new colonial classes are now exploiting, through new means.

The rest o us – the consumers o digital platorms and the workers in a datafed world – must continuously confrm our acquiescence in the order o data colonialism. We are required to be connected: downloading apps, joining platorms, keeping them updated. Data elites need us and our devices to be constantly plugged into systems o data extraction.

A recent episode rehearsed in a neat political ormat the basic steps by which, across the world, people are enlisted to participate

#### THE NEW COLONIAL CLASS

in data relations every day. In August 2022, in the build-up to India's Independence Day, supporters o the ruling Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) were encouraged by no less than its leader, Prime Minister Narendra Modi, to put up a national ag outside their house, and post a picture on a special-purpose website. The campaign was called Har Ghar Tiranga (tricolour on every house). Digital rights campaigners were concerned the intentions were not exclusively celebratory. For every picture was required to have a geotag, which in eect tied the location o the supporter's house with their phone number and any other identiying data needed to upload the photo.

Sixty million Indians did this, and received an appreciative certifcate rom the Ministry o Culture in return. Mamta, a 4-year-old teacher rom Uttar Pradesh, was one o them: 'I eel proud, as i I have won a war.' When asked, she admitted she had not thought much about the privacy aspects.6

What ideas, what imaginative resources, do we need to develop to resist the enticements oered by data's new colonial class?

#### CHAPTER 5

## **VOICES OF DEFIANCE**

THE WORST THING for Chunfeng was when customers left bad reviews. 'The delivery guy took too long and my food was cold when it arrived. Plus he was rude when I complained.' Did these people even know what traffic was like at that time? As a *zhongbao* – a gig contractor delivering food in a busy city in China – Chunfeng could have up to half of his day's wages deducted for just one such infraction. That didn't seem fair to him.

Things were better back in the days when the app was just a small start-up, run by a couple of nerdy guys with university degrees. He had quit his job at the factory, tempted by the opportunity, he was told, to set his own hours and be his own boss. Even back then, the money had never been as good as was promised, but he would tell himself he had independence, and he enjoyed driving around the city. Then the company was acquired by one of the largest food delivery apps in China, and things went downhill immediately. Platform fees were always rising, with more and more of the money going to the company (including a portion of Chunfeng's occasional tips). Didn't the owners or the government realise food and gas prices were going up, not down? How was he supposed to support his family?

And yet, he couldn't bring himself to quit. He had worked very hard for that 4.5-star rating. If he left to work for the competition (where things were not necessarily better), he would have to start rom zero. So it all seemed rather hopeless, not just or him, but or everyone. A group o drivers had started to meet in person to discuss what to do (they avoided as much as possible chatting about it on We Chat, because they believed algorithms monitored conversations or this kind o talk). What angered them most was to be told that this couldn't be helped, because it was 'The Algorithm' setting the prices. Well, who tells the algorithm what to do? Surely, as he pointed out at a meeting, the algorithm can be adjusted to benet the bosses or to benet the workers. There was a lot o anger at the meetings. Things were getting ugly. A driver had set himsel on re in another city to protest unpaid wages.1 In Indonesia, they heard about some delivery drivers who literally tried to sew their lips shut, in protest against low wages and poor work conditions. Yes, there had been a couple o attempts to protest in his city, but it was hard to get drivers to show up, when it meant losing even more wages. Plus, the police were quite good at dispersing small groups quickly with arrests or beatings.

Then Chuneng had an idea: let's use the app against them! On the day o the next protest, at the appointed time, everyone would order something cheap through the app and have it delivered to the main square, where the protest would take place. Hundreds o drivers would converge at the same time. Let's see what the police and the algorithm will do then, thought Chuneng.

Chuneng's story reminds us that resistance is always possible, even under the most oppressive circumstances. There are always possibilities, even i temporary ones, to turn tools o power, such as a ood delivery app platorm, against their owners. In this and the next chapter, we consider how to build sustained resistance against something as vast as a new social order built on data extraction. Practical resistance is hardly possible without some sense o why one is resisting and that, in turn, depends on something that at rst sight might seem distant rom practical action: ideas about diferent

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

utures that challenge the naturalness and inevitability o the path the world is on.

The good news is that we don't need to invent wholly new ideas in order to fnd inspiration. We can learn rom others who con ronted analogous problems in the past, especially those historical voices who tried, and sometimes ailed, to be heard as they spoke up about the colonial capture o the world's resources, or about the dangers o unlimited computing power. I we want to imagine a world where data colonialism can be eectively challenged, we must rediscover these voices.

#### Colonialism's Witnesses

Not everyone on Spain's plunder missions was at ease with what they saw. A ew were willing to challenge the new colonial system that was meant to beneft them. The best known was Dominican priest Bartolomé de las Casas, who in 1516 was the frst to be appointed to the post o 'Protector o the Indians'.2 He was one o the early voices who spoke against the devastation he witnessed.

Las Casas, born in Seville, was initially part o the Spanish conquest. He arrived in the Caribbean in 1502, and soon became a landowner and slave owner, although rom early on he trained as a priest. In time, the pain and injustice he saw, including as a chaplain participating in various colonising campaigns, drove las Casas to a new understanding. He came to believe that a genocide was in the making, and so began a personal war against the emerging colonial system. We know this rom his remarkable book, A Short Account of the Destruction of the Indies, published much later in 1542 and dedicated to the Castilian king Philip II.

Las Casas's struggle was a rustrating and protracted one, with ew victories along the way. Working with like-minded individuals, las Casas petitioned none other than Charles V, the Holy Roman Emperor (Philip II's ather), to dissolve the encomienda system, in which the Crown issued land titles to colonisers and permitted them to enslave the native population. Las Casas also advocated or laws that would protect the rights and lives o indigenous people. The emperor was somewhat convinced, going as ar as passing a set o New Laws in 542 to govern the New World, suspending all new colonisation campaigns in 550, and even considering the surrender o control over Peru (though he didn't ollow through on that in the end).

Las Casas – as activist, archivist, advocate and public intellectual – was instrumental or a while in shiting public perceptions o the conquest. His greatest opportunity to do so came in 550, when he was called to Valladolid, Spain, to debate once and or all the question o the natives' humanity. His opponent was the priest and scholar Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda, deending the Crown and the Church. Against Las Casas's arguments or the humanity o the 'indians', Sepúlveda's main point was that colonisation was a kind o just war, sanctioned by God, country and man because the 'indians' were barbarians, not humans.

In the end, the committee appointed by the Church to issue a verdict on the humanity o the colonised reused to do so. Political and material interests won out and the colonial expansion continued. The colonisers learned how to co-opt the language o critics like las Casas to make extractivism more palatable. New laws to govern the colonies, issued in 573, shited their language rom that o 'conquest' to 'pacifcation'.

And yet las Casas's voice lives on. We can still read his eyewitness account o what he saw, and still hear not only his ury, but also his clear-sightedness about the moral evils o colonisation.

But it is to colonised peoples themselves that we must turn or the clearest vision. As anthropologists David Graeber and David Wengrow point out in their remarkable book The Dawn of Everything, there were, in act, rom the beginnings o historical colonialism, strong and articulate voices among colonised peoples

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

who challenged the colonisers and their claims to legitimacy. It is only recently that Western scholarship has started to take those voices seriously. Until now, those voices tended to be heard in the form of reports by European eyewitnesses or versions penned by European theorists, and for too long contemporary scholars did not believe in their authenticity.3 As a result, Graeber and Wengrow argue, not only has history ignored the actual impact indigenous critiques had on European colonial societies, but philosophy has never acknowledged the role that indigenous voices played in formulating what became known as 'Enlightenment' values. Let's not repeat that mistake in relation to the choices contemporary societies must make about data.

A remarkable example of that tradition of indigenous critique is the musician, writer and academic, Leanne Betasamosake Simpson. She is a Michi Saagiig Nishnaabeg and member of the Alderville First Nation in Canada, and the author of many books and a recent music album, Theory of Ice. There is no way we can do justice here to the breadth and depth of her critique not just of colonialism, but of the damage that capitalism and Western ways of living are doing today. But we want to signal, drawing on her recent book, As We Have Always Done, some of the challenges she poses to all forms of colonialism.

One thing on which Simpson is very clear is the landgrab at colonialism's heart. She discusses how, during a university-sponsored research project on the land use of indigenous people of the Great Lakes region, she met the Elders of that area to build a map of their communities' use of the land. The very process of map-making and recording the key events in those communities' history brought to the surface the sheer extent of the losses they had endured. As she writes:

Standing at the foot of a map of loss is clarity. Colonialism or settler colonialism or dispossession or displacement or capitalism didn't seem complicated anymore . . . It seemed simple. Colonizers wanted the land. Everything else, whether it is legal or policy or economic or social . . . was part o the machinery that was designed to create a perect crime – a crime where the victims are unable to see or name the crime as a crime. 4

But or Simpson it is not just the historical seizure o land that matters, but the way o thinking that went with it:

Colonialism and capitalism are based on extracting and assimilating . . . The act o extraction removes all o the relationships that give whatever is being extracted meaning. Extracting is taking. Actually, extracting is stealing – it is taking without consent, without thought, care or even knowledge o the impacts that extraction has on the other living things in that environment.5

This very act o seizing territory, without consent and without care or the consequences, indicates a dysunctional relation to the world and a way o living with people that is extractive rather than reciprocal. We have ound similarly asymmetrical ways o dealing with people in data colonialism, too, even though the details are inevitably dierent.

Simpson has translated her ideas about colonialism into practice. In 2012 she participated in the 'Idle NoMore' campaign, which used digital tools to challenge the continuation o settler colonialism in Canada.6 But she also, rom this experience o practical action, refected on the limits o online-only campaigns, and on the act that movements not built on ace-to-ace communication risk creating more value or digital platorms than or the people involved.7

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

In a remarkable passage, Simpson articulates the practice o communal knowledge-making and its ethics:

Meaning, then is derived not through content or data or even theory in a Western context, which by nature is decontextualized knowledge, but through a compassionate web o interdependent relationships that are dierent and valuable because o dierence.

On this social view o knowledge, each person must not only develop their own ideas, but share those ideas with others in a way that tries not to 'interere with other beings' lie pathways', that is, in a way that does not impose her own understanding on others.8 In this indigenous way o understanding ideas as what emerge in the context o living together on shared land and territory, a completely dierent understanding o shared knowledge emerges. Within this perspective, data colonialism's driving principle – that you can get knowledge by extracting patterns at a distance rom myriad data sources across the planet, many o them accessed without consent and outside o rich human relationships – becomes both nonsensical and alarming.

As an indigenous person, Simpson thinks dierently about resistance, oering a serious practical vision o what resistance practically involves. Data colonialism may still be in development and so, as yet, all short o the terrible accumulated violence o fve centuries o settler colonialism, which is Simpson's main target.
