There is no denying this. But while remaining aware o what Simpson's ideas mean or her specifc struggle, we can still apply them to the dierent but related context o data colonialism. For Simpson, it is a matter o rejecting as a whole the civilising narratives that colonialism oers, rethinking and reworking how we live, not merely adjusting this or that isolated practice.9 At the core or Simpson (and also perhaps las Casas) resistance means holding on to values, even in the most dicult circumstances: values that, as Simpson and many other indigenous thinkers have advocated, emerge not rom abstract thought o lone individuals, but rom shared lie in a particular territory.

This is an important thread that will run through this chapter. For it is such socially generated values with which we risk losing touch when our lives become ever more layered with data territories, imposed by distant platorms and AI systems.

#### No Modernity without Colonialism

We are in the middle today o a revolution in how we understand the world, a revolution o which this book about data is just a small part. That revolution consists in grasping, as our new starting point, that all the knowledge developed about the world in the past fve centuries has been shaped by the colonial landgrab and its consequences.

To say the least, this is a challenge to mainstream ways o thinking about technology, AI and indeed everything. It is not that the basic acts o colonialism have ever disappeared rom view. On the contrary, in colonial nations such as the UK, they have been endlessly celebrated as the triumph o those nations' positive mission in the world!

Today's much-needed revolution o thought, however, means acknowledging two things. First, that what has been imagined as the 'internal' history o European and North American societies was always shaped by a larger global history o violent exploitation o colonial territories. This process was assumed by the home colonising nations to be 'external' to their history, something too ar away to matter or their own identity. And second, Europe and North America's apparently privileged role in the past fve centuries o world history, including supposedly universal histories o Western

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

culture and science, was the outcome o that colonial exploitation, not its justication.10 Our Western view o history resulted rom the West's power to decide how history gets told, a power that owed directly rom colonial dominance and ensured that other histories o what colonialism meant were almost entirely orgotten. This historical erasure is now becoming unsustainable in the Global North, but in the Global South, those alternative stories have never been entirely out o circulation.

Under conditions o historical erasure (themselves the direct result o the power inequalities o colonialism), ideas and indeed remembering history diferently take on a directly practical orce. Three writers rom the diverse areas o economic history, social theory, and the history o ideas, can help us see this more clearly.

#### A Rebel Historian

Eric Williams was born in the ormer British colony o Trinidad and Tobago in 1911. He became leader o its majority political party in 1956, the country's rst premier in 1962 and its rst president when it became a republic in 1976. His preeminent political role ensured him the status o 'ather o the nation'. He was thereore, by any measure, a successul ghter against colonialism. But at a time when the struggles o ormer colonies to claim independence are largely over, it is Williams' ideas that have the greatest practical orce.

Ater completing his doctorate at Oxord in the 1930s, Williams went on to publish a book called Capitalism and Slavery that upended the mainstream histories o Britain's Industrial Revolution as narratives o heroic homegrown innovation, with scarcely a mention o the colossal colonial wealth that uelled it.

Based on extensive documentation, Williams showed that those British mainstream histories were a sel-serving myth that bore little relation to the realities o the eighteenth-century English economy. They conveniently orgot the huge benets England derived rom the notorious triangular trade that linked () the selling o English manuactured goods to Arica to (2) the supply o slaves to English colonies in the Americas and (3) the subsequent export o staple crops (cotton, sugar and tobacco) back to England. Williams quotes an English economic commentator o the time who was not embarrassed to say that the British Empire was 'a magnicent superstructure o American commerce and naval power on an Arican oundation.'

Williams went urther and challenged the idea o colonisers that Arican populations were enslaved because o their unique physical suitability (supposedly, only they could physically endure the gruelling work o the plantations). The explanation or who became slaves was, according to Williams, much simpler: the economics o cheap labour. Yes, there was an important precondition, that the indigenous Caribbean population had been mostly decimated by the early efects o colonial arrival. But once slaves were imported to the Caribbean rom Arica as replacement labour, they were 'just there' or plantation owners to exploit, a pattern that would be replicated in the colony that later became the United States. According to Williams, then, Aricans were not racially predisposed to slavery; they just happened to be the cheapest available source o mass labour needed to exploit cash crops.2

In these various ways, Williams was anticipating current debates about the relations between capitalism and colonialism by decades. He punctured the ctional idea that the English Industrial Revolution resulted rom the miraculous innovation o white English entrepreneurs. Brute orce and basic economics were, as he showed, much more important, particularly the economics o colonialism. While Williams' statistics on the economic contribution o slave labour to eighteenth-century England have been debated, his challenge both to economic history and the history o colonialism/capitalism has been crucial. His message: there can be no capitalism without colonialism, or without the subjection o labour, including via slavery.

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

The myth o capitalism as purely an 'internal' Western development has taken even longer to dismantle, since it also meant taking seriously the implications o the US's nineteenth-century slave plantations. Already in the 1930s, the Black sociologist W. E. B. Du Bois had analysed very clearly their corrosive consequences or contemporary American society, but his work was almost entirely ignored by mainstream history and sociology. It has taken until the last two decades or a movement o historians to excavate the central role that colonialism and slavery played in the growth o the US and UK economies in the nineteenth century – indeed in the emergence o a global economic system. The result is a new understanding o capitalism as never separate rom colonialism, a capitalism, in the words o the historian o cotton Sven Beckert, based on, 'slavery, the expropriation o indigenous peoples, imperial expansion, armed trade, and the assertation o sovereignty over people and land by entrepreneurs.'13

Why has it been so dicult or Western societies to see capitalism's ongoing involvement with, and dependence on, colonialism? Eric Williams ended his country's colonial status back in 1976 and yet, today, a quarter-way through the twenty-rst century, we are still struggling to grasp these points. What i this blindness is itsel an ongoing cultural efect o colonialism? I so, there must be something more enduring to colonialism than just its political structures. But what exactly?

#### The Coloniality beyond Colonialism

For an answer to this question, we must turn to another voice, the Peruvian sociologist Aníbal Quijano, and his concept o coloniality. Writing more than thirty years ago, Quijano wanted to understand what linked the practical side o colonialism (the conquest o territory, the pattern o low-status work in colonial 'peripheries' and higher-status work in colonial 'centres') with the ways o thinking that accompanied colonialism, above all its racism. Remember the idea that Sepúlveda presented, in response to las Casas, that the colonised peoples o the Americas were barbarians inerior to Europeans, a lower orm o humanity that deserved nothing better than to be conquered and ruled.

In contrast to Eric Williams' purely economic explanation o why slavery was targeted at peoples o Arican descent, Quijano argued that, rom early on, the everyday unctions o colonialism depended on a distinctive way o imagining humanity and its capacities or knowledge. The way power was exercised under colonialism by particular races over others was itsel or Quijano not an aberration, but something distinctive about the modern way o understanding the world. It was this corrosive idea that made colonialism more than just an unending sequence o senseless violence. With this new 'civilising' story o inerior races that deserved to be colonised, colonialism as a project gained momentum and legitimacy. Quijano called this understanding the 'coloniality o power'.

The coloniality o power captures two key ideas embraced by colonisers. First, that there is one and only one way o rationally organising the world, which requires the distinctive way o producing knowledge associated with what we know as 'Western science'. Second, that this orm o knowledge production authorises a hierarchy o races (even i it has become increasingly dicult to say this explicitly). This hierarchy diferentiates between those who are closer to rationality (white men, supposedly) and those who are arther rom it (non-white people, we are told). The 'inerior' races (or example, indigenous groups) become merely the objects o the knowledge produced by the Men o Science, who overwhelmingly come rom Europe and North America.

Obviously, it is Quijano's whole point to insist that this is a debased argument. And yet, i we are honest, it captures not only many o the subtle dynamics around race in contemporary lie, but also why many o the injustices in algorithmic decision-making

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

discussed in Chapter One get treated somehow as natural and unavoidable. Indeed it gets to the core of why the language of Big Tech and AI today seems so powerful, even when its aws are becoming ever more obvious.

Today's practices of Big Data and technology-driven decisionmaking seem on their surface to have nothing to do with race, gender and class. But what if the era of Big Data, with its new asymmetrical power relations, is generating new ways of thinking hierarchically about human beings?

A key implication is suggested by some reections Quijano made about how to resist coloniality. Not, he says, by rejecting knowledge and rationality, as if they were tainted for ever through their exclusive association with the West. The best way forward for resisting the coloniality of power is to develop a better, nonhierarchical idea of knowledge and rationality, for there is no way of relating to the world that does not depend on some idea of knowledge, some idea of rationality.

What would this look like? Quijano proposes an inclusive vision of knowledge which aims to encompass, rather than to exclude, important dierences in how dierent groups of people view the world. In fact, Quijano insists that in most cultures other than the West, knowledge is already understood as built from diversity of perspectives, rather than by denying diversity. On that view of culture, it is the inclusiveness of a way of understanding the world that qualies it as real knowledge, and not just a personal perspective on the world.14

Two things are striking here. First, this apparently abstract account of knowledge has a lot in common with indigenous visions of how knowledge is produced in communities (remember Leanne Simpson). Indeed, other theorists of coloniality such as the anthropologist Ramón Grosfoguel have criticised Quijano for not explicitly recognising the voices of indigenous thinking that are audible in his work.15

Second, this more inclusive view o how real knowledge gets produced has, again, huge practical implications in a world like ours where the practices o Big Tech and Big Data oer a directly opposed top-down institutionally driven view o knowledge. This is not just a matter o people lacking the technical expertise to understand the impenetrable language in which most explanations o algorithmic workings are generally couched. Imagine you wanted to challenge how Facebook's algorithm works, or how Amazon or Tik Tok categorise your desires, or how a social welare system's algorithms operate. You will have great diculty getting answers, because, even i the algorithms were disclosed to you and you understood the details, your questioning o their rationality would be regarded rom the start as naïve and uninormed. Big Data's knowledge is the knowledge o institutions, not people, and in this it perpetuates exactly the eature o Western science that makes it, at a deep level, colonial.

I this is the case, rejecting data colonialism may mean challenging the view o human knowledge that has been at the heart o colonialism or fve centuries, by insisting on the continuing connections between real knowledge and live communities o practice. This idea will be o major importance when we turn directly to practical resistance in Chapter Six.

#### Reimagining Humanity beyond Colonialism

Another writer who helps us think dierently about knowledge, science and rationality is Sylvia Wynter. Wynter was born in Cuba, grew up in Jamaica, and has lived much o her lie in the US, where she is Proessor Emerita o Spanish at Stanord University. Her work has been devoted not just to defning 'coloniality', as Quijano did, but to unsettling and overcoming it. Like Quijano and Williams, her core idea was itsel deeply practical: to resist head-on dominant Western views o history shaped by the victors o colonial struggle.

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

For Wynter, there is no point in abandoning knowledge and rationality, just because their Western forms are tainted by the history of colonialism. If a hierarchical and racialised view of knowledge has become dominant, it is our job to develop a more inclusive view.16 Taking inspiration from her, it would be similarly absurd to abandon the idea of data; instead, we must rethink it within a more inclusive view of how human beings know the world.

In other words, the point of critical thinking is not just to reject but to replace an unsatisfactory view of human knowledge. In that spirit, we need a process of thinking about data that includes a wider range of people and perspectives. Why continue with data practices that simply reproduce, in one way or another, the inheritance of colonial and racist thinking? This is exactly the movement that critical data scientists have recently initiated, inspired by the writings of information scientist Saya Noble, sociologist Ruha Benjamin, and others.

Most interesting of all is Wynter's attempt, from the perspective of the history of ideas, to reinterpret what happened in 1492 – the year Christopher Columbus 'found' what became known as the Americas – and the decades of debate at the Spanish court that followed. As Wynter points out, the discovery of a continent that was unknown to Europeans was a huge challenge to the authority of the Catholic Church, coming only two decades before Copernicus's discoveries further undermined earlier understandings of Earth's role in the cosmos, and it was this that generated the debate between las Casa and Sepúlveda that we mentioned earlier. For Wynter, it was las Casas's interlocutor Sepúlveda – condently insisting on the privileged right of rational Western man to seize the territories of inferior beings – who represented something radically new: the birth of a secular, but also deeply racist, vision of the world. It was those ideas that won out, and it is the continuation of those arguments at the heart of powerful understandings of knowledge and humanity that must still be countered in Big Data discourse today.17

Wynter's proposal or rereading history does not mean reverting back to the worldview that was undermined in 492. It means taking seriously the historic crisis o the closed worldview that had erupted by the early sixteenth century and fnding – fve centuries on – new and better solutions. That requires, once again, an inclusive, rather than hierarchical, view o humanity.8 I we ollow Wynter, our goal becomes not to abandon thinking about what things like Artifcial Intelligence mean or human knowledge, but to look or ways o reimagining AI and Big Data which genuinely move beyond the colonial ways o thinking that, until now, have shaped them.

#### Warnings from an Earlier Computer Age

To take on that challenge, however, a very uncomortable truth must be conronted: that AI and Big Data are already retooling coloniality (the thought-system o colonialism) or an age o continuous surveillance, instantaneous communication, and limitless management power.

I that is true, in order to challenge today's new orms o colonial thinking it is not enough to reinterpret past history in general terms. It is essential to reinterpret the history o the computer technologies that have made today's AI and Big Data possible. To do this, it helps to take a step back into computing's almost orgotten past. One o the mathematicians who helped invent the computer was Norbert Wiener. Rather than simply celebrating this new technology, Wiener was ull o oreboding. In the original 947 preace to Cybernetics, one o the most revered texts in the history o computer science, he wrote:

It has long been clear to me that the modern ultra-rapid computing machine was in principle an ideal central nervous

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

system to an apparatus or automatic control . . . Long beore Nagasaki and public awareness o the atomic bomb, it had occurred to me that we were here in the presence o another social potentiality o unheard-o importance or good and or evil.19

Those words are chilling, especially when we remember that he was writing just two years ater the dropping o atomic bombs on Japan. Wiener is saying that the development o computers is a comparable danger.

O course, he could not have anticipated the world o connected computers which has launched today's Big Data, digital platorms and Artifcial Intelligence (the 'rapid' computer he wrote about was a mainrame that occupied a whole room and issued an answer in days rather than micro-seconds). But even back then, what worried him was the social potential or using computers as tools o control, as the means to rule and dominate others. In other words, his ears had already anticipated the concerns that data colonialism raises today.

And yet, in spite o Wiener's celebrated status as a ounder o the computer age, these ears were ignored. No one heeded them when networked computers started to scale to wider society in the late 1980s. No one heeded them when control o the internet passed rom public hands to private corporations in the early 1990s. And no one heeded them when social media platorms in the early 2000s were designed as data territories under those platorms' exclusive control.

I Wiener's warning has been remembered, it is only very recently and in a rather rarefed context: the development o very large-scale AI, the sort that underlies ChatGPT and other recent developments. Stuart Russell, a global AI expert and BBC Reith lecturer, recalls Wiener's concerns in his recent criticisms o where

Artifcial General Intelligence (AGI) is heading.20 Wiener had not been araid to use moral language. He wrote o computers' 'potentially unheard-o importance or good and or evil'. It's exactly Wiener's moral reerence point, Russell argues, that we must recover, i we are to correct recent developments in AGI that seem driven only by technological rationales. Large-scale AI is expanding to levels o complexity that we struggle to understand in terms o human values and thought-processes, potentially taking away the capacity to intervene at all.21 Novelists like Ian Mc Ewan and scientists like the late Stephen Hawking have echoed Russell's ears; it was Hawking who said that 'the development o ull artifcial intelligence could spell the end o the human race'.22 Wiener's writings help us see the kernel o truth in the chorus o recent calls by leading AI experts to 'halt' its most advanced orms.

But Wiener's warnings didn't end there. Ater the passage we cited, he expressed a concern that computer machines could take over human work, a ear that had been expressed by late nineteenthcentury novelists such as Samuel Butler. Wiener was agnostic about whether such computerised task-saving was good or bad or humanity. But o one thing he was sure:

It cannot be good or these new potentialities to be assessed in the terms o the market, o the money they save . . . The answer, o course, is to have a society based on human values other than buying or selling.23

On the chances o this, and the chances o avoiding the social evil rom computing he eared, Wiener was pessimistic:

We do not even have the chance o suppressing these new technical developments . . . The best we can do is to see that a large public understands the trend . . . [T]here are those who hope that the good o a better understanding o

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

man and society which is oered by this new eld may anticipate and outweigh the incidental contribution we are making to the concentration of power . . . I write in 1947, and I am compelled to say that it is a very slight hope.24

Wiener feared computers becoming institutions of social control, managed by market mechanisms with little regard for human values and directed only to the acquisition of power.

For sure, Wiener's fears were shaped by the then recent history of Nazism and fascism, and perhaps the politics of Soviet Russia too (less than two years later, George Orwell published the novel 1984). But it is wrong to dismiss Wiener's fears as outdated. Not only has the renewed relevance of Orwell's work to our surveillance-saturated societies been noted recently, but Wiener's fears have echoes in the massively unequal power relations of today's data territories.

#### Early Apprehensions about AI

A small group of computer scientists in the 1970s and 1980s con rmed Wiener's concerns about the social costs of expanding computing power in an uncontrolled way. Typically for the time, these voices were white and male. That caveat aside, we still have much to learn from them. So, let's turn to one of those voices: Joseph Weizenbaum, an MIT professor and refugee from Nazi persecution who, after work on computer systems for banking, became famous in the 1960s for designing one of the rst natural language processing systems.

The programme was called ELIZA. Paradoxically, Weizenbaum's concerns about the direction of computing arose not from the failures but from the apparent successes of ELIZA. The program attracted attention when it proved capable of interacting with a real person in a therapeutic situation, not only giving the impression of understanding them, but also oering some advice. ELIZA, it seemed, had triumphantly passed the famous Turing Test, which asked if a computer-generated output would lead a human to think they were communicating with another human. When the transcript o the therapeutic 'conversation' was published, the 'advising' computer quickly gained the nickname DOCTOR. But Weizenbaum was horried: ar rom seeing the script as just a basic demo, scientists read it as a blueprint or automated psychotherapy (think o the time and money society could save!). I scientists couldn't understand the limits o computing in such an obvious case, Weizenbaum eared there was a problem with the 'scientic outlook' itsel, a problem he set out to investigate.25

For Weizenbaum, it was obvious that there were limits to what computers could do. Computers, ater all, are machines which apply rules: rules that only work i they are absolutely explicit. Human beings, by contrast, understand things by relying on context, much o which remains implicit. There must thereore be acts o understanding that humans (indeed, non-human animals) can perorm based on contextual understanding, but computers can't. We need to understand this, beore relying on computers too heavily.

Weizenbaum was also interested in a diferent kind o limit: moral limits. As a computer scientist, he understood that a computer's dependence on explicit instructions necessarily imposed limits on how it would unction in a social setting:

[A] computing system . . . accepts only certain kinds o 'data'. Which means that relying on such a computing system or social tasks means clos[ing] many doors that were open beore it was installed.26

Yet some o these doors that computing closed of might be humanly important. I so, we have to ask not just what a computer can do, but what it ought (or ought not) to do. As Weizenbaum put it, ' "can" does not imply "ought"'.27

What increasingly worried Weizenbaum (and led him to write the book Computer Power and Human Reason) was that so many

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

scientists ignored these inevitable limitations o computers. Mainstream computer science, according to Weizenbaum, was too interested in control: not the broad social control Wiener eared, but the narrow instrumental control that operating a computer gave programmers. Yet this narrower type o control also had social consequences: it was a orm o power that Weizenbaum believed to be spreading through society. The fnal chapter o his book was called 'Against the Imperialism o Instrumental Reason'. A new sort o social order was being built, he eared, that required us to place moral limits on our use o computers.

Perhaps, Weizenbaum wondered, 'there are some acts o thought that ought to be attempted only by humans'.28 And, though he did not talk o racism or colonialism exactly (yet he does mention the word 'imperialism'), the reason or Weizenbaum's concern had something in common with the questions Quijano and Wynter were raising about the coloniality o power: 'these [computer] systems . . . have reduced reason itsel to only its role in the domination o things, man and fnally, nature.'29 Weizenbaum's ear or the type o society that will result i computer power is used in this narrow way takes us back again to Simpson's idea about the dangers o decontextualised knowledge. I knowledge emerges outside o a human context, we risk an irresponsible type o knowledge, exactly the ears now being voiced about AI.

Weizenbaum expressed those ears or a world o computer connection unimaginably less developed and commercially driven than ours. And his core idea retains practical orce today: the idea that computers are, in the end, just tools that can and should be brought back under human control, i managed in accordance with human values.30 It was only the narrowly instrumental way o thinking about computers that made us orget this.

What do these voices rom the early history o computing tell us? That the data colonialism thesis, ar rom being outlandish, echoes exactly the worries about the unrestricted uses o computer power that leading gures in computer science have been voicing rom the beginnings o computer science.

#### Imagining the Battle to Come

Realising this common theme in the history o computing and its common ground with much broader debates about colonialism takes us one step closer to imagining what resisting data colonialism might mean in practice.

The work o historian and theorist Achille Mbembe is helpul in this regard (he was born in Cameroon, and now lives in South Arica). In earlier work, Mbembe provided a clear diagnosis o the continuing eects o colonial history in what he called the 'postcolony' o Arica, countries where opportunities, resources and political structures remain prooundly marked by centuries o colonial extraction (by Britain, France and others). Mbembe unpacked the many levels o violence that colonialism involved: the initial seizure o the land, the violence that stabilised colonial power, and the violence that sustained colonial power over the long term. This violence was both physical and symbolic, and it still leaves a proound mark on the organisation o contemporary Arican societies.31

In his more recent work, Mbembe has turned to thinking about the uture: the uture o Arica and o the world, a world prooundly shaped by technology. He is the opposite o naïve when it comes to the continuing power and infuence o the so-called Global North across the world today. But our starting point or thinking about the uture (uture resistance, uture solutions) cannot, Mbembe insists, be Europe or the Global North. Europe, he writes, 'is no longer the center o gravity o the world. This is the most signicant event, the undamental experience, o our era.'32 Since the violence o colonialism is continuing, the challenge and resistance must be ramed rom perspectives outside the Global North. But how?

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

When Mbembe says that the violence o colonialism is continuing, he does not just mean that the neocolonial legacy continues in the sites o historic colonialism – o course it does. Mbembe is particularly concerned by new orces o data extraction driven, not least, by Silicon Valley, and targeting human lie everywhere. The transormation implied by the endless extraction o inormation is, in Mbembe's view, aecting the Global North too, or at least those outside their global elites. He is worried by broadly the same developments that we have been discussing in this book: new territorial relations o data extraction, extended management power, increasing surveillance, the unthinking expansion o AI. Mbembe calls this 'the becoming-artifcial o humanity'.33

These new developments, he argues, have a proound implication or how any o us anywhere can imagine resistance. His way o putting this is provocative:34

[There is] the very distinct possibility that human beings will be transormed into animate things made up o coded digital data. Across early capitalism, the term 'Black' reerred only to the condition imposed on peoples o Arican origin . . . Now, or the frst time in human history, the term 'Black' has been generalized. This new norm o existence . . . expanded to the entire planet is what I call the Becoming Black of the world.

What does Mbembe mean by this phrase? He is not reerring exclusively to skin colour, and he is not only reerring to racial identity. When he says the term 'Black' has been 'generalized', he is reerring to a condition o unreedom imposed on Black people or centuries, but now extended to 'the entire planet' through the datafcation o human lie. Seen rom the decolonial ramework we have advanced in this book, we can reinterpret the development to which Mbembe reers as the condition o data colonialism itsel. It is through this that, as Mbembe says, 'capitalism sets about recolonizing its own center'. In this transormation, no region is spared. Resistance everywhere becomes a matter o working out what 'reserves o lie . . . [can] escape sacrifce,'36 not only in the Global South, but potentially everywhere.

But how to resist colonialism i we understand it in this way? We need to ocus around a common question. But once again, we can't invoke a 'common humanity' and ignore the continuing orces that until now have divided humanity and reproduced inherited colonial hierarchies. So or Mbembe, like Wynter, the task is to rethink what we mean by humanity. Given the divisive legacy o colonialism, there is only one way to do that, and that is in the context o the still larger common challenge which overshadows his argument: the challenge o inhabiting together a planet under threat o destructive climate change.37

It is at this point that we need to turn to Canadian author and activist Naomi Klein, who has written with great orce about both climate change and the continuing need to conront the violent legacy o colonialism. We cannot possibly do justice here to the range o Klein's inuential writings. But let's ocus on one core point, which goes to the heart o what resistance to data colonialism is about.

Klein's work has not usually ocussed on data. But an important exception was Klein's intervention early in the pandemic in reaction to New York's Governor Andrew Cuomo's enthusiastic adoption o plans by the ex-CEOs o Microsot (Bill Gates) and Google (Eric Schmidt) to introduce more tech – and much more data extraction – into the everyday lie o New York's citizens.

Klein had made headlines a decade earlier when she spotted the pattern in how big business o all types, and powerul governments, used disasters to create opportunities or the private and public sector to exploit vulnerable populations more ruthlessly. She called this 'the shock doctrine' o 'disaster capitalism'. Its ethos? Always

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

use a temporary crisis as leverage to install permanently the uture models o value extraction that beneft you, i you are a corporation or government. The frst example was the imposition o the very frst neoliberal economic regimes in Chile, ater the coup against President Allende in 1973.38

Klein saw a similar pattern in New York City in the early months o the pandemic, and the core o this pattern was unlimited data extraction. Called 'visionary' by Governor Mario Cuomo, Eric Schmidt's and Bill Gates' plans were to introduce systems or remote teaching, remote work, remote health provision, and so on – in other words, systems or extracting value through data and data-related services. The supposed goal was to fx the temporary, i extremely serious, problems caused by the Covid-19 lockdowns. As Cuomo put it, 'all these buildings, all these physical classrooms – why, with all the technology you have?' Why, that is, would you even need them, i you had the inrastructure to allow people to work and study remotely?39 As we know, this shock doctrine brought with it increased extraction and increased surveillance. 'Pivoting' to tele-presence was a great business or the tech sector, one o the ew that experienced record profts during the pandemic. Not as ortunate was the working class, the so-called 'essential' workers, or whom going remote was not a possibility.

In this context, Klein is challenging exactly the vision o unconstrained data extraction that we saw was a core vision o data's colonial class. But the way she rames this argument connects with her earlier work on the environmental crisis. In her acclaimed 2014 book, This Changes Everything, she notes that climate change will not be addressed until we stop the proft-driven extraction practices, particularly o ossil uels, whose sales drive the consumption that results in global warming. Political incompetence aside, Klein argues, a key reason or the ailure to address this is that, rom the perspective o the extractors – and the populations in the Global North and elsewhere who condone them – the damage is always happening somewhere else: in a distant country, or even in places in one's own country that somehow don't count. This 'somewhere else' is not just where the extraction happens, but also where climate consequences are most acutely elt. Klein calls these ignored places 'sacrifce zones'. Colonialism has been particularly good at creating them.

As Klein writes, 'extractivism became rampant under colonialism because relating to the world as a rontier o conquest – rather than as home – osters this particular brand o irresponsibility'.40 This idea o extraction carried out with impunity at a distance was perected under capitalism, but it was already there at the origin o historical colonialism. Historically, there have always been constraints on where extraction could happen (diculties o particular mining locations), or how much proft could be generated (or example, variable transportation costs).41 Without these constraints, extraction would have been even more extreme, or the idea o extractivism, especially when carried out at a comortable distance, knows no limits.

This opens up a rightening possibility or the case o data extraction, which Klein hinted at in her piece on the pandemic 'screen new deal': the sources o human data are not ar away rom the corporations that want them, they are just a device away. Switch your device on, and you are already plugged directly into an external system that can seamlessly extract data rom you and your lie. The riction o distance, which once constrained extraction during historical colonialism and capitalism, is now reduced almost to nothing. The lie sources rom which Big Tech wants to extract data – all o us digital consumers – are right there on the other side o a screen. So, Klein warns us, i we let ourselves be misled by those data-extracting narratives, we may give up some things that are pretty important or our whole uture as human beings. Not least our autonomy.

#### VOICES OF DEFIANCE

And this is exactly what we mustn't do. As things turned out, the adjustments many of us made early on in the pandemic – towards remote working and ever greater reliance on data-extracting platforms – were not converted into completely new ways of organising work. Although slowly and with resistance, we have, most of us, got back to working together face-to-face, at least some of the time.

But suppose the uncertainty of the early pandemic had continued much longer, due to delays in developing vaccines. Suppose the temptation to buy in permanently to the sort of proposal made by Big Tech leaders Schmidt and Gates had taken over, propelled by the belief, as one industry commentator put it, that 'humans are biohazards, machines are not'. The 'shock doctrine' (borrowing Klein's term) of data extractivism might then have locked us into patterns of remote working, teaching and surveillance for the future,42 committing ourselves to a world, as Klein puts it, 'in which our homes are never again exclusively personal spaces but are also, via high-speed digital connectivity, our schools, our doctor's oces, our gyms, and, if determined by the state, our jails'.43 In fact, as we've seen in earlier chapters, that is exactly the future that many people do indeed seem to have accepted: a future whose costs will be extremely unevenly distributed, and whose environmental costs will certainly be high. We really must, as Klein insists, start saying no.

Through a series of steps between apparently abstract ideas, we have reached the point where we can start to imagine what resistance to data colonialism could practically look like.

#### Resources for Resistance?

Ideas are tools. We manage our lives dierently when equipped with new ideas. In this chapter we have unlocked some new ideas that can arm us against the worst of data colonialism. Let's sum up what we have learned.

#### Data Grab

- Never give up on the basic values o what we believe to be acceptable or unacceptable (las Casas).
- Never accept new notions o knowledge whose apparent useulness depends on overriding the world o human interrelations that we already trust and value (Simpson, Klein).
- Rethink our understanding o what counts as knowledge and rational behaviour. Reject claims to knowledge that, at root, reinorce inequality and domination. Understand what knowledge is and where it comes rom (Quijano, Simpson, Weizenbaum).
- Rethink humanity as a shared project against the background o the accumulated violence o centuries o colonialism (Wynter and Mbembe).
- Acknowledge the special dangers that ollow when we use computers as i they had no social consequences, and as i we do not have the right – indeed the duty – to set limits on their usage (Wiener, Weizenbaum, Mbembe).
- And fnally: be ready to see colonial extractivism, old and new, or what it is: a grab or power, happening right there in ront o us, in the banal details o everyday lie (Klein).

With these tools in hand, we are now ready to start thinking in detail about what resistance to data colonialism might look like.

#### CHAPTER 6

## A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

JOSEFINA LUCÍA IS a trans-woman programmer, an activist for dissident technology, and a teacher living in Buenos Aires, Argentina. In a country where the life expectancy for a trans person is 35 years, and only 18 per cent of trans people obtain formal employment, Josefina Lucía and her friends are determined to make a difference. Together, they helped start Alternativa Laboral Trans (ALT), a worker cooperative specialising in technology and owned mostly by trans and non-binary people.

ALT's mission is to expand job opportunities for this vulnerable community. To accomplish this, ALT offers website design and development services, and conducts workshops on programming and the application of technologies that are not based on extractivist models. When building a website or app for themselves or for their clients, ALT works with technologies that allow trans people to keep their creations under their control as much as possible – for instance, by using open-source software instead of commercial software. ALT also acts as an incubator for tools such as Lupa (magnifying glass, in Spanish), an app for reporting and visualising institutional gender-based violence which functions as an educational and awareness tool.

There is a tangible need for these kinds of tools, given the high rates of violence that is directed towards gender non-conforming individuals, and the ways in which historically online platforms have sought to force these individuals to conform by limiting the options available to them to dene their gender. ALT is also working with the Wikimedia Foundation to develop a platform that will help First Nations people store and share geolocation data about objects and sites that are culturally signicant to them.

We have been using ctional composites based on non-ctional details at the start of every chapter, but in this case Josena Lucía and ALT are one hundred per cent real (see altcooperativa.com). People like them and the others discussed in this chapter are proof that data colonialism can be resisted. For that, we don't need to start with grand, world-changing social movements (although we might eventually get there). We can start with small local acts that demonstrate that, as for Josena Lucía and ALT, movements to decolonise our data are often tied to other social justice movements. Together, they prove that there are alternatives to the ways extractivist technology corporations, organisations and governments manage the world.

Certainly, data colonialism is not a problem with an easy x. The countless examples we have discussed throughout the book have made that painfully clear. One cannot simply brush away these news forms of dispossession and inequality with a single new law, a revolutionary technology or even a social revolution any more than we can undo colonialism's ve centuries of dispossession and inequality. Data colonialism has unleashed a torrent of social impacts that go well beyond the capturing of data from our daily lives. Let's highlight eight:

1. Workers' rights and their sense of autonomy are being diminished; whole sectors of the economy are being reorganised around data extraction, giving more power to managers and less to workers;

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

- 2. algorithms increasingly make important decisions about the lives o the most vulnerable and disadvantaged people in our societies; ethnic minorities, women, gender non-conorming and low-income people have been targeted through new and pernicious orms o surveillance;
- 3. corporations trying to sell us stu, and political parties trying to get us to vote or certain candidates or agendas, use data to manipulate us in opaque ways;
- 4. a global pandemic was used as an excuse to collect more data rom everybody;
- 5. platorms that are more interested in proft than in protecting users' rights have washed their hands o the spread o disinormation and speech that promote hate and violence;
- 6. data centres, and many other physical aspects o our digital communications environment, continue to deplete the environment o water and energy;
- 7. the long-term impact o data colonialism on our mental health has barely been studied;
- 8. all o this, while good uses o data or non-commercial purposes receive little support, with most o the attention and money going to commercial and exploitative uses.

And remember: just because some o us are not eeling the ull eects o data colonialism, it doesn't mean that others are as ortunate. Data colonialism, like historical colonialism, is creating an ever more unequal social and economic landscape.

And yet, colonial history has also taught us that it is possible to resist. As long as we are willing to make the connection between what is happening with our data and the larger problem o historical colonialism, we may paradoxically make data questions more manageable. We can learn rom previous orms o anti-colonial resistance what has worked, and what hasn't. The good news is that, fnally, as a way to reject the entire project, an unstoppable orce is emerging . . .

#### Resistance Is Already Here, and Nothing Can Stop It

Resistance to colonialism emerged with colonialism itsel. There is already a 5-year history o resistance, even i it is not always visible in our mainstream history books or celebrated in our media.

This history is denitely complex. Yes, colonies became independent nations, but oten through violent processes that did not benet all citizens o those newly ormed nations equally. The truth is that anti-colonial liberation movements have been mired in paradoxes, contradictions, and new orms o injustice that replicate some o the old orms o injustice themselves. Nevertheless, out o these complicated histories, inspirational resistance movements have emerged.

These movements have allowed us to assess how colonialism, past and present, is intertwined with poverty, migration, ractured social and gender relations, and even with the impending climate disaster. That is why the wider reckoning with the legacy o historical colonialism, and with the postcolonial growing pains o ormer colonies, has become a pressing global conversation. While our argument ocuses primarily on the eld o data extraction, we must recognise how this orm o extractivism is connected to others. By rejecting data colonialism head on, we ofer one important, but ar rom exclusive, starting point or addressing the legacy o historical colonialism over the past ve centuries.

Conronting extractivism, however, means being honest about one important point: how we are, individually and collectively, implicated in the new social order that is being built through new orms o data extraction. We cannot build resistance without solidarity, but that means being clear rom the start about how we have individually contributed to this system in various ways.

In other words, we need to be honest about assigning blame where necessary. The scientists and engineers behind the creation o these extractivist technologies share some o the blame, certainly.

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

It's no excuse or them to argue that they couldn't oresee the impact that their tools would have, although i that's the case, blame should also be assigned to the proessors and schools who educated them, and who neglected to include in their curriculum basic critical thinking tools and notions o ethics and social responsibility. O course, corporations and states deserve much o the blame, as we have seen throughout the book. But what about shareholders, who demand that those corporations put prots beore people? What about the media, which regularly acts as a mouthpiece or the civilising narratives o data colonialism, instead o asking critical questions? And what about us, the users o these technologies? It is one thing to lack knowledge, and another to look the other way when we perceive the impacts o extractivism.

Some clarity about our individual level o responsibility is there ore a good basis or then doing something about the problem. And people are already hard at work resisting data colonialism in many creative and collective ways.

#### How Citizens Are Resisting

Citizens have exerted pressure on their local governments to curb the use o surveillance systems and AI. In the United States alone, 17 communities have issued bans against the use o acial recognition by police, including Berkeley, Boston, New Orleans and San Francisco.1 This is a good starting point or resisting data colonialism, since the technology has been shown to be inaccurate (especially or the aces o non-white people) and a threat to privacy, inormation security, and ree expression.

The state o Caliornia has gone as ar as to ban predictive policing (the practice o using AI and other orms o analytics to identiy uture criminal activity), and the city o Seattle passed the nation's strongest regulations on surveillance technologies.2 This continues a trend o citizens around the world taking a stance against some o the most toxic efects o AI and data collection, sometimes adopting more progressive positions than ederal governments. One inspiration is the Reclaim Your Face coalition, which helped launch a European Citizens' Initiative to push or more strict regulation o biometric surveillance.3

This strategy is not without its challenges, however. According to the most recent data, some o the US cities mentioned above may be undoing such bans in the ace o increasing crime rates and pressure rom police departments to use 'any available tools' to reduce crime. Lobbying eorts by corporations eager to sell their technological solutions to local governments are also to blame or the ban reversal.4 Meanwhile, the global movement to build Smart Cities and Smart Welare systems regularly excludes citizens rom decisions about the surveillance technologies that will shape their lives.5

Eliminating such invasive technologies will be a long struggle. In the Netherlands, or instance, public pressure orced the government o Prime Minister Mark Rutte to resign in 2021 ater a scandal revealed an AI system had alsely accused thousands o parents, primarily immigrants, o committing childcare raud (Rutte later returned to power, only to resign again in the summer o 2023). Some Dutch cities have continued to use AI to detect childcare raud, targeting specifc neighbourhoods or even individual buildings or surveillance. 6 I data colonialism is part o a social order, as we have repeatedly emphasised, resistance must be or the long-term.

#### How Workers Are Resisting

What was unthinkable a ew years ago is at least a possibility now: there is in the US an Alphabet (Google) Workers Union, an Amazon Labor Union, and an Apple Retail Union, while in the UK Amazon workers are getting support or their unionisation rom the longestablished GMB Union; and workers at Meta (at least those in the mailroom) are also trying to unionise.7 The video game industry,

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

notorious or its labour abuses, is seeing a rising union movement.8 As we noted in Chuneng's story in the last chapter, workers are also organising themselves against the depredatory practices o gig platorms. From Uber drivers in South Arica to Zomato ood delivery contractors in India, gig workers throughout the world are coming together to try to orm unions or to exert pressure on governments to pass laws that guarantee their basic rights. Workers in Nigeria are preparing lawsuits against Uber with advice rom their peers in the UK, as they all ght a corporation that preers to deal with workers not as a group, but as disempowered individuals. In the process, transnational worker coalitions like the International Alliance o App-Based Transport Workers (IAATW) are orming.9 Yes, union membership is still at a record low in places like the US.10 But these are encouraging signs.

While it is essential to change laws in order to protect giglabour employees, workers are sometimes taking matters into their own hands and developing DIY approaches to dealing with the platorm's algorithms. In India, drivers have ormed groups like the Commercial Cab Driver's Awareness and the Telangana Gig and Platorm Workers' Union (two o dozens o similar groups that exist on the chat app Telegram) to post questions, troubleshoot problems, and get advice rom more experienced drivers. In Indonesia, drivers have used social media to orm communities where they provide nancial assistance to those experiencing hardship, help each other x their motorbikes, and even ofer 'account therapy' to coax the algorithm to behave in ways that are more benecial to them. The goal: to try and reverse-engineer the apps to counter their exploitative efects.11 And in a number o Arican countries, workers or the online task platorm Upwork have ound ways to help each other get that crucial rst task on the platorm.12 In New York City, a group calling themselves Deliveristas Unidos (made up mostly o immigrants) efectively demanded basic labour rights against the gig economy platorms that exploited them, including a guaranteed minimum wage, transparency about how tips are calculated – one o their slogans was 'Tips are not wages!' – and even the right to use restaurant bathrooms (although ensuring that those demands are met has been dicult).13 In spite o successes like these, the eorts o United Taxi Workers o San Diego demonstrates how dicult it is or collectives to develop their own technological solutions outside o large-scale capital investment and extractivist inrastructures.14

Nonetheless, workers in precarious situations are nding ways o resisting data colonialism. The Homeless Worker Movement in Brazil, or instance, is actively engaged in developing their own approaches to digital sovereignty. For them, this means democratising access to ICT and media production tools in order to 'circumvent unavorable political structures, mobilize resources, carry out campaigns, promote militancy engagement and document the memory o our popular struggles'.15

Meanwhile, corporate and state workers in the Global North are playing a crucial role through their dissent in bringing about change rom within the belly o the beast. The cases o government whistle-blowers Edward Snowden and Chelsea Manning, who helped expose the US global surveillance apparatus, are perhaps too unique to generalise. But their courage paved the way or other whistle-blowers and dissenters like Frances Haugen, who revealed Facebook-Meta's willingness to put prot beore the saety o its users; or Timnit Gebru, the Google researcher whose pioneering work on the limits and faws o large-scale AI we discussed in Chapter Three; or Meredith Whittaker, another ormer Google employee who helped organise a walkout in 2018 that involved more than twenty thousand employees worldwide. Countless other individuals and groups have started to speak out against some o the most powerul corporations in the world, especially their lucrative contracts with government agencies. One o their petitions reads:

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

We are part of a growing movement, comprised of many across the industry who recognize the grave responsibility that those creating powerful technology have to ensure what they build is used for good, and not for harm.16

The courage of these workers, whose lives in various ways are closely entangled with the power of Big Tech, show us that Big Tech is not a monolith and it too can be a site of resistance.

#### How Activists Are Resisting

The extractivist practices perpetrated by data's new colonial class are no longer invisible or unchallenged. We are witnessing the birth of activist movements against data extractivism that range from local grassroots collectives to well-funded transnational think tanks. No Tech ForICE (notechforice.com) was started by the latinx coalition Mijente to highlight the use by the US Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency of the technologies and data services supplied by Palantir, Amazon, Thomson Reuters and others that facilitate inhumane and sometimes illegal methods to persecute immigrants. The No Tech For Apartheid movement (notechforapartheid.com) is a project of MPower Change and Jewish Voices for Peace that works on bringing to light the collaborations between the Israeli government and companies like Google and Amazon to surveil Palestinians (according to Amnesty International, Israeli authorities are using advanced surveillance technologies to entrench a new 'automated apartheid').17

The case against the Israeli government is interesting because it has also generated a coalition of dozens of activist groups working to expose the state's spyware industry. Groups like the Palestinian Boycott, Divestment and Sanctions movement are calling for a ban of this software.18 But, as has been well documented, the danger is also global. Pegasus software – which is sold by the NSO Group under the protection of the Israeli state – has been used to monitor and target citizens in at least thirty-our countries including France, Germany, India, Mexico and Spain. The sotware has resulted in what Amnesty International calls massive human rights violations, including the harassment and murder o journalists and activists.19

Other important alliances are emerging between activists resisting data colonialism and those working in areas like labour, consumer protection and the environment. Take or example the emerging global coalition o individuals (rom Chile, Ireland, the Netherlands and elsewhere) who are challenging how data centres use vast amounts o energy and contribute to global warming.20 Or take the project Our Data Bodies whose researchers have worked closely with marginalised US neighbourhoods in North Carolina, Michigan and Caliornia to understand how data practices impact on them dierentially and to support the evolution o alternatives.21

#### How Indigenous Communities Are Resisting

First Nations understand colonialism like no other community, having experienced and resisted it directly or centuries. There are many aspects to this resistance, including fghting or ood and water sovereignty. But data is also becoming part o the struggle. An Indigenous Data Sovereignty movement has materialised to ensure that indigenous people maintain autonomy over their data resources. Indigenous people seek to control the data they generate, how it is used, and even what it means or their communities now and in the uture.

Instead o rejecting digital technologies altogether, indigenous communities are thinking careully about how they can be appropriated to support shared goals and, as indigenous Mixe theorist Yásnaya Elena Aguilar Gil puts it, 'repurposed or resistance'.22 These communities are building their own telephone and internet inrastructures (just like they have been doing with radio or a long time), creating their own video streaming platorms to tell stories in their own narrative structures, writing their own Wikipedia entries,

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

and developing their own apps and web browsers in their own languages.23

The issue o indigenous languages is particularly important to these communities, since it is estimated that between 50 and 90 per cent o languages will be endangered or dead by the year 2100.24 Yes, Big Tech oers its own 'solution' to language disappearance through datafcation, digitising indigenous voices to build speech recognition apps that can be used to keep those languages alive, but this only replicates colonial structures o dependence. In indigenous communities like the Māori in Aotearoa (otherwise known as New Zealand), people are rejecting that model. To save the indigenous language te reo, community activists Peter-Lucas Jones and Keoni Mahelona have developed their own AI language tools, and developed methods to collect and manage language data in a manner that allows the community to retain control and ownership o the process. When describing why they opted to build their own digital hosting platorms, instead o simply uploading everything to a Big Tech platorm, their answer was very clear: 'Our data would [that way] be used by the very people that beat that language out o our mouths to sell it back to us as a service . . . It's just like taking our land and selling it back to us.'25 In a contrasting case, volunteers on the Iran–Iraq border contributed hundreds o hours o labour to ensure that Sorani Kurdish was available in Google Translate. The company paid them nothing and now owns the results o their work.26

We come here to a undamental issue: the desire or autonomy. More and more groups across the world want to use digital technologies and data to ensure the continuity o indigenous, marginalised, minoritised or low-resource languages, but in ways that they control. The survival o their language means the survival o their culture, which means securing the ability to use it saely and on their own terms in an online world whose technologies they also want to control.27

#### How Everybody Can Resist

The movement or reusing data colonialism goes still wider. It includes cultural workers: artists, lmmakers, novelists and journalists. It also includes students and teachers, politicians and constituents, and everybody in non-prot organisations working to ensure the right to encryption or the right to repair. Resistance can be largescale (boycotting a Big Tech platorm) or small-scale (reusing to accept the HTTP cookies when visiting a website (which apparently only 0.5 per cent o users do).28 Even putting down our phones or a ew hours might be an act o deance.

It is easy to make un o the inuencer announcing to their social media ollowers that they are doing a weekend digital detox. But we are seeing ever more examples o individuals who are quitting these exploitative technologies.29 And businesses too: not everyone will be able to take the nancial risks that the CEO o Lush did, who said he was willing to lose US \$13 million in revenue as a result o closing their social media accounts,30 but it is inspiring nonetheless.

More accessible orms o resistance do exist. Take the teenagers in New York who have ormed a Luddite Club and exchanged their smartphones or ip phones or no phones at all. One o them proclaimed: 'You post something on social media, you don't get enough likes, then you don't eel good about yoursel. That shouldn't have to happen to anyone'.31

While the actions o any single individual or group are generally invisible, collectively they demonstrate that the movement to resist data extractivism is going on in many places and taking many orms.32 Inspired by the thinkers and activists we learned about in the last chapter, we can reclaim the moral orce o resistance, develop new ways to link past and present, and rethink the project o humanity. A certain mix o outrage, curiosity and passion or justice may be required to join this anti-colonial movement. But since everyone is afected by data colonialism, resistance can come rom people in all walks o lie and all sorts o places. One common denominator is crucial and it is the greatest weapon against colonialism that has ever been designed. The Luddite Club, and everyone else resisting data colonialism, already seem to possess it in abundance: imagination!

#### Radically Reimagining How We Use Data

'Decolonisation' can mean many things, not all o them radical or transormative. But i we're serious about it,33 decolonisation means the incredibly dicult work o ceasing to see the world rom the coloniser's point o view, and then imagining and implementing alternatives. While moving beyond the legacy o colonialism in every aspect o social lie is a task beyond the scope o this book, let's think what this would mean in relation to data.

Decolonising data means dismantling extractivist models or collecting data, rejecting proft-driven views o our lie as a territory ripe or dispossession, and turning data into a collectively owned tool or transorming the world in positive ways. But how can we do this?

A good starting point is to think about what data colonialism means or our personal space and time: can we imagine decolonising the space and time we give up to platorms and processes that extract data rom us? The space colonised by the amiliar dataextracting technologies – surveillance cameras, ftness trackers, Smart appliances, digital assistants, data-extracting chips under our skin – that turned our space, the space o our lives, into a territory o continuous extraction or proft. And the time colonised by these same data technologies, as we interact with them, and supply them with inormation about ourselves; a time flled with alerts, notifcations, updates and reminders that has become less and less our time, and more and more the time we must surrender in service to the coloniser's agenda.

To decolonise our space and time may involve learning the art o what artist and writer Jenny Odell calls 'doing nothing'. This is not a deence o procrastination, but something much bolder. It means disengaging rom the attention economy, the economy that revolves around getting us to spend more time online in order to extract more data rom us, so that we can re-engage with something else. 'That "something else" is nothing less than time and space', Odell writes: decluttering our attention, bringing our ocus back to what surrounds us, and how we spend our time in it.34 Re-engaging with nature, with each other, and with the challenges o the world. Having said that, we should recognise that not everyone can aord to disengage rom platorms, particularly those in precarious economic situations who might depend on the gig economy to make a living. But the dominant power o Big Tech will not be deeated unless its decolonisation starts somewhere.

New eminist struggles are a huge inspiration or this imaginative politics o resistance. One powerul thinker is the Argentinian Veronica Gago. For her, the problem is always extractivism: treating women's bodies and labour as 'zones o extractivism' open to seizure by males in what she calls the 'colonization o new territories'.35 Gago insists that it is the space and time o daily lie that is the rst rontier to be deended, since 'domestication and colonization are inseparable'.36 Which means that liberation is possible only when we conront how the system dehumanises people in a daily and intimate manner. This opens up some very difcult questions: Can we start to unthink common notions o private property? Can we open up to some ideas o collective property? When thinking about our data, these questions could be the start o a longer imaginative journey in which eminism plays an important part in resisting data colonialism.

Earlier critiques o modern technology can help us, too. Take the theologian, philosopher and social critic Ivan Illich, who was born in Austria but lived much o his lie in Mexico. In his 1973

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

book Tools for Conviviality, Illich reected on how our tools tend to detach us from our real goals, in the process creating 'useful things for useless people'.37 Illich suspected that when the 'useful' technologies of the industrial age oer us a supposedly easier and more convenient life, they encourage us to surrender a degree of control and autonomy over life itself. Consider Whats App or We Chat: it might make it 'easier' to communicate with friends and family, but thereby converts everyday communication into an input of a prot-generating process. Put bluntly, in the long run a life full of 'smart' devices is really not smart at all.

The answer, Illich proposes, is a society of responsible tools, a convivial society in which humans control their technologies, not the other way around. What makes this society convivial is that its 'technologies serve politically interrelated individuals' in ways that promote 'autonomous and creative intercourse among persons'.38 Conviviality, as Illich uses it, is an evocative word. It suggests not just a warm atmosphere, a craic as the Irish term puts it, but a technopolitical project in which individual freedom gets realised only through the interdependence between people. Far from being against technology, Illich saw that our interdependence can be facilitated by our technologies, but only if we put careful limits on our tools so that they work with us, rather than govern us in ways that benet multinational corporations.

Illich's ideas link to radical feminist thinking, to indigenous thinking, and to recent proposals for disconnection. While his writing is half a century old, it still captures the need for new forms of resistance. Decolonising data is not about renouncing data per se, but about embracing models that produce convivial data: data that can help us relate to each other responsibly and better, and that can help us protect each other and the environment. And the core, once again, is our human creativity and imagination. Trust in this, and we can have hope that convivial data, data that serves actual communities, will become possible.

To move in this direction requires sustained eort. Projects in Data Science or Social Good (DSSGx) are becoming trendy crowdsourcing exercises sponsored by Ivy League institutions, and even the G8 have announced that they will push their governments towards making data open by deault.39 But generating data and making it accessible to the public is not enough. Ater all, we already have plenty o data on global warming or the eects o racism, which has obviously not been enough to convince some individuals, corporations and governments to change. The larger and more dicult goal o transorming data into better social relations is what's at stake in 'convivial data'. But already there are examples o just this, such as initiatives that use local data to address structural racism in communities.40 Others, such as the collective Data Against Feminicide (datoscontraeminicidio.net), train organisations to standardise data collection practices, develop data analysis tools, and think about what it means to serve as stewards o that data.41

Precisely because decolonising data is a project or living dierently with data, it must encompass not one answer, but many. It must ormulate solutions that are not only technological but social, political, regulatory, cultural, scientifc and educational. And it must connect to struggles that seemingly have nothing to do with data, but that in reality are part o the same underlying struggle or justice and dignity.

Some people might reject this search or alternatives out o hand, as evidence o a desire to do away with Western reason, even Western civilisation altogether. But, as we saw when we considered Quijano's work, that is too hasty. I we learn anything rom the history o colonialism, it is that concepts like reason, pluralism and autonomy are not constructs to which only the West, or Western models o society, can lay claim. In act, Western thinking 'borrowed' (to put it euphemistically) many o these concepts rom non-Western traditions.42 So it's not a question o doing away with rationality; it's a question o doing away with the Western claim

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

(echoed by Big Tech) o exclusivity over rationality, diversity and autonomy, as i only Western models o those things could exist.

Once we begin to multiply and apply the alternative rationalities and alternative ways o producing and applying data described above, the process o decolonising data begins in earnest. We are already seeing communities take control o their data, take control o the process o putting limits on data technologies (ollowing Illich), and reimagine what data is and what it's or. These communities are asking, or instance: 'Can AI be decolonised, or is there something intrinsically colonial about the way in which its algorithms are trained through extractivism?' No one has defnite answers yet, but the conversations have started. Honest discussions about our own role and participation in data colonialism have also started. These dialogues are oriented towards values such as autonomy, care, community and reason.

What matters above all is to interpret these values not in a narrow way that eeds power and domination, but in an inclusive and pluralistic way. There's still great power in the vision o the Zapatistas' land resistance movement in the southern Mexican state o Chiapas 30 years ago, who proposed 'a world in which many worlds ft'. Data, i produced and used in the right way, can ft with this plural vision o the world, and create new types o data territories, where data is gathered, processed and managed according to the goals and norms that real communities design and approve.

#### Introducing the Playbook

One thing is clear: single-track approaches to solving data colonialism are not going to work. We can try to quit this or that platorm, pass this or that law . . . but by themselves, isolated actions are not going to have much o an impact because o the complexity and persistence o the problem – which is, ater all, part o a larger problem with a long history! We need to be able to think and act more holistically. The resisters and thinkers that have inspired this book have all done just that.

The activist organisation Mijente proposed a ramework that can help. This ramework was adapted rom Latin American activists such as the Pobladores en Lucha (Residents in Struggle) movement in Chile.43 Taking inspiration rom this model, collective resistance on the largest scale always means tackling data colonialism at three levels simultaneously: by working within the system o data colonialism, by working against the system, and by working beyond the system.

#### Play #1: Working within the System

Having read about the multiple abuses perpetrated by data colonialism's corporations, and the nation-states that beneft rom them, your frst reaction might be to abandon any kind o collaboration with representatives rom the private and public sectors. That type o response has its uses, which we will discuss later. But walking away rom this kind o work risks strengthening the hand o governments and corporations who might not have our best interest in mind. What we mean by working with or within the system, then, is that we must go on demanding our rights rom states and corporations, even i they are unwilling or unable to guarantee them. The moment we cease to demand those rights, we give governments and corporations permission to do as they please, and to orgo any kind o obligation to us. Even i we know the serious limits to any response we expect to get – i our grievances are even heard at all – we must go on exercising our rights as citizens and consumers, whether at the local, national or international level.

What kind o pressure can we exert within the system to decolonise data? We need to push or better and more regulation o the market. Attempts so ar to rein in Big Tech have been slow.

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

The European Union has made important strides in ormulating rameworks or what a well-regulated Social Quantication Sector might, in part, look like. Recent European legislative changes are too complex to discuss in detail here. But without question the GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation), which came into orce in 2018, laid down a basic challenge to the idea that Big Tech market power should grow without restraint by insisting on the human rights implications o 'the processing o personal data'.44 New legislation such as the Digital Services Act and the Digital Markets Act goes urther and regulates the services that platorms provide and the asymmetrical market structures rom which they benet. Meanwhile the EU Commission has imposed a record ne on Meta (€1.2 billion) and is considering a ban on acial recognition in public places.45

Many legal commentators worry that EU legislation relies too heavily on the principle o consent: giving consumers the opportunity to consent, or not, to whatever Big Tech wants to do with their data. As noted in earlier chapters, data gets extracted in many circumstances where real consent is impossible, since orce plays a greater role. And even the best legislation is only as good as its enorcement by courts and regulators. For example, Amazon recently avoided a multi-billion-dollar ne or monopolistic behaviour by agreeing to make some changes to its shopping website, changes which it was already planning to do.46 And the new EU legislation, even when it comes ully into orce, may ace court challenges by Big Tech giants. Broader doubts also remain about whether EU legislators really want to halt the expansion o Big Tech's data extraction or just to ensure that data markets operate more efectively in accordance with narrowly dened privacy rights.47 I so, the expansion o data territories will not be halted.

Worse, in other places like the United States and the United Kingdom, signicant regulation has been delayed. These are the places where laissez-aire capitalism promotes the disastrous illusion that it is best to let corporations regulate themselves, and where corporations employ powerul lobbies to give money to politicians in return or avourable deals that allow them to continue to do as they please. This is exactly what worries many NHS data scientists as they ace the prospect o a long-term dependence on a corporation like Palantir, or example.48

Rushed legislation that simply attempts to prove that governments are doing something can be just as dangerous, as in the case o the Kids Online Saety Act (KOSA), introduced to the US Congress in 2022. A coalition o over ninety civic organisations critiqued the bill on the grounds that, while claiming to protect children's privacy, it would end up exposing them to more invasive tracking, fltering and monitoring tools.49 The act was rejected but has been re-introduced in 2023 with minor modifcations.

Change within the system is always going to be slow and complicated. But it is possible. Anti-trust regulation is one area that looks promising, even in the US. People like Lina Khan, current chairwoman o the US Federal Trade Commission, are taking a very close look at the question o whether companies like Amazon and Meta are too big, and whether their monopolistic practices serve consumers well. In August 2022, Khan issued an 'advance notice o proposed rulemaking' about the damages caused by commercial surveillance. This has generated a large number o responses rom business and civil society. A debate is under way about how to 'change the paradigm' that has accepted corporate surveillance or so long.50 But it remains unclear how much the FTC will be allowed to do i a deeply conservative Supreme Court and a potential Republican government in 2024 have their way. Big Tech nonetheless ears these proposals, which is why they have attempted to orce Khan to recuse hersel rom such investigations, as i her commitments to address Big Tech's problems make her unqualifed to implement them.51 Wherever we look, this is going to be a long war.

#### A PLAYBOOK FOR RESISTANCE

A dierent and potentially bolder approach to regulation would involve the nationalisation of data by countries, a proposal made particularly by countries in the Global South. This would involve declaring data a national resource, as some countries have done in the past with oil or other natural resources and industries.
